# 3. Suppliers and fulfillment

## How Supplier Fulfillment Works and Which Model to Choose

### Core Order Flow in Drop Shipping

#### The basic fulfillment sequence

In a drop shipping model the store holds no stock. Each customer order triggers a separate order with the supplier, and the supplier ships the goods directly to the customer [L1119.001].

Fulfillment is the process in which a connected supplier or fulfillment company receives each order, inspects the product quality, packs the product, ships it directly to the customer, and handles after-sales issues such as refunds and returns [L0685.002].

The standard sequence is:

1. The customer places an order on your store [L0007.023].
2. You pass the order information to your supplier or manufacturer [L0007.023].
3. The supplier picks and packs the order [L0007.023].
4. The customer receives the order at their doorstep [L0007.023].

#### Payment and supplier charging (older advice, 2020)

The 2020 Shopify-based flow works as follows:

1. The customer pays on the Shopify store, and Shopify Payments pays the money out within 48 hours [L0664.086].
2. Contact the supplier and ask it to ship the specific products to the customer's address [L0664.086].
3. The supplier charges your card and ships the item to the customer [L0664.086].
4. Confirm with each supplier when it charges the card, because timing differs per supplier [L0664.086].

#### Payment and supplier charging (older advice, 2021-22)

The 2021-22 flow for a single order is:

1. The customer buys the product on the store and pays immediately [L1481.017].
2. The seller receives the payment in the bank account [L1481.017].
3. The seller places an order with the supplier for that exact product and asks the supplier to ship directly to the customer's address [L1481.017].
4. The seller pays the supplier [L1481.017].
5. The supplier packs and ships the product from its warehouse, and it is delivered to the customer's home [L1481.017].

### Choosing and Managing Drop Shipping Suppliers

#### Test first, then specialize

Start testing products with dropshipping suppliers such as CJ or Hyper SKU, which allow low-risk testing of many products [L0562.017]. Move to a dedicated private supplier once a product has proven demand [L0562.017].

During the testing phase, use two suppliers: AliExpress for sourcing and CJ Dropshipping as the fulfillment provider [L0642.043]. Switch to a private supplier only once a product is proven [L0642.043].

When testing a new product, a dropshipping supplier is a good way to start. The cost per unit is higher, but it removes the need to stock inventory before the product is validated [L1315.012].

#### Supplier count

Use one main supplier when you plan custom packaging or simple fulfillment. Multiple suppliers mean separate shipments and no saving from combined orders. Switching suppliers later costs time on packaging and branding [L0524.031].

A single Shopify store can also be fulfilled by several suppliers at the same time, for example CJ Dropshipping and another supplier. Testing two suppliers on the same product shows which one gives customers the better experience [L1216.022].

Both approaches are valid. Use one main supplier when packaging and branding matter and shipment consolidation is valuable. Run two or more suppliers on the same product when you need to compare customer experience [L0524.031, L1216.022].

#### Timing and delivery expectations

Do not worry about exact delivery times in the early stage of a product [L1444.048]. Once orders grow, find a new supplier before delivery delays become a problem [L1444.048].

When order volume grows, move to a private supplier to cut processing and shipping time. A private supplier with about 10 days to delivery was described as a good target [L1444.047].

The fulfillment option should be chosen only after the product is validated. Before that, decide on a shipping method with the supplier [L1108.037].

#### Products to keep on drop shipping

Keep expensive, high-ticket products on the traditional dropship model if good suppliers already exist. Check how many customers buy them before committing [L0487.054]. Private labeling is not needed when strong products are already available [L0487.054].

High-ticket dropshipping has three main risks that must be managed: freight cost to get the product into the country, return risk, and damage risk. Managing these three keeps the model viable [L0660.016].

#### Products to avoid

Used or second-hand products should not be sold, because inventory is always limited and the business cannot scale [L0967.043].

Understand how suppliers, product sourcing and fulfillment work before choosing products, because they determine how orders reach the customer [L0914.002].

### Hybrid Model and Own Warehousing

#### Hybrid model

The hybrid model combines direct dropshipping from suppliers with your own fulfillment center. It is a valid business model. All blueprint teachings apply, but fulfillment operations are a separate business requiring more capital and time. It is not recommended for a first store [L0268.075].

#### Early in-house fulfillment

In the early phase, fulfillment can be done in-house with friends and a rented warehouse. This costs less than an outside 3PL. Consider outsourcing once volume grows [L0492.025].

For reference, a warehouse of about 50 square meters in Switzerland cost about 200 per month. Warehouse costs depend strongly on the country [L0492.024].

#### Three fulfillment setups

Three fulfillment setups exist. Each trades off cost, delivery time and capital risk [L1217.001]:

| Setup | Description | IDs |
|---|---|---|
| Single-order dropshipping | Supplier such as CJ Dropshipping or Zendrop ships each order | [L1217.001] |
| Bulk stock in a Chinese warehouse or 3PL | Stock held centrally and shipped from there | [L1217.001, L1217.003] |
| Bulk stock shipped to your own address | You hold the stock and ship it yourself | [L1217.001] |

#### Transit times and market coverage

From a Chinese warehouse, delivery takes about 5-7 days to the US and about 3-5 days to the UK and Europe [L1217.002].

A Chinese warehouse holding bulk stock can ship to the US, UK, Europe, Australia and New Zealand from one stock pool. Managing many SKUs across several markets becomes hard to keep in sync, so this suits a few main products [L1217.003].

Shipping stock to a warehouse in the target country can improve conversion rates compared with shipping from China. Holding stock is only worthwhile while the season continues [L1315.013].

#### Benefits of holding stock in a warehouse

A warehouse that holds your stock lowers the risk of bulk buying. Unsold units can be returned, or new stock can be sent quickly [L1217.007]. You avoid waiting weeks for a restock after selling out fast [L1217.007].

#### Third-party logistics providers

Deliver is a 3PL that offers Prime-like 1-2 day shipping. It is only worth using at heavy daily volume from a single country [L1217.009].

ShipBob is a US-based third-party warehousing company used to store and ship stock. The speaker reported poor customer support and unnecessary charges, and planned to test another warehouse, ShipDudes, which is based on the US East Coast [L1315.014].

### Amazon FBA

#### When Amazon FBA fits

Amazon FBA is an option for dropshipping if you sell in a limited set of countries. Amazon takes about 30% commission, and customer data is limited [L0637.053]. Consider ordering inventory only with a clear sales forecast from the last 2-3 months [L0637.053].

Amazon offers its own warehousing and third-party logistics services to sellers who use Fulfillment by Amazon. This can reduce handling for sellers who buy inventory in bulk. Inventory decisions still require a clear sales forecast, because the upfront stock is a financial risk [L0637.069].

Amazon can hold your inventory in its fulfillment warehouses and ship customer orders from there. This makes it a practical starting option for a product that you sell on Amazon first (older advice, 2021-22) [L1154.008].

#### Amazon FBA workflow

1. Place order with supplier [L0227.011].
2. Wait for lead time, typically 4-8 weeks [L0227.011].
3. Receive inventory [L0227.011].
4. Send products to the Amazon fulfillment center [L0227.011].
5. Amazon handles all storage, packing, shipping, customer refunds, and payment processing [L0227.011].
6. Receive wire transfer to bank account every 14 days [L0227.011].

Your role is marketing and inventory management only [L0227.011].

### Pre-order, Made-to-Order and Print on Demand

#### Definitions

Pre-order fulfillment means ordering from the supplier only after the customer has paid, so no stock is bought in advance [L0779.009].

Made-to-ship fulfillment is a production model in which the garment is made and shipped to the customer as orders come in [L0779.010].

Print on demand (POD) is a fulfillment method in which designs are printed onto garments only after an order is placed [L0779.011].

#### Production time and customer communication

Made-to-order and custom products can be sold. Make the production time very clear on the product page, and follow up after purchase so the customer knows the lead time [L0806.028].

#### Print-on-demand books

Print-on-demand book suppliers can often customize the cover and the inner content of a book on request. This typically costs little or nothing extra and needs little time. Ask the supplier which customizations they offer before editing anything [L0333.012].

#### Personalized products

Personalized products such as custom Christmas ornaments need a designer to create the designs and a personalization form, usually added through a Shopify app. These products are harder to dropship than standard products because they require more setup work [L1185.033].

#### Digital products without fulfillment

Digital ebooks need no shipping or stock. Create the PDF with ChatGPT for content, Midjourney for images and Canva for layout. Upload it to the Digital Downloads app so it is delivered to the buyer automatically after purchase. The buyer receives the PDF immediately [L1131.034].

A staged launch of an ebook product works as follows [L1131.035]:

1. Create only one ebook first and run ads to test demand [L1131.035].
2. After the first sale, email the buyer that fulfillment is delayed [L1131.035].
3. Create and send the ebook within two to three days, or the same day if possible [L1131.035].
4. After the first and second sale show demand, create the full ebook range, improve the offer, refine interest targeting and then scale [L1131.035].

### Brand Partnerships as an Alternative

#### How brand partnerships work

Brand partnerships through Karo let you list another Shopify store's product on your own store without holding inventory or handling fulfillment. The order is recorded in both systems and labeled as fulfilled by the brand partner. The model is similar to dropshipping but works like affiliate marketing, where you earn a cut of the sale (older advice, 2021-22) [L0869.006].

#### Adding partner products (older advice, 2021-22)

1. Open Karo's Brand Partnerships side [L0869.007].
2. Search for another Shopify store in the network that sells a product you want to offer but cannot source from a dropshipping supplier [L0869.007].
3. Add that product to your store [L0869.007].
4. When a customer buys it, the order is created in both systems and marked as fulfilled by the brand partner [L0869.007].
5. The partner handles fulfillment, and you receive a sales commission [L0869.007].

### Moving Between Models

#### Triggers for moving to a 3PL

Switch a SKU to a 3PL when it sells about 1,000 units per month. Alternatively, switch when it gets at least 10 orders per day for that unit, sustained for at least three weeks [L1217.010].

#### Stepwise progression

1. Start with supplier dropshipping, for example CJ Dropshipping or Zendrop, while you are still testing products [L1217.011].
2. When a product reaches about 5-10 orders per day, move it to a personal agent that has a minimum volume requirement [L1217.011].
3. When a SKU reaches about 1,000 units per month, or about 10 orders per day for at least three weeks, move it to a 3PL with bulk stock [L1217.011, L1217.010].
4. For stock arriving by sea, project needs about 20-30 days ahead and reorder accordingly [L1217.011].

#### Moving to a private supplier or own warehousing

Once a product has proven profitable, consider moving to a private supplier or your own warehousing [L1315.013]. Use this step only after validation, because the upfront commitment is larger than with dropshipping [L1315.012, L1315.013].

#### Moving from drop shipping to a private supplier

When order volume grows, move to a private supplier to cut processing and shipping time [L1444.047]. Find the new supplier before delivery delays become a problem [L1444.048].

## Supplier Tiers: Bronze, Silver, Gold and Who to Avoid

### Supplier Tiers at a Glance

Supplier tiers are an informal ranking, not an official industry standard, so searching for "gold, silver, bronze supplier" will not return a definition [L0711.001]. Each tier leads to different results, so check the tier before deciding which suppliers to pursue [L0590.002]. Supplier type determines your profitability, how much price competition you face, how much control you have over prices, and whether your store can scale [L0110.008].

| Tier | Access cost | Approval | MAP enforced | Share of your suppliers | Service | Sources |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bronze | Fee or payment required | Anyone who pays; no real screening | Usually not | Avoid | Poor | L0110.001, L0486.001, L0711.002, L0711.007 |
| Silver | No fee | Each applicant reviewed | Yes | Default: 100% of first five, about 95% long term | Good to great | L0110.002, L0486.011, L0711.009 |
| Gold | No fee | Strict; requires business history | Yes, acted on immediately | About 5 to 10% of suppliers | Excellent | L0110.006, L0711.012, L0711.014 |

### Bronze Suppliers: Why to Avoid Them

**What bronze means**
- Bronze suppliers accept anyone who applies and usually charge a fee for access to their catalog [L0486.001].
- They accept any applicant without screening, and the only requirement is payment. Treat this as a major red flag [L0711.002].
- Bronze suppliers typically ask for approval fees of $50, $100, $1,000 or more before authorizing a retailer [L0711.004].
- A supplier that asks you to pay before you can sell its products is classified as bronze and skipped [L0486.002, L1111.026].
- Bronze suppliers are the ones most likely to cause MAP violations [L1279.020].
- Bronze suppliers charge a fee to become an authorized retailer, while suppliers whose relationship benefits both sides through product sales are preferred [L0934.033].
- Bronze suppliers often make money from fees or from poor sales terms [L0806.018].

**Why they hurt profit**
- The same products are listed by countless other sellers, and you cannot stay profitable under that price pressure [L0486.001].
- Without MAP enforcement, retailers undercut each other until margins disappear, even when orders come in [L0486.003, L0110.001].
- Example: a chandelier with a MAP of $1,000 that competitors sell for $600 to $800, with no action from the supplier [L0711.006].
- Competitors selling at thin margins (for example around 5%) undercut you, so listings and paid traffic sent to those products are largely wasted [L0711.008].
- Products from bronze suppliers show inconsistent quality, with high rates of shipping damage and defects [L0711.008].
- Service is poor: slow replies, delayed shipping and missing tracking numbers. A retailer may have to follow up a week after submitting an order to learn whether it shipped [L0711.007].
- Bronze suppliers offer no real advantages to a retailer. Their drawbacks are unenforced MAP, inconsistent quality and poor support [L0711.017].
- A store built on bronze suppliers may produce early sales, but the business tends to fall apart quickly and become a constant problem [L0711.021].
- Their products often bring more headaches than profit, so do not run ads for them or sell them [L0806.018].

**Rule for building your network**
- Bronze suppliers must be avoided entirely when researching suppliers and building your network [L0112.002].
- Never sell for bronze suppliers, even when their catalog looks attractive [L0486.016].
- Avoid bronze suppliers that require you to buy their product or pre-purchase inventory before you can sell [L0801.025].

**Exception for existing listings (conflicts with the rules above, see "Conflicting Guidance")**
- Handling a bronze supplier with no MAP policy and an MSRP below 2X wholesale:
  1. Check whether its products still sell at a healthy margin without ad costs [L0934.034].
  2. If they do, keep them on the site and optionally in the product feed for free listings [L0934.034].
  3. Set their Google Ads cost per click to one cent so no ad money goes to them [L0934.034].
  4. Remove them if nothing sells [L0934.034].

### Silver Suppliers: The Default Tier

**Identifying a silver supplier**
- Silver suppliers review and approve each retailer individually based on credibility [L0110.002].
- Identify silver suppliers by three characteristics: they charge no fees to sell their products, they require approval before onboarding, and they enforce MAP pricing [L0110.004].
- A silver supplier does not charge for access and reviews its applicants. If a supplier meets both criteria, classify it as silver [L0486.011].
- A supplier you found through niche product research, whose website has no page offering paid access to sell its products, is generally silver. Confirm the other silver traits, such as MAP enforcement and retailer review, before applying [L0486.019].
- Silver suppliers do not show a "where to buy" section [L0486.014, L0801.034].

**What silver suppliers offer**
- They enforce MAP quickly once a retailer reports a competitor selling below it. They contact the offending retailer to correct the price or remove it as an authorized seller [L0711.010].
- Example: a MAP of $1,000 with a competitor listing at $900 leads to immediate action [L0711.010].
- They provide good to great customer service, with fast shipping, quick responses to inquiries and care for their retailers [L0110.002, L0486.010].
- They usually assign a dedicated sales representative, reachable by email or phone, for orders, tracking, new variations and questions [L0711.011].
- They ship quickly and respond promptly because they earn from retailer sales rather than from fees, so their interests align with yours [L0711.011].
- They protect your margins through MAP enforcement and provide quality control and fast shipping. They are plentiful, with thousands available [L0711.018].
- Their drawback is competition: any legitimate store can sell their products, so traffic and paid ads must be well optimized [L0711.018].
- Silver suppliers tend to offer good to great customer service and are preferred partners [L0486.010].

**What silver suppliers require**
- Silver suppliers review every retailer that applies and approve only selected sellers [L0486.006].
- Expect a refusal if you apply without a finished demo store and a complete business setup, so prepare both before contacting them [L0486.006].
- They review every applicant, including the live store, so the store must be built before applying [L0711.009].
- Trust is the foundation of the relationship. Silver suppliers use your business setup and presentation as signals of legitimacy and professionalism before granting access [L0110.010].
- Approval is the supplier's decision that it trusts you with its brand. Silver and gold suppliers expect retailers to earn that trust before approval [L1422.003].

**Silver suppliers in practice**
- Silver suppliers approve most sellers and will make up nearly all of your suppliers until you have a sales track record [L1274.029].
- A store can be profitable with only silver-tier suppliers. Gold-tier suppliers add strength but are not required [L1281.120].
- Silver suppliers have similar margins to gold but are less selective [L1283.007].
- Silver suppliers are the core of the business and make up most brands sold over time [L0969.006].
- Silver suppliers because they approve new retailers without needing a reputation [L0466.033].
- Start outreach with silver suppliers first to build momentum [L0251.049, L0466.033].
- Select Silver as the supplier tier in the contact plan, because over 95 percent of the brands you will sell for are silver tier [L0936.004].

### Gold Suppliers: The Top Tier and a Later Goal

**Identifying a gold supplier**
- Gold suppliers are extremely selective, enforce MAP, do not sell directly to the public, and actively refer customers to authorized retailers through a "Where to Buy" page [L0110.006].
- Identify a gold supplier by a "where to buy" section on its website that links to other online stores, typically 5 to 10, in your niche [L0486.014, L0801.034].
- Seeing that the supplier already refers buyers to other stores shows it is gold without prior approval [L0711.016].
- A "where to buy" section listing retailer logos is a good sign that a supplier is gold. Other suppliers usually need to be contacted to confirm their level [L1068.034].
- Gold suppliers usually list their approved retailers on their site [L1283.007].

**Scale and selectivity**
- Gold suppliers authorize only a small number of retailers, often around 5 to 10 companies in total [L0486.012, L0110.006].
- Gold suppliers are the highest-quality tier but a small minority, roughly 5 to 10 percent of a retailer's suppliers [L0711.012].
- They build relationships closely and work with only a very small number of selected retailers [L0711.012].

**Requirements and approval timing**
- Gold suppliers require proof of established business history, existing traffic and a sales track record before approval [L0110.007, L0486.012].
- They are a long-term goal after your business has proven sales success rather than a starting point [L0110.007].
- Gold suppliers often reject new stores until they show traffic, sales or time in business [L0853.016].
- Gold suppliers do not approve new retailers on day one because their screening is stricter. Approval typically comes after the store has traffic, sales and a track record with silver suppliers, often three to twelve months later [L0711.013].
- Gold suppliers that send traffic by linking to your store can take 3 months, 6 months, or more than a year to authorize [L0938.004].
- Some brands approve a new retailer right away, while gold-level suppliers can take a year or two because they want to see a long sales history [L0857.033].
- Gold approvals are initially difficult but gradually become easier as relationships develop and trust grows [L0112.004].
- Gold suppliers are usually the hardest to get approved with. Still contact every supplier in your niche, because outreach is quick, and circle back on rejections [L0934.036].
- Gold suppliers are normal to lag in approval [L0969.006].

**Service and referrals**
- Gold suppliers enforce MAP and act right away against sellers who list below it [L0711.014].
- They give excellent customer service, with a point of contact who reaches out proactively to help increase sales [L0711.014, L0486.013].
- They do not sell to the public themselves, so they refer customers to their authorized retailers [L0486.013, L0486.021].
- Approved gold-tier retailers receive referrals: when a customer wants to buy directly from the supplier, the supplier passes the lead and contact details to an authorized retailer [L0711.015].
- The referral flow is the main reason gold suppliers are so limited in number [L0711.015].
- Gold suppliers are most likely to link back to your store. Not all suppliers will do this, and tier placement determines this benefit [L0359.030].
- Expect referrals only after you have been approved and have proven traffic and sales [L0486.021].
- Gold suppliers combine MAP protection, customer support and quality control with referrals, and they limit competition because they work with only a handful of online retailers. Their main drawback is that approval is hard without a sales history [L0711.020].

**Using gold for credibility**
- Once suppliers begin approving you, use the approved brands to build credibility before approaching your top targets [L0667.059].
- Over time, get approved with as many brands in the niche as possible, as long as they are silver or gold, because a low-selling brand with strong margins can fund monthly promotions [L1113.018].
- Once you have revenue and traffic, circle back to gold suppliers, whose approval odds are much higher [L0251.049].
- Reapply to gold once the store is proven, after building with silver suppliers first [L0853.016].

### Identifying Tiers Before You Apply

Check in this order:

1. Check whether the supplier charges for access to its products. If yes, classify it as bronze and drop it [L0486.017].
2. Check whether it enforces MAP and reviews applicants. If yes, it is a silver supplier [L0486.017].
3. Look for a "where to buy" section linking to 5 to 10 online stores. If yes, it is a gold supplier, and you should plan to approach it after building traffic and sales [L0486.017].

Supporting checks:
- A supplier without a MAP policy is classed as a lower tier than suppliers that meet all criteria, including MAP. It can still be workable if margins hold after shipping [L1285.017].
- A supplier that enforces MAP belongs to the higher-quality group. Prioritize enforcing suppliers when choosing whom to approach first [L1275.017].
- Prioritize slow-responding, frequently out-of-stock or slow-shipping suppliers lower than reliable ones, because these can signal financial instability and may not survive a cash-flow shock [L0967.019].
- A supplier with an outdated website, low-resolution images or thin product information can still be worth carrying, because your job is to give customers a reason to buy. Clearer product content and professional presentation on your own store can make that difference [L1086.067].

Sequencing:
- Start by contacting silver-tier suppliers, and work up to gold-tier brands once you have experience [L0667.059].
- Submit only your top two or three niche ideas for verification [L0667.059].
- Do not wait for the best supplier before you begin. Open an account with any supplier who will work with you, build demand, and premium suppliers may approach you later [L1441.044].
- Premium or top-tier suppliers are not needed at the start [L1441.044].

### Red Flags: Paid Access and Fees

- A legitimate supplier never charges a retailer for access to its products, including approval fees. If a supplier asks for money to get approved or to sell, decline and move to the next supplier on your list [L0711.003].
- Do not pay an opening fee or buy a minimum starter quantity just to open a supplier account. Counter a minimum order request by explaining when you will reach that volume [L0664.012].
- Never pay for access to any supplier. Use the course login on the dropship platform for supplier access, which is included in the membership [L0964.024].
- Distributors that require a paid membership fee to access products should be avoided. Distributors without a fee can be worth working with if they enforce pricing controls [L0429.060].
- Do not accept offers that charge per order. Example: $25 per order plus a $100 fee per invoice on small invoices is a warning sign. Ask whether there are sales tiers without fees. If there are none, do not sell the products [L0806.019].
- Do not pay a one-time pre-purchase stock fee, such as $10,000. Propose a one-month test listing with ads instead. If the supplier refuses, launch with other brands and return later with a stronger store [L1283.011].
- Some suppliers outside China charge yearly license fees, reported at up to $300 to $500 [L1214.005].
- Big Buy requires a paid membership, reported at about €300 per year [L1214.005].

### Red Flags: Paid Directories and Middlemen

**Paid directories**
- Paid supplier directories that charge a monthly fee (for example $150 per month) for access to products are middlemen and should not be used. Their products are not worth the cost [L0359.026].
- Do not pay monthly or yearly fees to paid supplier directories, even when they claim access to over a million products, because they typically offer no direct relationship with the brands [L0466.019].
- Paid directories sit several layers away from the brand through distributors, overstock sellers and resellers, so pricing control, MAP compliance and current product data are lost. Products are often outdated and already marked up at least twice, and every paying retailer competes with the same products [L0466.020].
- Avoid supplier directories. They mostly connect you to middlemen, and the margins through them are usually too thin [L0962.008].
- Avoid supplier directories that sell access to brand catalogs for a percentage. They skip the direct relationship with the brand, which is what makes the business work long term [L1277.032].
- Supplier directories and paid supplier lists were judged not worth the money. Finding suppliers through your own direct research gives much better brand quality [L0551.012].
- Supplier directories and paid apps that promise instant access to thousands of products are bronze-level sources [L0486.002].

**Middlemen**
- Do not pay middlemen for supplier lists or catalog access. A paid monthly CSV of supplier products is a red flag and can harm your account standing [L0247.030].
- Bronze-tier suppliers that route you through a marketplace or middleman such as Alibaba are generally not worth working with. Look for suppliers elsewhere [L1113.019].
- Overseas contacts may be middlemen rather than factories, so verify before investing. When an overseas contact offers an affiliate-style commission, ask for their U.S. contact or U.S. warehouse and for the reseller program [L1274.033].
- Source A suppliers charge no fee for approval or access. They are the manufacturers or brand owners themselves, not middlemen or distributors [L1422.002].
- Using a distributor is only acceptable without a fee and with pricing controls [L0429.060].

### Red Flags: Preloaded Stores and Store-Building Offers

- Some bronze suppliers offer to build a preloaded store with all their products for $2,000, $5,000 or $10,000. Such an offer is another red flag for a bronze supplier [L0711.005].

### Red Flags: Affiliate-Only Deals and Low Margins

**Affiliate-only offers**
- Affiliate-only offers from a dealer that refuses drop shipping, such as an 8% commission, are not worth it. Promoting as an affiliate only makes sense for a related niche you would never sell yourself [L0664.014].
- Do not accept offers that pay a commission of about 8% to 12% on sales. That is an affiliate arrangement, not an e-commerce or dropshipping deal, and does not leave enough margin [L1274.032].
- Suppliers that sell to physical stores often suggest becoming their affiliate instead. Affiliate promotion only fits when customers may want the product but it does not make sense for the store to sell it directly, for example promoting a drum school to buyers of beginner drum sets [L0666.017].
- Affiliate offers are not suitable for your own core products, because the store cannot show add-to-cart buttons or run the usual ads for products you do not sell. Affiliate links fit indirectly related products, such as courses or services for the same audience, and the margins are very small [L1283.013].
- Affiliate-level margins of 10 to 15% leave too little room for ad spend. Such products should be listed only if the sale costs nothing and should not receive paid traffic [L1111.048].
- Suppliers that offer only an affiliate program should be deprioritized. Follow up with other suppliers and return to affiliate-only suppliers once the store has traffic and sales [L0855.072].

**Low margins and no MAP**
- A supplier requiring a minimum annual sales volume (for example $10,000 per year) or tiny margins (for example 12%) is a red flag. Starting with a supplier that has a credible brand can help get approved by more suppliers later [L0472.023].
- When a supplier has no MAP policy and offers only a thin percentage margin (for example 20 to 35 percent with volume tiers), treat it as bronze and avoid it, because advertising costs, merchant fees and shipping leave no margin [L0665.014].
- Margins of 15 to 20 percent place a supplier in the bronze category, and bronze suppliers should not be used [L1283.007].

### Red Flags: Trial Terms and Unusual Setups

- Do not accept terms requiring you to sell products at no profit as a trial period before negotiating a profitable margin. This indicates a low-tier supplier and is not worthwhile. Avoid suppliers that require this as a mandatory trial phase before allowing margin negotiation [L0276.029, L0276.041].
- Negotiate a test where products go live on your store first, so the best sellers show up before any inventory is bought [L0801.025].
- Be cautious with a wholesale company that asks retailers to send customers to its own website without a cart and to take orders by email or phone. This setup is unusual and a poor fit for a dropshipping store, so question its terms before accepting [L0967.097].
- Do not sell Black Friday orders through supplier stores based in China. The risk of bad customer experiences is not worth the sales [L1097.019].
- Prefer silver or gold suppliers for reliability and work with as many suppliers as possible. Holiday peaks such as Black Friday, Cyber Monday and Christmas can cause stock shortages with disorganized suppliers [L0357.024].
- Avoid sourcing from marketplaces like AliExpress. Reasons: long shipping times, unbranded products and little to no quality control [L1277.033].

### Conflicting Guidance

**Bronze: never sell versus sell on existing listings**
- Never sell for bronze suppliers and do not run ads for them [L0486.016, L0806.018].
- Exception for products already listed, where the margin works without ad costs and MSRP is below 2X wholesale: keep them with a one-cent CPC and remove them if nothing sells [L0934.034].
- Applies when: you are building a store (rule above) or you already have listings that earn a healthy margin without ad spend (exception) [L0486.016, L0934.034].

**Tier versus judgment**
- A supplier's tier does not decide whether to work with it. Judge the supplier by the cost of goods, which should be about 50% of the selling price, by whether it enforces MAP, and by the discount it offers [L0664.089].
- Tier rules still apply to silver and gold screening, approval and MAP expectations. Use the cost-of-goods test to judge any supplier you can actually access [L0664.089, L0110.002].
- Applies when: you are comparing offers from suppliers you can access without paying [L0664.089].

**Bronze without MAP**
- A supplier without a MAP policy is lower tier, but it can still be workable if margins hold after shipping [L1285.017].
- Thin percentage margins with no MAP are treated as bronze and avoided [L0665.014].
- Applies when: margins are high enough after shipping and ad costs (workable), or when margins are thin with volume tiers (avoid) [L1285.017, L0665.014].

**Gold approval timing**
- 3 to 12 months after the store has traffic, sales and silver track record [L0711.013].
- 3 months, 6 months, or more than a year, for suppliers that link to your store [L0938.004].
- A year or two for brands that want a long sales history [L0857.033].
- Applies when: the supplier's own site has a referral section or wants to see sales history [L0938.004, L0857.033].

**Silver share of suppliers**
- 100% of your first five suppliers, and about 95% of all suppliers long-term [L0110.003, L0486.005].
- 90 to 95 percent of a retailer's suppliers [L0711.009].
- Majority of both total suppliers and sales revenue [L0112.003].
- Nearly all suppliers until you have a sales track record [L1274.029].
- Applies when: the first five suppliers and the long-term base are being planned (first five and about 95% long term) [L0110.003], or when the store is still building its track record (nearly all) [L1274.029].

**Gold share of suppliers**
- Around 5 to 10 retailers in total, which counts how many retailers a gold supplier authorizes [L0110.006, L0486.012].
- Roughly 5 to 10 percent of a retailer's suppliers, which counts how much of your supplier base gold makes up [L0711.012].
- Applies when: you are judging how many retailers a gold brand works with versus how many gold brands you should hold [L0110.006, L0711.012].

### Target Mix and Sequencing

**Target mix**
- Silver: 100% of your first five suppliers and about 95% of all suppliers long-term [L0110.003, L0486.005].
- Gold: about 5 to 10% of your suppliers [L0711.012].
- Bronze: none; avoid entirely [L0112.002].
- A store can be profitable with silver only, and gold adds strength but is not required [L1281.120].

**Sequence**
1. Build a store with silver suppliers, which should make up most of your supplier base [L0853.016].
2. Build sales, traffic and brand reputation with silver suppliers [L0711.021].
3. Once you have sales and traffic, apply to gold suppliers [L0251.049, L0853.016].
4. Keep contacting other suppliers while waiting for gold approval [L0938.004].

### Older Advice

The following units are older advice, from 2018 to 2022, and should be checked against current conditions before use:
- 2018: paid directories and layered distribution [L0466.019, L0466.020]; silver first [L0466.033, L1274.029]; gold proof of traffic [L1085.032]; affiliate commissions [L1274.032, L1274.033].
- 2019: holiday stock and tier preference [L0357.024]; gold "where to buy" logo sign [L1068.034]; outdated websites [L1086.067]; MAP as quality group [L1275.017].
- 2020: supplier list tool [L0426.005]; opening fees and minimum orders [L0664.012]; affiliate-only offers [L0664.014]; tier versus judgment [L0664.089]; wholesale without cart [L0967.097]; financial instability [L0967.019]; directories [L0551.012, L1277.032]; AliExpress [L1277.033]; fees and bronze [L1111.026]; affiliate margins [L1111.048].
- 2021: bronze pre-purchase [L0801.025]; gold where-to-buy [L0801.034]; directories and middlemen [L0962.008]; bronze MAP violations [L1279.020]; gold reject until proven [L0853.016].
- 2022: distributors with fees [L0429.060]; tiers summary [L0969.006]; thin margins with no MAP [L0665.014]; silver-only profitability [L1281.120]; approving with many brands [L1113.018]; Alibaba middlemen [L1113.019].

#### Further details

- Potential drop ship suppliers are classified into three tiers-Bronze, Silver, and Gold-based on quality, reliability, and approval dynamics. [L0112.001]

## Qualifying Suppliers: Location, Stock, MAP, Direct Brands vs. Distributors

### Supplier criteria at a glance

| Criterion | Requirement | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Location | Supplier is in the same country as your store (for a US store, a US-based supplier) | L0247.018 |
| Stock | Supplier holds the product in stock in the country or region where you sell | L0962.012, L0994.012, L0994.013 |
| Origin of manufacture | Does not matter if stock is local; matters if everything ships from abroad | L0466.039, L0941.024, L1085.014 |
| Relationship | Brand or importer that owns the brand name, approved directly | L0276.042, L0359.024, L0665.123 |
| MAP | Enforced by the supplier, or a documented reason to accept a non-MAP supplier | L0110.005, L0472.021, L1069.092 |
| Direct-to-consumer | Sells at MSRP while you sell at MAP, or its own store is weak | L0247.021, L0249.012, L0803.031 |
| Channel | Allows you to sell on the channels you need, and ships on your account | L0835.027, L0967.028 |
| Responsiveness | Answers phone and email before approval | L1111.041 |

### Order of checks before you contact a supplier

1. Check dropship friendliness of the niche before you build the store, because suppliers may refuse retailers that are not physical stores or do not hold their own stock. [L0460.002, L0460.025]
2. Check how many online retailers already sell each brand, and prefer brands with fewer competing sellers, especially when the business depends on paid ads. [L0711.019]
3. Check whether the supplier's products are already sold by other online-only stores, because that shows dropship friendliness. Not every supplier will approve, but enough approvals are likely when every criterion is followed. [L0359.022]
4. Check whether competitors have a retail store, showroom or warehouse, because suppliers with retail presence often refuse drop shipping. [L0671.038, L0671.177]
5. Verify the stock location (see the stock verification section below).
6. Identify whether the seller is the brand, an importer, a manufacturer or a distributor (see the distributor section below).
7. Check MAP policy and enforcement (see the MAP section below).
8. Check the direct-to-consumer conflict (see the direct-to-consumer section below).
9. Check marketplace and channel restrictions, and product shipping restrictions.

### Stock location: the first filter

- Source from domestic suppliers in the market where the store operates. For a US store, that means US suppliers. [L0247.018, L1069.006]
- A supplier's location (warehouse or showroom) is treated the same way when deciding whether it is a valid source for a store. The decision is based on the same data for each. [L0964.025]
- Choose suppliers that hold the product in stock in the country or region where you sell. Overseas suppliers shipping to customers are a very small exception and are not recommended. [L0962.012]
- Most of the supplier base should be domestic. Foreign suppliers should be used only where they offer unique products and good quality. [L1086.035]
- Domestic suppliers are preferred because shipping is faster and cheaper and customers are happier. [L0962.013]
- When a domestic supplier with short lead times is available, prefer it over an overseas middleman with long lead times, even if the unit price is slightly higher. Long lead times create customer cancellations and backorder handling. [L0852.122]
- Where possible, find a supplier of the same product located in the target country, such as the US, instead of shipping from CJ. In the US, the number of warehouses made little difference in the session. [L1108.039]
- Products made overseas but stored in US warehouses are fine, since they ship quickly. [L0941.024]
- Suppliers outside the US can still be a good fit if their products are stored in US warehouses and can ship quickly and efficiently. Warehouses close to your customers make shipping easier. [L0960.022]
- Suppliers with warehouses in the country you sell in are a positive sign. [L0963.082]
- Suppliers located outside the US can be used for US sales if they already have US distribution. In that case the sourcing process is the same as with a US-based supplier. [L0971.084]
- Suppliers located in Canada or Mexico are fine to contact for a US store. Competitors selling for them often already use US distribution, a US fulfillment center or a US warehouse. [L0856.048]
- Suppliers headquartered abroad that also have a US office may stock their inventory in the United States. Ask where a product is stocked before excluding an international supplier. [L0834.118]
- Many large foreign brands already serve the US market through US distribution, a US fulfillment center or a US warehouse, and can be sold to US customers. [L0806.027]
- A supplier with a foreign domain (for example.ca) that is listed on a US retail site probably also has US distribution. Record its contact details and check its contact page for a US option. [L0940.026]
- North American brands can supply a US store. Ask them which carriers they use and which other companies already ship to the US, then reverse engineer what competitors do. [L0671.041, L0671.180]
- Start with US-only shipping. Suppliers in Canada that sell to the US may already hold stock in US warehouses or fulfillment centers, so reach out to them if they appear on competitor sites. [L0470.085]
- Prefer suppliers that hold inventory where you operate and where your customers are. [L0469.027]
- Working with suppliers that hold stock in your target country is the standard. [L0671.039, L0671.178]
- Suppliers outside the US may hold stock in US warehouses. Add them to your supplier list and ask whether they ship from a US warehouse and how stock reaches customers. [L0469.020]
- Suppliers based in Europe: ask whether they have stock or distribution centers in the United States, because local stock speeds up delivery. [L1002.065]
- Shipping to a different country from the sourcing market is possible, for example selling a product that performs well in the US in Australia, which is in winter when the US product has been popular. Sourcing a product from one market and selling it in another is a common way to test products. [L0678.028]
- Ship international orders only through the supplier's own shipping arrangements. If a supplier does not ship to a given country such as Canada or the US, do not sell its products there. [L0934.041]

#### Delivery benchmarks

| Scenario | Delivery time | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| US-based supplier with US stock, for US customers | 2 to 3 days (Source C source) | L0233.013 |
| High-ticket product from a US manufacturer that stocks in the US | 2 to 7 days (Source B, 2021 to 2022, older) | L0458.059 |
| Low-ticket items shipped from China to US customers | 1 to 4 weeks | L0458.059 |
| International dropshipping (supplier abroad) | 21 to 30 days | L0233.013 |
| Overseas supplier with no stock in your market | 2 to 4 weeks | L0360.006 |
| Repeat orders from supplier with local stock | 4 to 10 days (a first order taking 8 to 10 weeks is unusual) | L1111.035 |

- Customers in a country expect delivery within about a week, so local stock matches what they already expect. [L0458.059]
- Faster fulfillment reduces perceived time delay and effort/sacrifice, which multiplies the value of the same product. [L0233.013]
- Shipping time is a factor to review before choosing a supplier. Slow suppliers with long shipping times can hurt the customer experience and should be weighed against product, price and margin. [L0440.019]

#### Stock verification procedures

**Find where a supplier is based when you only know its product from another store (Source A, 2024):**

1. Find the supplier's product listed on a competitor's store. [L0360.005]
2. Google the supplier's name to find its own website and the country it operates from. [L0360.005]
3. Contact the supplier and ask whether it holds stock or uses a fulfillment center in the target market. [L0360.005]

**Check a foreign supplier's US stock before building a store (Source A, 2022, older):**

1. Find US retailers that sell the supplier's products. [L0854.020]
2. Contact them as a normal customer and ask whether the product is in stock and where it ships from. [L0854.020]
3. Alternatively, ask the supplier directly whether it has US distribution or a fulfillment center that ships to US customers. [L0854.020]

**Ask a supplier about its stock location by phone or email (Source A, 2018, older):**

1. Contact the company by phone, email, live chat, or Facebook message. [L0466.017]
2. Ask whether they keep the specific product in stock themselves and, if not, where it ships from. [L0466.017]
3. Phrase the question as a shopper's question, not as a competitor's question. [L0466.017]
4. If other sites seem to ship the same product from one location, mention it and ask whether they do the same. [L0466.017]
- Do not tell a company you are building a competing store when you ask whether they dropship; it makes the answer less reliable and can end the relationship. [L0466.018]
- If a supplier's location is unclear, check it directly rather than assume it holds stock. Most dropshipping websites can be identified from their site with about 80 percent certainty. [L0466.016]
- When contacting a foreign supplier, it is professional and inoffensive to ask whether they have a U.S. warehouse or fulfillment center before requesting authorization to sell their products. [L0276.004]
- A lead-time gap matters: contact the supplier, ask for its shipping and fulfillment location, and check whether it holds stock in your country. Overseas phone numbers do not always mean stock is overseas, so ask directly before deciding. [L0801.029]
- For a foreign-based supplier selling into a market, confirm that it stocks inventory in that country, for example through a local distribution center. Ask directly by phone or email, since this is common among international brands. [L1069.007]
- A store that says products from certain brands may not ship the same day is usually dropshipping those items from third-party warehouses it does not control. Only the brands it ships itself are stocked in its own warehouse, and those are the exceptions in the notice. [L0459.012]
- Suppliers may hand out an international product catalog without stock in your market. Check whether they have stock locally before committing to them. [L1111.053]
- A first order that takes 8 to 10 weeks while repeat orders ship within 4 to 10 days is unusual. Question the supplier about the stock location before accepting the arrangement. [L1111.035]
- If a supplier says it is US-based but ships from overseas, drop-shipping from there will cause slow shipping and is a problem. [L0941.024]

### Overseas and China sourcing

#### China

- Do not dropship from China. Long delivery times, inconsistent quality and unexpected import charges make customer experience poor; work with domestic suppliers instead. [L0359.025]
- Do not build a long-term model on drop-shipping from China. It creates more problems than it is worth. [L0469.027]
- Do not drop ship from China. Work with suppliers that hold stock in your target country. [L0671.039, L0671.178]
- Do not approve suppliers that only source from China or that produce products bearing company names or logos. Approve suppliers based in the US or other countries with brand-authorized inventory. [L0253.021]
- Do not dropship from AliExpress. [L1069.006]
- Products that are made in China or India can still be acceptable when the brand or importer holds stock locally. A seller should prefer those brands or importers. [L0852.121]
- Products made overseas but stored in US warehouses are fine. Avoid drop shipping from overseas, such as China, when stock is shipped from there. [L0941.024, L0963.082]
- Exception: a custom, high-demand item from a single overseas maker, where production and shipping time are acceptable to customers and the margin still works after all costs. Do not actively seek out overseas suppliers as a general sourcing strategy. [L0358.057]
- Brands that manufacture outside the US often work only through approved master distributors. Expect to apply to those distributors, and plan for smaller margins when buying through them. [L1068.037]

#### Other overseas suppliers

- Overseas suppliers are usually a poor fit for dropshipping because transit takes weeks, and freight on large or heavy products can approach the product price itself. [L0358.057]
- Shipping from a supplier abroad often costs about as much as the product, so international dropshipping is rarely worth it. It can only make sense for very small, light items that ship fast. [L0468.045]
- Do not drop-ship from other countries as a general rule, because shipping times and fees are often too high. Only consider it when the product is clearly in demand and customers accept long waits and extra cost. [L0834.030]
- Avoid suppliers who ship from overseas to the target market when the product also has US stock. Long international delivery times and high shipping costs erode both margin and customer trust. [L1111.034]
- Avoid dropshipping from Europe to North America or vice versa. Shipping costs are too high and reduce margins too much. If selling in Europe, create a separate store and source suppliers with inventory stocked in Europe. [L0270.063]
- Shipping from Europe to the US is often not cost-effective, especially for large or heavy goods. Check the shipping cost against the selling price before deciding; a long delivery time can be shown as a benefit if the margin still works. [L0357.021]
- Suppliers outside the US are acceptable when the products are only available there and quality is good. The risk to avoid is cheap, slow marketplace dropshipping, where delivery takes weeks, quality disappoints and customers keep asking where their order is. [L1086.033]
- Suppliers in nearby countries such as Europe can work if shipping remains affordable and no high import taxes apply. [L0962.013]
- Foreign-manufactured products are normal. Avoid suppliers that dropship from another country and look for stock in your country of business instead. [L0466.039]
- Some suppliers are shipped from abroad under FOB or online-only landing prices. These import terms are rarely used in this model. Confirm that the supplier already has stock in the country where you sell. [L0468.044]
- If a manufacturer is abroad, use a distributor only when the product is stocked in your country. Ask the manufacturer which distributor or local warehouse holds stock. [L0468.046]
- If a supplier abroad has no stock in your target country, be hesitant, because shipping costs and delivery times will be high. [L0671.040, L0671.179]
- Before selling a foreign supplier's products to customers in another country, ask whether the supplier has its own warehouse or a fulfillment center there with stock. If everything ships from abroad, shipping can be expensive and take two to four weeks. Such a supplier is usually not worth it unless it is a top-tier supplier. In that case, the origin and shipping time must be stated on every product page. [L0360.006]
- Foreign supplier and sale in a second market: a separate store that only targets that region, loaded only with products that should sell there at the needed prices, with traffic only to that region. [L0853.102]
- Use a matching country domain for a non-US market. A supplier's warehouse location affects lead times and costs. If suppliers are based in Europe and you sell from Europe, use a matching domain such as a.com.eu domain. [L0968.016]

#### Canada

- A US business address is not required to sell to US customers from Canada. If you display an address on your website, a forwarding address from a US location (that forwards mail to Canada) is recommended for legitimacy. [L0253.001]
- Many suppliers in Canada and other countries have a distribution center in the US, so ask for one before assuming high international shipping costs. Costs differ for each supplier and must be checked one by one. [L0357.027]
- Procedure for a supplier in Canada (or another country) that sells to US customers (Source A, 2019, older):
  1. Contact the supplier individually. [L0357.026]
  2. Introduce yourself and your location. [L0357.026]
  3. State that you sell in the US. [L0357.026]
  4. Ask whether the supplier has a US distribution center and whether there are extra fees or shipping charges for US delivery. [L0357.026]
- For Canada-based suppliers, first check whether they hold US inventory. If not, ask which carrier they use to ship to US customers and how they avoid border delays. [L0469.021]
- Ask suppliers if they ship to Canada before you add Canada to your store, and whether they already use a go-to shipper for it. Start with the US market first because it is the bigger market. [L0804.033]
- If products are already warehoused in the US, they can be sold to both the US and Canada. Choose Canadian suppliers only when the business sells only in Canada. [L0963.088]

#### UK, Europe and other markets

- Products from UK suppliers are acceptable only when no US source exists or US sellers all source from the UK. Product pages must state clearly that the products are made and shipped from the UK, with the estimated shipping time to the US. [L0467.029]
- Brexit and similar trade barriers make supplying UK customers from European warehouses more complicated. Check current import and export rules before sourcing across that border. [L0781.012]
- Operators in the UK may need to adjust the standard drop-ship model and explain the process to local suppliers who are not familiar with it. Successful UK sellers have documented those adjustments and shared them with their supplier partners. [L1068.082]
- The same MAP reseller rules are described as applying in the UK, Germany, Australia and the US. If a market appears to have no MAP for a brand, research how experienced resellers in that market handle the brand rather than assuming it cannot be sold. [L0663.077]

### Brand, importer, manufacturer and distributor

#### Who to contact

| Supplier type | Default | Use when | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Brand that owns the brand name, or its importer | Preferred | Brand sells direct to end customers at MSRP and allows resale at MAP | L0665.123, L0665.012, L1086.036 |
| Manufacturer selling to you | Preferred | Brand or manufacturer approves you directly | L0855.070, L0962.006 |
| Holding company that owns several brands | Acceptable | Owns the brands it sells | L1069.009 |
| Distributor, wholesaler or clearinghouse | Fallback | Only when no direct brand approval is possible, and after checks | L0665.012, L0962.006 |
| Website selling several brands at wholesale | Check first | Acceptable only if it is a distributor that enforces MAP | L0964.029, L0970.049 |

- Always start by seeking direct relationships with brands. Use importers, wholesalers or manufacturers that carry other brands only when you cannot get approved by enough direct suppliers. [L0962.006]
- Going directly to the supplier is preferred over a distributor. Only use a distributor after an extra layer of research, not by default. [L0359.024]
- Going straight to the brand gives you a direct contact, current product information, pricing control, and authorized retailer status. Direct brand relationships are the basis for profitable dropshipping. (Source A, 2018, older) [L0466.021]
- Contact brands directly, not distributors or wholesalers. If an overseas supplier has US warehouse presence and carries legitimate branded products (verifiable through competing stores), contact them. Never lead with general distributors as primary suppliers. [L0276.042]
- Prefer approval directly with the brand (the importer that owns the brand name) over a distributor, because distributors control MAP enforcement, pricing and relationship terms. Use a distributor only when no direct brand approval is possible. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0665.012]
- Distributors do not own the brands they sell and rarely control prices, so margins are lower. Avoid distributors when a direct supplier approval is possible. [L0855.069]
- Use dropship suppliers that own their brand and sell under it, not intermediaries, supplier directories, or dropshipping from China. The store sells the supplier's brand to the customer. [L0759.003]
- Work directly with brands or their authorized local importers, and avoid supplier directories and middlemen. Middlemen add cost and make it harder to get approved. (Source A, 2020, older) [L0852.024]
- Distributors that resell many brands as middlemen usually add a markup and often do not enforce MAP pricing. Buy directly from the brand whenever possible. [L0970.048]
- Do not contact brick-and-mortar retailers that also sell online for wholesale, because they have access to brands that only work with physical stores. Focus supplier contacts on brands that work with online-only stores, so approval is possible across them. [L0968.015]
- Work with the brand, since the brand name on the product is what matters. A brand may manufacture its own products or use one or several third-party manufacturers, so the setup behind it does not change which supplier you contract with. (Source A, 2019, older) [L1086.036]
- An importer that owns the brand name is the supplier to approach for an imported product, because it is the company that brings in and sells the product. [L0665.123]
- A holding company that owns several brands is an acceptable supplier, while a middleman distributor that does not own the brands should be replaced by direct brand relationships. (Source A, 2019, older) [L1069.009]
- A supplier is any company you can be approved to sell for directly. Manufacturers can also be suppliers, but some suppliers do not manufacture their own products. (Source A, 2023) [L0855.070]
- Verify the supplier's business model with these checks:
  - A company whose site looks like an agency or marketing firm can still be a wholesale distributor of brands. Check whether it lists brands and wholesale distribution before adding its contact details. [L0940.025]
  - A website selling several brands is not always a distributor, since it can be the parent company that owns those brands. Identify which model the seller follows before deciding whether to work with it. [L0970.049]
  - A third-party website that sells a supplier's products at wholesale is only acceptable if it is a distributor that still enforces MAP. If it is a company that buys from the supplier and resells, do not list those products. [L0964.029]
  - When the same company name appears on two supplier lists with different wholesale prices, it may be a distributor rather than the brand itself. Ask the supplier to explain the difference and keep a screenshot as evidence. (Source A, 2020, older) [L0852.025]

#### When a distributor is acceptable

- When a brand routes business only through a distributor, it is acceptable to work with that distributor. Before doing so, ask the distributor whether it enforces MAP policies and whether its markup leaves enough margin for the store. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0429.026]
- A distributor is acceptable only if it is the brand's wholesale arm that manages everything, enforces MAP, and gives you a margin you can compete with. Before deciding, request price lists, check whether MAP is enforced, and check the margin and competition. (Source A, 2023) [L0835.028]
- Distributors are acceptable only if they enforce MAP pricing and the product margins are significant after their markup. Compare distributor price lists before committing. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0962.009]
- Example: in medical equipment for home use, many manufacturers all pointed to one distributor that carried their full range. The distributor enforced MAP pricing and the margins were good, so that route was viable. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0962.010]
- A supplier that sells through a distributor or dealer network may not offer a clean drop-ship account. Only work with it if it is the only way to win the brand, which happens for a very small share of suppliers. (Source A, 2020, older) [L0468.056]
- Working through a distributor is a fallback when a brand sells only through distributors. It is worth doing if the products sell, but direct relationships with the brand are the goal. Distributors often have no MAP policy, so exclude their products from the price match policy. (Source A, 2019, older) [L1069.008]
- A seller in a country should prefer brands or importers that hold stock locally. A distributor is acceptable only when no direct brand link exists. (Source A, 2020, older) [L0852.121]
- In one past case about 90 percent of brands in a niche sold through distributors, and the distributor enforced MAP policies. Such cases are rare, so contacting the distributor can still be worth it when a store is already built around that niche. (Source A, 2023) [L1094.011]
- Some suppliers claim they must work through distributors by region because of non-compete clauses. First confirm whether they actually enforce MAP; if they do not, the distributor requirement is not a real barrier and the niche can still be pursued. (Source A, 2021, older) [L1090.017]
- Distributors can be harder to get authorized, so mention the name of the person or company that referred you when you contact them. Ask early whether they enforce pricing policies, since not all distributors do. (Source A, 2023) [L0470.092]

#### Distributor checks

- Some brands only sell through a distributor or clearinghouse. If that is the case, vet the intermediary on MAP enforcement, pricing policy, policy toward competitors, how hard approval is, and shipping speed before listing its products. [L0359.023]
- A distributor that is not a manufacturer usually makes profit harder to earn, and distributors often have no MAP policy. Contact the individual brands the distributor sells, because direct relationships give better pricing. (Source A, 2019, older) [L0357.022]
- For supplier distributors such as TD Synnex or Ingram Micro, check whether they have a MAP policy. If none is enforced, do not use a price match guarantee and confirm the margins still work. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0963.087]
- Do not work with a supplier that has granted sole distributorship for a market to a physical plus online store there. Pass on that supplier instead of competing with the exclusive partner. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0962.011]
- Some suppliers route new retailers to third-party distribution centers, and those centers may refuse new accounts or ask for a large first order. Ask each center directly whether it opens accounts for new retailers before counting that supplier as available. Treat a refusal from a distribution center as a supplier-level blocker to work around, not as a final answer for the whole niche. (Source A, 2025) [L0966.107]
- A distributor that bans returns while competitors and the manufacturer offer 30-day returns is a warning sign. Move on to other suppliers and reapply directly after three to six months of established business. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0429.062]
- Brands that manufacture outside the US often work only through approved master distributors. (Source A, 2019, older) [L1068.037]

### MAP policy

#### Definition and purpose

- Minimum Advertised Price (MAP) is a pricing floor set by suppliers that prevents authorized retailers from advertising products below that price, protecting fair margins for all sellers and preventing destructive price competition. [L0110.005]
- Minimum advertised price (MAP) is the lowest price a retailer may advertise a supplier's product for. When a retailer lists below it, a supplier that enforces MAP contacts that retailer, who must raise the price or lose its authorization to sell. [L0711.023]
- MAP is a contractual floor: an authorized retailer signs an agreement not to advertise the product below a set price, usually calculated as wholesale cost multiplied by a stated factor. Some suppliers have no MAP at all, and in some countries MAP policies cannot be enforced by law. Check whether a supplier enforces MAP before judging it. [L1051.001]
- Brands use MAP to protect their brand value and their retail partners: if one retailer sells below the floor, others lose sales and the perceived value of the product drops. MAP therefore keeps prices stable across all sellers. [L1051.002]
- MSRP is the manufacturer's suggested retail price and MAP is the minimum advertised price. A supplier that sells online at MSRP while letting you sell at MAP gives you a price advantage over the supplier's own website. (Source A, 2018, older) [L1085.012]
- Silver and gold suppliers expect you not to list their products below their Minimum Advertised Price (MAP). Undercutting their pricing breaks the supplier trust relationship. [L0121.001]
- As an Authorized Retailer you represent the supplier's brand. Poor customer experiences with your business create negative associations that damage the supplier's brand reputation, not just your own. [L0121.002]

#### Testing MAP enforcement

- Search for a supplier's products on Google Shopping before calling. Widely varying prices for the same product suggest that MAP is not enforced, which is a red flag. (Source A, 2019, older) [L1068.035]
- Procedure to check whether a supplier enforces MAP (Source A, 2021, older):
  1. Search Google for the supplier's company name. [L0853.039]
  2. Look at the supplier's most popular products in Google Shopping. [L0853.039]
  3. Compare the prices sellers list for the same product. [L0853.039]
  4. If prices vary widely, the supplier probably does not enforce MAP. [L0853.039]
- Procedure for a brand that unauthorized sellers list at MAP (Source A, 2023):
  1. Keep advertising your own authorized status and the brand's warranty. [L0970.050]
  2. Ask the brand to contact the unauthorized sellers and have their listings removed. [L0970.050]
  3. If that does not work, the brand can file a DMCA notice with the host company of the listing site. [L0970.050]
- Sellers should check a supplier's MAP enforcement before approval. Being approved without a MAP policy to sign is a red flag that the supplier does not enforce MAP. (Source A, 2023) [L1094.017]
- A supplier that approves a retailer without sending a MAP policy to sign is a red flag, since it likely has no MAP policy at all. Check whether a supplier enforces MAP before relying on it. (Source A, 2023) [L1094.121]
- A supplier that says it has no control over MAP prices is not a true MAP supplier. For such suppliers, keep selling only if the products sell at MAP and traffic is profitable. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0429.024]
- When a supplier with a MAP policy does not enforce it, it is usually not worth staying with them. First contact them directly and make the problem clear before deciding to drop them. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0665.013]
- Competing stores should not be allowed to disrupt MAP through discounts. A non-competing store that advertises your brand keywords and sells other brands at low prices can be addressed by contacting the brand that you sell for, which can ask Google to block the advertiser from bidding on its brand name. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0665.021]
- Ideally every supplier carries a MAP policy. A small number without one, such as two out of twenty, can be accepted. Those brands must be excluded from the price match policy, and their competitor prices should be checked before any ads run. (Source A, 2019, older) [L1069.092]
- Suppliers that enforce MAP and ship on your own account are preferable to non-MAP suppliers. Shipping on your own account can feel intimidating at first, but it becomes routine once you work with several suppliers. (Source A, 2023) [L0835.027]

#### Non-MAP suppliers

- A supplier without MAP pricing is not an automatic rejection. MAP suppliers are preferred, but the decision depends on what competitors charge for the same products. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0671.008, L0671.159]
- Supplier MAP policies are enforced, but many MAP suppliers are workable. Non-MAP suppliers can also be profitable, though some countries do not allow MAP, so the decision depends on the market and what products are available. (Source A, 2025) [L0472.021]
- Non-MAP suppliers can be a valid first step. Having live products on your store increases trust, which can help you get approved by other suppliers. (Source A, 2024) [L0471.039]
- Non-MAP supplier research, in order (Source A):
  1. Apply to every brand in your niche that you can get approved for (example: 50 brands means contacting all 50), and collect their price lists. Being an authorized retailer does not oblige you to sell their products. [L1051.003]
  2. Identify which suppliers are MAP and which are not from the price lists. [L1051.003]
  3. For each non-MAP supplier, build your own price list with columns for Wholesale, MSRP (manufacturer suggested retail price) and Competition. [L1051.003]
  4. Search each product name on Google Shopping as a customer would, and record the average competitor price in the Competition column. Use established, legitimate-looking stores for the average. [L1051.003]
  5. Check each supplier's average competitor price against its wholesale cost using the 2x rule (see next units). [L1051.003]
  6. Only upload products that pass the 2x rule; skip the rest without regret. [L1051.003]
  7. Monitor KPIs in the Google Ads account on a fixed schedule (see below). [L1051.003]

### Direct-to-consumer conflict

#### How common and how to judge it

- Roughly 20% of suppliers also sell directly to consumers on their own website, and most do not. A supplier's direct website only leaves room for profitable drop shipping when the site is weak, outdated, slow, has poor images or poor product descriptions. (Source A, 2020, older) [L0664.015]
- If a supplier's own site is polished and offers too little discount for you to afford paid traffic, skip that supplier. If the site is weak, you can still compete with a bonus gift, expiring coupon codes or by matching the price. (Source A, 2020, older) [L0664.016]
- A supplier's own online store is not a red flag if it sells at MSRP and allows you to sell at MAP. Weak supplier websites (one photo, poor descriptions, missing features) are an opening to take sales with a better product page. (Source A, 2018, older) [L1085.013]
- A manufacturer that sells direct to consumers is not automatically a problem. It is harder to compete with when its site uses advanced pixel tracking and is clearly built for online selling, but it is fine when online sales are only an add-on to a brand website. (Source A, 2024) [L0965.025]
- Meta Pixel Helper is a free Chrome extension that lights up with the number of Meta pixels on any site you visit. Several pixels, such as a page view plus an AMP page view, show that a supplier or competitor is investing in direct online sales. (Source A, 2024) [L0965.026]
- Check whether a supplier advertises directly to consumers (Source A, 2023):
  1. Search the brand name on Google. [L0804.024]
  2. Check Google Shopping for the brand's listings. [L0804.024]
  3. Check the search text ads at the top of Google. [L0804.024]
  4. Check whether you see their Facebook remarketing ads. [L0804.024]

#### Conditions for selling a brand that sells direct

- Sell for suppliers who sell direct to consumers only if: (1) they sell at MSRP and allow retailers to sell at MAP, or (2) their direct-to-consumer experience is poor and you can provide superior UX, more information, and an easier buying process. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0249.012]
- A supplier that sells direct to the public can still be acceptable if its direct price equals MSRP and it lets retailers sell at MAP. Example: MSRP of 1,500 USD for direct sales and MAP of 1,200 USD for retailers. (Source A, 2021, older) [L1090.015]
- A supplier that sells direct can also be acceptable if its own online store shows clear neglect, such as outdated design, slow loading, broken links, poor product images or weak information. In that case a retailer can provide a better shopping experience. (Source A, 2021, older) [L1090.016]
- Suppliers that sell direct to the public at MSRP while allowing retailers to sell at MAP are workable. Avoid suppliers with strong direct-to-consumer websites that compete with your store, unless their site looks outdated and shows little effort. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0968.014]
- Suppliers selling directly to the public at MSRP that allow retailers to sell at MAP are acceptable. Suppliers selling direct where e-commerce is weak can still be profitable. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0969.043]
- Supplier with a sale on its own website: add products only if it sells at MSRP and allows retailers to sell at MAP, or sells direct but shows little investment in its e-commerce site. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0969.044]
- A supplier who sells direct to consumers is acceptable if they sell at MSRP while retailers sell at MAP, or if their direct site is weak and unmarketed. If they run a strong direct-to-consumer business with ads and remarketing, expect your margin to be eroded. (Source A, 2023) [L0804.022]
- Sell a brand that also sells direct to consumers only if you gain an advantage: either the supplier sells at MSRP while you sell at MAP, or the supplier's site looks outdated so you look more trustworthy. Otherwise do not sell that brand. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0803.031]
- Sell a brand that sells direct only if the supplier sells at MSRP while you sell at MAP, or if it sells direct from a weak site. Avoid suppliers with strong direct-to-consumer websites that compete with your store. [L0247.021]
- Suppliers that sell both online and through physical retail locations are still viable. Cross-check their pricing on competitor stores and Amazon to ensure they are not undercutting your pricing significantly. Allow them through your approval process unless you identify specific margin or MAP policy issues. (Source A, 2023) [L0251.003]
- A supplier that sells direct to consumers at MSRP, with a physical showroom, is acceptable if you can still sell at MAP. Supplier retail pricing should give you room to sell below it. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0853.101]
- Choose suppliers whose competitors have no retail store, showroom or warehouse presence. Check competitor websites for this before approaching suppliers, because suppliers with retail presence often refuse drop shipping. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0671.038, L0671.177]

#### Undercutting risk

- Suppliers can sell to the public directly and undercut retailers, which is a warning sign. If a supplier's recommended retail prices or its own site sell below what you can offer, do not list that brand or negotiate price parity before advertising it. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0801.030]
- Avoid suppliers that sell directly to the public and would undercut your MAP price. Prefer suppliers who sell only to businesses and let you sell at MAP. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0469.024]
- If a supplier sells directly to the public at a lower price than your planned selling price, do not upload that supplier's products when you know this in advance. If you find out later, keep the products on the store but do not include them in Google Ads. (Source A, 2020, older) [L0551.015]

### Marketplace and channel restrictions

- If a supplier forbids selling on Google, Amazon, eBay, Facebook or Bing, ask for clarification, because most suppliers only restrict marketplaces. Ask how the store is expected to advertise before deciding to work with them. (Source A, 2020, older) [L0967.028]
- Suppliers that sell on Wayfair are not a problem, because large marketplaces often deliver poorly and treat their suppliers badly. Suppliers may then prefer to send business directly to the seller. (Source A, 2018, older) [L1085.015]
- If a supplier requires an inquire-only button and no cart, do not list its products, because Google Shopping ads need products that can be bought directly. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0671.059]
- Some suppliers require that orders are shipped using the retailer's own shipping account, and some do not accept customer returns at all. (Source A, 2025) [L0837.041]

### Product restrictions that affect supplier choice

- Battery-powered items such as Electric Bicycles, Electric Scooters, Go-Karts, Golf Carts and RC Cars, RC Planes, RC Boats and RC Helicopters can face shipping restrictions for their batteries. Confirm the carrier's and supplier's lithium battery rules before committing to these items. [L0546.016]
- Products with a built-in battery often cannot be shipped to the US. Confirm with the supplier or carrier that a battery product can reach the target country before sourcing it or running ads. Discovering a shipping restriction after ads are live wastes test budget. [L0552.052]
