- On AliExpress, a product needs at least one listing with a 4.6+ rating and at least 50 reviews, ideally with all listings at 4.6+. If no seller reaches 4.6+, the product is probably low quality. [L0636.011]
- Skip AliExpress products with more than 10,000 orders, because high order counts mean the product is already heavily scaled and riskier to sell. [L0636.012]
- Check consumer reviews for damage before choosing a brand (Source A, 2019, older):
  1. Search for the brand's products on Google as a customer would. [L0663.039]
  2. Read the consumer reviews. [L0663.039]
  3. Look for a repeated pattern of items arriving damaged. [L0663.039]
  4. Avoid or adjust products where damage is a trend, especially if you must ship on your own account. [L0663.039]

### Supplier vetting procedures

- Procedure to confirm a brand is legitimate (Source A):
  1. Research brand by searching on Google. [L0255.007]
  2. Search brand name alone. [L0255.007]
  3. Search brand name with 'reviews'. [L0255.007]
  4. Evaluate search results for supplier reliability and reputation issues. [L0255.007]
  5. If problems appear, avoid listing their products. [L0255.007]
- Procedure to confirm a brand's own website and independence (Source A):
  1. Search Google for each brand name in Column A. [L0056.009]
  2. Find and visit the brand's official website. [L0056.009]
  3. If it is a private label or marketplace-exclusive product, delete the row. [L0056.009]
  4. Confirm the supplier operates independently. [L0056.009]
- Procedure to confirm a supplier serves only online stores (Source A, 2019, older):
  1. Search Google for the niche. [L1069.003]
  2. Use only the websites of brands that sell exclusively online, and skip brick-and-mortar retailers. [L1069.003]
  3. Confirm the company has no showroom and no warehouse; an office alone is acceptable. [L1069.003]
  4. Source supplier names from the websites of your true future competitors, since suppliers found there are more likely to work with online-only stores. [L1069.003]
- Supplier traffic rank does not matter for choosing a supplier, since suppliers usually do not try to attract traffic. A competitor's traffic rank is a useful signal instead. (Source A, 2020, older) [L0551.013]
- Search for a supplier's products on Google Shopping before calling, and treat widely varying prices as a red flag. (Source A, 2019, older) [L1068.035]
- Before judging a supplier, check whether it enforces MAP. Some suppliers have no MAP at all. [L1051.001]

### Supplier responsiveness, returns and support

- Before approval, make sure a supplier answers the phone and emails. A brand that never answers may not support products after approval. (Source A, 2020, older) [L1111.041]
- If a supplier cannot answer technical product questions such as steel type or weight capacity, treat that as a red flag for working with the supplier. If a question is about product selection (such as which barbell suits a given body weight and lift), create a buying guide on your site rather than dismiss the question. (Source A, 2022, older) [L0429.035]
- A supplier that offers no return policy at all is a warning sign, and it is more worrying when every approved supplier has the same policy. Investigate those suppliers before building products on them. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0853.003]
- Do not buy an expensive supplier demo product just to learn their customer support, because such requests are unusual. Only consider it when there is a strong resale market, and check first how quickly the product can be resold. (Source A, 2019, older) [L0356.032]
- Avoid suppliers that expect the retailer to provide local support, repairs or solutions to customers' technical problems. These demands move a heavy support burden onto the store. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0247.028]
- Some suppliers request an opening order before granting catalog access. Respond that you need assurance you can sell their products at the required price, and keep contacting other suppliers in the meantime. (Source A, 2021, older) [L0247.029]
- A store that says products from certain brands may not ship the same day is usually dropshipping those items from third-party warehouses it does not control. [L0459.012]

### Choosing a supplier by motivation

- Choose a supplier based on your motivation: speed, quality or price, because these three priorities lead to very different supplier choices. [L0780.024]
- Shipping time is a factor to review before choosing a supplier, weighed against product, price and margin. [L0440.019]
- Partner with US-based suppliers achieving 2 to 3 day delivery instead of international dropshippers with 21 to 30 day turnaround. Faster fulfillment reduces perceived time delay and effort/sacrifice, dramatically multiplying the value of the same product. [L0233.013]

### Red flags and rejection signals

| Signal | Action | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Supplier only sources from China, or products bear company names or logos | Do not approve | L0253.021 |
| Supplier says it has no control over MAP prices | Keep only if products sell at MAP and traffic is profitable | L0429.024 |
| Approval granted without a MAP policy to sign | Treat as sign of no MAP enforcement | L1094.017, L1094.121 |
| Supplier cannot answer technical product questions | Treat as red flag | L0429.035 |
| Supplier never answers phone or email | Do not rely on it after approval | L1111.041 |
| Supplier offers no return policy, or distributor bans returns | Investigate before building products on it | L0853.003, L0429.062 |
| Inquire-only button and no cart | Do not list products | L0671.059 |
| Supplier requires an opening order before catalog access | Respond with price assurance request and keep contacting others | L0247.029 |
| Supplier expects local support and repairs | Avoid | L0247.028 |
| Supplier sells direct and undercuts your price | Do not list or negotiate price parity before advertising | L0801.030 |
| Same company name, different wholesale prices on two lists | Ask for an explanation and keep evidence | L0852.025 |
| First order 8 to 10 weeks, repeat orders 4 to 10 days | Question stock location | L1111.035 |
| Distribution center refuses new retailers | Treat as blocker to work around | L0966.107 |
| Website sells several brands at wholesale and buys from suppliers to resell | Do not list those products | L0964.029 |
| Supplier is approved but its products are not sold by other online-only stores | Check dropship friendliness first | L0359.022 |
| Supplier that forbids marketplaces | Ask for clarification before deciding | L0967.028 |

### Where sources disagree

- **Overseas sourcing.** The strict position is no dropshipping from China or from abroad by default [L0359.025, L0671.039, L0671.178, L0963.082, L0469.027, L0834.030]. The exceptions are a custom, high-demand single overseas maker where delivery and margin work [L0358.057], a nearby country such as Europe where shipping is affordable and no high import taxes apply [L0962.013], an outside-US supplier whose product is unique and of good quality [L1086.033], and a UK supplier only when no US source exists [L0467.029]. The 2019 position that long delivery can be a benefit if the margin still works is older and applies only when the margin holds after shipping [L0357.021].
- **Origin versus stock location.** A product made in China is not a problem when a brand or importer holds it locally [L0852.121, L0941.024, L1085.014, L0466.039]. The problem is shipping from overseas. Use stock location as the test, not the country of manufacture. [L0963.082, L0941.024]
- **Delivery numbers.** The 2 to 3 day figure for US suppliers comes from Source C [L0233.013]. The 2 to 7 day figure for a high-ticket US manufacturer comes from Source B, 2021 to 2022, older [L0458.059]. Both describe US stock. Use the number from your own supplier's stated process.
- **Non-MAP suppliers.** Red flags are an approval with no MAP policy to sign and a supplier that has no control over MAP [L1094.121, L1094.017, L0429.024]. Non-MAP suppliers remain workable as a first step, when competitor prices support the margin, when a small share of brands lacks MAP, or in markets where MAP cannot be enforced [L0671.008, L0671.159, L0471.039, L1069.092, L0472.021, L1051.003]. The distinction is whether the supplier enforces MAP, not whether it has a policy on paper.
- **Distributors.** The default is direct brand approval first [L0962.006, L0359.024, L0665.012, L0855.069]. Distributors are accepted when they enforce MAP and leave enough margin [L0962.009, L0835.028, L0429.026], when they are the only route to the brand [L0468.056], or as a fallback that is not the goal [L1069.008]. The 2019 statement that distributors often have no MAP is older and still applies as a check [L0357.022].
- **Direct-to-consumer.** The 2021 position is that a strong supplier store that would match your price is not worth the time [L0247.021]. The 2023 position is that omnichannel suppliers are viable unless specific margin or MAP issues appear [L0251.003]. Both apply: check the pricing on competitor stores and Amazon, and decline a strong direct store that would match your price [L0247.021, L0251.003, L0804.022].
- **Showrooms.** A supplier showroom selling at MSRP is acceptable when you can still sell at MAP [L0853.101]. The same chapter advises choosing suppliers whose competitors have no retail presence, because retail presence often leads to refusals [L0671.038, L0671.177]. Treat showroom presence as acceptable only after you have checked whether the supplier approves drop shipping.
- **Distributors for region versus MAP.** A regional distributor requirement is not a barrier if the supplier does not enforce MAP [L1090.017]. Distributors that do enforce MAP are the exception that justifies working through them [L0962.009, L1094.011].

### Supplier Location and Domestic Stock

#### Start Local

- At the start, work only with suppliers based in the country where you sell, who already stock the products there, either directly or through a local distributor or subsidiary of an overseas parent company. For the EU, the products should ideally already be in the EU, possibly in several EU countries. [L1480.010]
- Do not start by shipping products across borders, for example from China into your sales market. Cross-border shipping can come later, but it is not the starting model. [L1480.011]
- Work only with local suppliers in the same country as the target customers, not with overseas manufacturers, so the product ships domestically. [L1481.013]
- Local, independent, established suppliers make operations much easier in many ways. Suppliers are usually experienced and open to working with new sellers. [L1480.012]
- Local suppliers in a market with a strong e-commerce and retail industry often have established, tested, high-quality products and are looking for online retail partners to sell them. [L1481.014]

#### Overseas Manufacturing and US Stock

- Products may be manufactured overseas, for example in Italy, as long as the supplier keeps the stock in a US warehouse that the orders ship from; the headquarters location does not matter. [L1113.020]
- Products from suppliers are usually from US-based brands that hold stock in the US, not products made in the US. Brands can be from the same country as the store's shipping base, and the store's suppliers do not need to manufacture locally. [L1114.017]
- Source from suppliers with stock or a warehouse in the United States. Dropshipping from Europe to the U.S. is generally too expensive to be workable. [L1274.034]

#### Checking Stock Readiness

- When a supplier cannot ship a product from U.S. stock right away, check whether it has a set of bestselling products that are always kept in its U.S. warehouse. Only list those in-stock bestsellers as U.S.-ready products. [L1115.007]

### Brand, Distributor and Importer

#### Source Direct

- Source directly from the brand rather than from wholesalers, because direct margins are much better. [L1113.027]
- Work directly with manufacturers rather than distributors or middlemen. Manufacturers usually carry several sub-categories of products that you can select from. [L1274.022]
- Buy from the brand itself rather than from a large retailer that resells its products. The closer you are to the actual brand, the better. [L1275.022]
- A brand that sells direct to consumers will often refuse a dropshipper at first, which is normal. Use the same approval steps and expect the relationship to be slightly different. [L1275.021]

#### When a Distributor Is Acceptable

- Use a distributor only when the supplier sells exclusively through that distributor. Also confirm the distributor enforces price policies; a middleman that adds a markup and has no pricing controls should be avoided when the supplier can be contacted directly. [L1283.009]
- Only work with a distributor if it enforces MAP. Brands that sell outside the distributor can often be approached directly. [L1275.026]
- In a niche where most brands only sell through a distributor, the rest of the brands (around 5 to 10 percent) may still be available for direct approval. Check MAP enforcement before signing up with the distributor. [L1275.027]
- Suppliers that sell direct to consumers and use distributors in the US may raise prices or limit stock for resellers in shortages. Distributors are therefore not a preferred source. [L1112.047]

#### Conditions for Online Sellers

- Some suppliers only approve stores that can show they sell online. Suppliers that also sell to brick-and-mortar retailers are acceptable if you found them through online-only retailers. [L1285.019]

### Direct-to-Consumer Conflict

- A supplier that already sells direct to consumers on Google Shopping is only worth approaching if it sells at MSRP and allows you to sell at MAP, or if its own product pages are weak enough that you can beat them. Otherwise, skip it. [L1113.088]

### MAP Policy and Enforcement

#### What MAP Is

- MAP stands for minimum advertised price, the lowest price a retailer may advertise for a product. After approval, the supplier gives you a MAP price list, and you may never list a product below its MAP. [L1422.004]
- Example: if supplier ABC sets the MAP at $1,000, no retailer may advertise its products below $1,000, and that includes you. [L1422.005]
- Track supplier price lists and MAP policies in writing from the supplier. Verify them before listing products because enforcement varies by supplier. [L1281.122]

#### Why MAP Matters

- A supplier often already has about five other retailers selling its products. Those retailers depend on stable prices to keep their margins, so MAP violations hurt all of them. [L1422.006]
- Undercutting MAP, such as selling a product with a $1,000 MAP for $700, may take all the sales from competing retailers. Those retailers then lose margin, get upset, and may stop carrying the brand, which damages the supplier's market. [L1422.007]
- MAP protects the profit margins of retailers, so good retailers keep stocking the brand. Suppliers want as many quality retailers as possible and will act against anyone who breaks the pricing policy. [L1422.008]

#### Checking Coupons and Discounts

- Each supplier's MAP agreement should be checked for whether discounts or coupons are allowed. If coupons are not allowed, the agreement will say so, and MAP software usually checks the displayed price, so a discount shown on the page is what gets flagged. [L1114.023]

#### Testing Enforcement in MAP Markets

- MAP rules are country-specific, but most countries outside the United States have no MAP. In those markets, you should apply for approval with every supplier you find, not only with suppliers who enforce MAP. [L1146.001]
- In MAP markets such as the United States, test whether a supplier enforces MAP by checking whether its retailers all sell at the same price, not below the minimum threshold. This test does not apply in non-MAP markets. [L1146.016]

**Procedure: Check MAP enforcement before contacting a supplier (older advice, 2018)**

1. Search the brand names from your niche research on Google.
2. Find each brand's best-selling products on the websites of the sellers that appear.
3. Take the product names and SKUs of those best sellers and search them on Google Shopping.
4. Compare the prices that different sellers list.
5. Consistent prices suggest MAP is enforced. Widely varying prices, such as $500, $550 and $800 for the same product, suggest it is probably not enforced, even if the supplier has a MAP policy. [L1274.025]

- If a product sells for $1,000 at five stores while one store sells it for $600, treat this as a red flag. Avoid advertising that supplier because its prices are not controlled. [L1274.026]

#### Regions with Restricted MAP (older advice, 2018)

- In regions where MAP policies are restricted, such as parts of Europe and possibly Australia according to the call, drop the price match policy. Screen every supplier more carefully to ensure no one is selling their products at a loss. [L1274.041]

### Marketplace Restrictions

- A brand that is already sold on large online-only retailers such as Wayfair can still be considered a dropship-friendly supplier. Being listed on a big marketplace does not on its own disqualify a brand. [L1223.034]
- Products sold on AliExpress may be near-copies of a legitimate brand's product, which can lead to a supplier being shut down and to legal trouble. A product that is suddenly removed from AliExpress may have been taken down after a rights holder complaint. [L1440.016]

### Drop-Ship Friendliness Checks

#### Quotes, Pricing and Fulfillment

- Source and validate the supplier before selling: get proper quotes, confirm pricing and confirm that the supplier can fulfill the product. [L1259.054]
- Get proper quotes, confirm prices and confirm the supplier can fulfill the item before selling. Contact several suppliers as backups to compare prices and quality and to ensure after-sale support and return policy. [L1260.055]

#### Pre-Upload Quality Check

- Before uploading a supplier's products, search the brand online for reviews and product quality. Check that the listing is worth the effort, since you will rely on organic traffic. [L1277.045]

## Supplier Count Targets and Niche Viability

### Why the Supplier Count Matters

- Multiple suppliers reduce business risk by avoiding dependence on one vendor, increase buyer confidence through brand variety, and build store credibility. Establish this foundation first, because a stronger product base makes conversion optimization work significantly more effective. [L0132.002]
- Supplier depth is one of the two primary levers for growing revenue, so a dropshipping business should secure as many suppliers as possible within its chosen niche. [L0512.003]
- Supplier count matters because not every brand you contact will approve you. You need enough brands that some saying no still leaves enough products on the site for the market to be viable. [L1484.003]
- Multiple supply sources reduce risk, because a supplier can close, have an owner stroke, or move on to another project. (older, 2021) [L0801.035]
- Expect the 80/20 pattern, where about 80 percent of sales come from about 20 percent of suppliers, so keep approaching many suppliers and following up over time. (older, 2022) [L1113.017]

### Minimum Supplier Count per Niche

- Set a minimum of 20 suppliers per niche before committing to it. Supplier availability is checked before the product count, so the niche can still be widened if the count is short. (older, 2019) [L0356.030]
- Before committing to a niche, confirm that at least 20 suppliers are available for it. [L1080.003]
- Before choosing a niche, identify at least 20 potential suppliers in your research. (older, 2022) [L1113.010]
- Aim for at least 20 different brands or suppliers per niche. If a niche cannot reach 20, niche up a level and add related products to the range. [L0359.021]
- Build a master supplier list of at least 20 brands before outreach begins, then aim to become authorized with every viable brand on that list over the long term. [L0191.003]
- Research at least 20 suppliers before starting outreach, because approval rates from day one are low for new stores. [L0928.012]
- Build a list of at least 20 different suppliers before you start calling. If one call goes badly, you still have 19 others to approach, so a weak first attempt does not end sourcing. [L0927.003]
- During niche research, find at least 20 different brands you could work with. Not all will approve on day one, which is acceptable. (older, 2021) [L1279.018]
- For the first store, find at least 20 brands or suppliers. Many will not approve, some will have low margins or no MAP, and some may charge for access, so the extra count is needed for a working store. (older, 2020) [L0967.017]
- Research should yield at least 20 suppliers, and ideally 50 to 100 or more, to work through. [L0938.002]
- Expect about 20 suppliers per niche as a minimum target, and as many suppliers as meet the criteria if there are more. (older, 2023) [L0964.035]
- Plan to find at least 20 suppliers before launching a first store, so enough remain when many of them decline. (older, 2019) [L1069.002]
- Find at least 20 potential brands per niche before launching, and confirm they sell through online-only stores without warehouses so the niche is drop-ship friendly. Brands that sell through distributors with warehouses usually do not drop ship. (2023) [L1094.010]
- Require at least 20 approved brands and at least 100 products per store as a minimum. Expect some suppliers to reject the application, so aim for a cushion. (older, 2020) [L1111.042]
- A store needs at least 100 unique products from at least 20 different suppliers. Each supplier should contribute several products rather than just one. (older, 2019) [L0663.046]
- Aim for at least 20 supplier candidates early, because some rejections are expected. That gives enough suppliers to launch, build traffic and make sales before the rest are approved. (older, 2021) [L0469.032]
- Aim for a supplier count of at least 20 brands in a niche during research. (older, 2021) [L0834.013]
- Contact at least 20 suppliers, because not every supplier approves a new seller on the first application. Approval standards have not become easier or harder over time. (older, 2020) [L1088.007]
- Before testing a niche's competition, source at least 20 suppliers. More suppliers are always better, and 20 is the minimum for the supplier competition test. (2024) [L0971.001]
- Contact at least 20 suppliers to see the range of margin differences, because some will refuse and others will offer better margins. (2024) [L0666.020]
- Aim to get approved with at least 20 suppliers in a niche, then put paid ad budget only on products from suppliers that have real margins. (older, 2021) [L0671.004]
- A competitor site listing 20 or more brands and at least 100 products shows enough variety, even if you do not recognize the brands. Use competitor catalogues to gauge this. (2023) [L0934.029]
- Working toward about 20 suppliers on your list runs alongside the first ads, so keep the list growing while traffic runs. (2023) [L0855.055]

### Market-Specific Minimums

Source B's market thresholds (2021-22, older) are lower than Source A's 20-supplier standard. Both values apply to different situations.

| Market | Minimum brands or suppliers | When it applies | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| USA | 20 or more | Standard US target; 20 is the threshold, 100 or more is fine (older, 2021-22) | [L1484.002, L1484.001] |
| Australia | More than 10 for the niche; 18 is acceptable (older, 2021-22) | Smaller market with fewer suppliers and customers, so lower competition | [L1484.002, L0490.005, L1484.004] |
| UK | 10 or more (older, 2021-22) | Smaller market | [L1484.002] |
| Any other market outside the USA | 10 or more (older, 2021-22) | Non-US markets generally | [L1484.002] |
| Smaller countries such as the UK, Australia or Canada (Source A standard) | At least 20 suppliers you can sell from before launching the niche | Standard target; fewer is acceptable only as a temporary stage | [L0935.001] |

- For a US niche, 18 suppliers would be low; the same count is acceptable for Australia. (older, 2021-22) [L0490.005]
- Smaller markets have fewer suppliers and fewer customers, so a lower brand threshold of 10 is accepted because competition is also lower. (older, 2021-22) [L1484.004]
- Some operators have built stores abroad with well under 20 suppliers. This carries more risk and should be treated as an exception rather than a plan, so the 20-supplier target remains the standard even when it requires a broader niche. [L0935.004]

### Market Size and Warehouse-Heavy Markets

- Supplier counts scale with population. The US, with roughly 330 to 350 million people, has far more suppliers per niche than smaller markets. Expect to find fewer suppliers for the same product in a small country and plan research accordingly. [L0935.002]
- Aim for at least 20 suppliers that sell into the country where you do business. Finding 60 suppliers in the US is more than enough for a niche. (older, 2022) [L0963.081]
- In markets where most suppliers keep a warehouse, expect lower approval rates. Contact a larger number of potential suppliers (for example 50 to 60 instead of 20) in the niche, and if too few suppliers exist, consider a broader niche to get enough products. (older, 2022) [L0969.014]
- For a store in a country outside the US, the main constraint is usually supplier availability. Check whether enough suppliers exist locally or whether suppliers in another country will ship into it. (older, 2021-22) [L0905.015]
- US suppliers shipping into Canada is a workable option, and Canadian suppliers are the ideal case because they avoid cross-border shipping. (older, 2021-22) [L0905.016]

### Building the Candidate List and Expected Approval Rates

- Build the list of potential suppliers in this order:
  1. Research and compile a master supplier list for all potential suppliers in the niche. [L0115.001]
  2. Complete the approval process with the first five suppliers. [L0115.001]
- Keep the list growing, but limit time spent on research. The goal at this stage is enough suppliers to launch the store. More suppliers can be discovered later, sometimes when brands contact the seller after seeing it sell in their market. (older, 2021-22) [L0932.005]
- Do not spend weeks perfecting the supplier list. At the start, you only need enough suppliers to stock the site so you can launch and begin growing sales. (older, 2021-22) [L0937.008]
- Aim for at least 20 suppliers in the list before moving on to the next stage. Once the store is running, more suppliers usually surface on their own, and some contact you after seeing you sell their products. (older, 2021-22) [L0937.007]
- Approval rates to plan around:

| Reference point | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Typical first-stage approval | About 50 percent at first, improving over time, with at least 20 suppliers contacted | [L0803.026] (older, 2022) |
| Decent approval including follow-ups | 50 percent is considered decent | [L0834.012] (older, 2021) |
| New store calling suppliers | Just over 60 percent of suppliers called | [L0771.028] (older, 2021-22) |
| Warning level | Approval rates well below about 5 percent signal a problem with the outreach method, not just bad luck | [L0834.013] (older, 2021) |

- Approval is a numbers game. Expect many rejections, and document every "reach out again" answer to follow up in three to six months, sometimes a year. (older, 2021) [L0834.012]
- Example from a bike, e-scooter and e-skateboard niche (older, 2021-22): a list of 30 brands was built from Google Shopping listings, and each brand was tracked with its phone, email and website. The owner needed only about 10 approved suppliers for a viable start. With about 60 percent approval expected from calls, that meant roughly 19 approvals from 31 suppliers. [L0771.026]
- A single approval is enough to start, so it is worth calling many suppliers rather than a few. (older, 2021-22) [L0771.028]
- A new store may land about 10 suppliers in its first week when outreach is active. Over about six months, one partner added around 30 more suppliers for the first store. [L0832.029]
- Suppliers unfamiliar with dropshipping can take a year or more to approve a new seller. The first months of a store need enough runway to wait for approvals, so plan the launch and budget around slow approval rather than assuming the first application will succeed. (older, 2018) [L1085.103]
- Approval is an ongoing process, so keep prospecting and following up while the first ads run. (older, 2020) [L0664.010]
- Keep contacting suppliers while the store is built and running traffic. Store building and supplier outreach can run in parallel, so do not wait for a full supplier list before launching. (older, 2021) [L0853.037]
- Do not stop supplier approvals when ads start. Get the first two suppliers live, switch on Google ads to create data and sales, and keep adding suppliers and products. [L0359.031]
- Apply for approval with every brand in the niche, including the lower-tier brands, and do not stop after the first one is approved. More approved brands give more products and more options when some are out of stock. (older, 2020) [L0358.006]
- Get approved with every possible supplier in the niche, then identify standout brands by margin strength and willingness to enforce MAP. (older, 2022) [L0249.017]
- Approvals from several brands, even ones that are not the top sellers, add to store credibility. A store with more brands also makes it easier to approach larger brands in the niche later. (2023) [L0470.034]
- Aim to get approved with every supplier and carry every available product in the niche over time. (older, 2020) [L0852.026]

### Launch Thresholds: How Many Approved Suppliers

Sources differ on the launch number. The values below apply at different stages, so each one is kept with its condition.

| Threshold | Value | When it applies | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Minimum to turn on paid traffic | 2 approved suppliers with products uploaded | Standard launch rule across sources from 2019 to 2025 | [L0274.005, L0966.021, L0941.015, L0836.037, L0855.055, L1002.054] |
| Guideline, not a hard rule | 2 approved suppliers | Upload all their products before ads go live (older, 2022) | [L1092.037] |
| Two to three approved | 2 to 3 approved with products uploaded | Activate Google Ads immediately, without waiting for all suppliers (older, 2019) | [L0243.031] |
| Four approved | 4 suppliers | Upload them and turn on ads; keep adding the rest in the background (2023) | [L0964.020] |
| Five approved (store optimization gate) | 5 suppliers | Before moving to conversion rate work on the store | [L0126.002, L0132.001, L0495.001, L0582.006, L0689.001] |
| Five approved (launch target) | 5 suppliers | Early approval target; more than five if possible | [L0532.009, L0689.002, L1275.072] |
| Five active suppliers to start traffic | 5 suppliers | Older launch guidance; keep looking for more while ads run (older, 2018) | [L1274.030] |
| Launch with one or two | 1 to 2 approved | A store can launch and generate traffic and sales, but sourcing continues (older, 2019) | [L1069.001] |
| One approved for ad setup | 1 supplier | Enough to start setting up ads, not to turn traffic on (older, 2020) | [L0664.010] |
| Do not start traffic with one brand | At least 2 brands live | Before turning traffic on (older, 2021) | [L0671.173] |
| Minimum before ads | Products from first supplier uploaded, then ads start once the second supplier's products are uploaded (2025) | Continuous addition afterward | [L0472.025] |

- Early on, the aim is approval from about five silver suppliers. Gold suppliers come later, after the store has built business history, so do not treat them as the first target. [L0486.020]
- Your first target is to secure approval from five suppliers. After achieving this, continue contacting suppliers on the list to eventually build relationships with silver and gold tier suppliers. [L0126.002]
- Get approved by at least five dropshipping suppliers before moving on to conversion rate work, and aim for more than five if possible. A supplier counts as complete only when its products are uploaded to the store. [L0495.001]
- Before moving on to store optimization, have at least five supplier approvals. Five approved suppliers are enough to start building and optimizing the store. [L0582.006]
- The starting target is five approved suppliers, and the products from all five should be live in the store before moving on. [L0689.002]
- Five approved suppliers are not a finished store. The long-term target is authorization with every brand on the master supplier list, so keep working the list continuously. [L0938.003]
- Five approved suppliers are only the first base. Every additional brand can change the business, so keep working the list. [L0938.003]
- Only about 5 authorizations are needed for the first store launch, but the long-term goal is to be authorized with as close to 100 percent of the master supplier list as possible. [L0938.002]
- Approval rates are low at the start: about five authorized suppliers is enough to launch. The full list is worked through over time, not in one go. [L0191.004]
- Five approved suppliers are enough to start running traffic to a store. Keep looking for more suppliers while the ads are running. (older, 2018) [L1274.030]
- Before launching a store, aim for at least five approved suppliers. If you have fewer, launch only with a clear plan to keep adding suppliers and products, because a store with only two brands has limited product breadth. (older, 2019) [L1275.072]
- Two approved suppliers are the minimum for sending paid traffic. A store launched on only two suppliers still needs more approvals added in the background to become a viable business. (2025) [L0966.021]

### Launch Sequence with Few Suppliers

- Launch a new store with at least two supplier approvals and their products uploaded. Then keep contacting the remaining suppliers every day, so the rest of the list does not slip to later. (older, 2019) [L1002.054]
- Ads can start while further supplier onboarding is still in progress. With two approved suppliers uploaded, running ads while the next suppliers are being added is recommended. (older, 2019) [L1002.055]
- You can launch ads with just two approved suppliers: upload their products, set up Merchant Center and Google Ads, and run a small budget. Keep recruiting more suppliers at the same time instead of waiting for those two to produce results. (older, 2020) [L1088.009]
- Launch Google ads with at least two approved suppliers uploaded, but keep working toward about 20 suppliers on the list at the same time. (2023) [L0855.055]
- Launch the store with the first two approved suppliers and their products uploaded, then turn on ads rather than waiting for all suppliers. (2025) [L0255.009]
- A store with the first two approved suppliers and their products uploaded can be launched in about one week. (2025) [L0255.010]
- Do not turn on paid ads until at least two brands have approved you and their products are live on the store. Research should identify at least 20 possible brands before you start applying. (older, 2021) [L1112.003]
- Start with a small catalog of about 20 products from two to four approved brands before turning on ads with small budgets. Keep applying to the remaining suppliers after the first brands are live. (older, 2021) [L1112.004]
- Two verified suppliers are enough to turn on traffic. Keep sourcing more suppliers every day instead of waiting for sales to come in. (older, 2021) [L0671.172]
- Do not start traffic with only one brand. Get at least two brands live before turning traffic on, and use the free time to get the rest of your suppliers approved. (older, 2021) [L0671.173]
- With two verified suppliers and about 50 products, small test budgets can be run to get the products approved and see traffic, while sourcing continues in the background. (older, 2021) [L0671.174]
- Once the first two suppliers and their products are live, you can start ads, but keep adding more suppliers in the background at the same time. (2024) [L0471.020]
- Two suppliers with products uploaded are enough to start the traffic campaigns and remarketing. About 20 products from two approved suppliers is enough to start. (older, 2022) [L1113.012, L1113.011]
- Launch with at least two approved suppliers, although this is a guideline rather than a hard rule. (older, 2022) [L1092.037]
- A store can launch with as few as two suppliers, with products uploaded, before starting paid traffic. Outreach for more approvals must continue daily in the background. (2024) [L0836.037]
- Two approved suppliers and their products (roughly 20 to 50 items) can go live with ads turned on. (older, 2020) [L0852.026]
- Minimum product count before ads: upload products from the first supplier as soon as it is signed, and start ads once products from the second supplier are uploaded. Products can be added continuously afterward. (2025) [L0472.025]
- Start a new store with two approved brands and immediately activate Google Product Listing Ads. A launch with 20 products from two brands is a reasonable minimum, and the catalog can grow to 200 to 500 products later as more suppliers are approved. (older, 2018) [L1085.035]
- Launch setup once the first two brands are uploaded to Shopify (older, 2018):
  1. Set up the Google Product Listing Ads feed and campaign.
  2. Activate Shopify Payments so the store can accept money.
  3. Set up Facebook dynamic remarketing.
  4. Check that all three are working with a test, then return to supplier approvals and keep uploading the rest of the brands.
  5. Newly uploaded products are picked up automatically by the feed and remarketing, so they can start generating sales as soon as they are live.
  [L1085.036]
- Launch, Shopify-style, with the following sequence (2023): get the first two suppliers approved and uploaded, turn on Google ads, then use traffic and sales data to choose which products to feature and which topics to cover in buyer's guides. [L0855.056]
- Procedure for the first live launch (2025):
  1. Get your first two supplier approvals.
  2. Upload those suppliers' products to your store.
  3. Remove the demo products.
  4. Turn on ads.
  5. Keep following up with other suppliers in the background while traffic runs.
  [L0966.019]
- All demo products must be removed and replaced with real products from authorized suppliers before advancing to Module 6 (ads); a minimum of two suppliers is required for that step (2025). [L0278.026]
- Launch with the first few supplier approvals rather than waiting for all. Once four suppliers are approved, upload them and turn on ads, and keep contacting and uploading the remaining suppliers in the background. (2023) [L0964.020]
- Accept having fewer products at launch, since 120 products is enough to start. Keep adding products in the niche as suppliers approve, and make supplier outreach a daily task. [L0941.016]
- The first store can start with about two suppliers and their products. Go live with ads and keep adding suppliers and products in the background every day. Do not wait to see whether the first two work. (older, 2021) [L0469.029]
- Start with about two suppliers and their products, go live with ads, and keep adding suppliers in the background. (older, 2021) [L0469.029]
- If suppliers refuse the niche at first, do not switch niches right away. Get two suppliers, launch the store with their products, and return to the others later, because rejections are normal. (older, 2021) [L0469.030]
- Once a niche is chosen, two approved suppliers with their catalogs uploaded are enough to launch. Launch means turning on Google Ads and Facebook remarketing to drive traffic, while approvals with more brands continue in the background. (older, 2022) [L1113.011]
- A starting catalog of two suppliers with about five products is thin. Aim for more suppliers and roughly five to eight products before launching ads. (older, 2021) [L0247.032]
- Upload all approved suppliers' products and go live, since the store is already built. (older, 2022) [L0429.037]

### Growing from the Launch Catalog

- Keep applying to more brands and uploading their products as approvals come in, aiming for around 20 brands over time. [L0941.015]
- There is no limit on how many brands you can sell, as long as each brand approves you. Keep applying to new brands while the store is live. (older, 2019) [L1275.023]
- If you have few approved suppliers, keep adding brands and products rather than stopping at launch. Adjacent products in the same niche (for example ottomans or end tables next to leather chairs) can add more brands and products. (older, 2019) [L1275.024]
- Set a goal of adding one or two new suppliers per week, even by circling back to earlier applicants. (older, 2022) [L0429.037]
- Products from five different suppliers give a usable sample of items and brands to test and build on. This is more useful than relying on a single supplier's catalog when choosing what to sell. [L0689.003]
- Build the first product base in this order:
  1. Get approved by five dropshipping suppliers.
  2. Upload the products from those five suppliers to the online store.
  3. Once the approvals are in and the products are live on the store, mark this step as complete before starting conversion work on the store.
  [L0689.001]
- Verify that all products from all five suppliers are live and visible on the store before moving on. [L0132.003]
- Upload products from each of the five approved suppliers to the store. [L0132.003]
- Continue supplier outreach and approval requests until five suppliers confirm they will work with the store. [L0132.003]
- Your store should have products from at least five different suppliers live before focusing on conversion optimization. [L0132.001]
- Once the store is running, more suppliers usually surface on their own. [L0937.007]
- Keep the product catalog on the store as approvals come in. Approval of a supplier alone is not enough to make progress, and only approved suppliers with uploaded products add to the count needed before moving on. [L0495.005]
- Add the rest of the approved suppliers' products once the store is live. Upload all their products before ads go live. (older, 2022) [L1092.037]
- Heavy or freight-shipped products priced around $1,500 to $3,000 can be a sound niche if they pass all niche criteria. Such orders can bring roughly $500 to $1,000 profit each. (2020) [L0664.018]

### When Too Few Suppliers Exist

- If you find fewer than 20 brands in your niche, validate viability before building (older, 2020):
  1. Call companies directly.
  2. Do not mention wanting to sell their products.
  3. Ask neutrally whether they work with online distributors.
  4. Use their answers to decide whether online distribution is feasible in this niche.
  [L0245.072]
- To check a market with no visible dropshipping, call the suppliers directly and have a proper conversation about dropshipping instead of relying on competitor stores as evidence. Approval from suppliers is the test that counts. (older, 2021-22) [L0905.011]
- Some markets have little visible dropshipping because suppliers were not open to it in the past, while many suppliers are now more open. Such markets can be underserved and are worth testing. (older, 2021-22) [L0905.010]
- If a niche has only one drop-ship-friendly retailer, check that it sells at least 20 brands before continuing. Then email from a personal address asking whether it has a warehouse or showroom, and proceed if it is online-only. (2023) [L0934.028]
- Count how many stores advertise the most popular products of a niche on Google Shopping. The competition threshold in that test is 10 stores. (2024) [L0971.002]
- Research the niche competition: at least 20 suppliers, then count stores advertising the top products on Google Shopping against the threshold of 10. [L0971.001, L0971.002]
- Expect approval rates to be low for a new store. In markets where most suppliers keep a warehouse, contact 50 to 60 suppliers instead of 20. [L0969.014]
- If you cannot reach about 10 suppliers after following up for roughly two weeks, move up one niche level to increase the number of products you can offer at launch. (2025) [L0966.024]
- Gold-tier suppliers that run out of stock: one option is to contact more suppliers in the niche before changing niches. (older, 2022) [L0969.098]
- Fewer suppliers are acceptable only as a temporary stage in a smaller country, not as the target. (2023) [L0935.001]
- Some stores abroad are built with well under 20 suppliers, but this is an exception. [L0935.004]
- Estimated suppliers in a niche worksheet is the count of brands across the future competitors you found, not the number of competitor sites. Estimates are rough; if a brand list is long, a rounded figure such as 100 is an acceptable estimate. [L0459.014]
- The count of brands in a Google Shopping check across the product types in the niche must reach at least 10 in total. Procedure (older, 2021-22):
  1. Set the search to the target country, for example the US market.
  2. Read the seller and brand names in the Google Shopping listings and scroll through the next pages.
  3. Count the potential brands or suppliers; the lesson requires at least 10 in total across the product types in the niche.
  4. Open a retailer that sells the product category and note the brands it carries.
  5. Mark the local-brand criterion as yes if the count is met.
  [L0458.019]

### Broadening the Niche

- If too few suppliers approve you, broaden the niche to find more brands and products with better margins. This is a fallback, not a day-one move. (2023) [L0970.051]
- If a niche cannot reach 20 suppliers, niche up one level and add related products to the range. [L0359.021]
- When niching up because you found fewer than 20 suppliers, identify an additional 20 to 40 suppliers in related categories to expand the product range. (older, 2022) [L0249.016]
- When a store has only a few approved suppliers with low margins or no MAP, grow the catalog with the steps in the "Growing from the Launch Catalog" section. [L0429.037]
- In markets where most suppliers keep a warehouse and too few suppliers exist, consider a broader niche to get enough products. (older, 2022) [L0969.014]
- Before choosing a niche, make sure the number of brands is enough; if it is short, broaden rather than launch thin. [L0356.030]
- Before moving to the niche, confirm the supplier count first. Supplier availability is checked before product count, so the niche can still be widened if the count is short. (older, 2019) [L0356.030]
- Expect that some niches will have too few suppliers for the target count. When that happens, the broader niche is the way to reach enough products. [L0969.014]
- Supplier-count thresholds below 20 are a temporary stage, so widen the niche to restore the standard. [L0935.001]
- If a niche cannot reach ~10 suppliers after two weeks of follow-up, move up one niche level (2025). [L0966.024]
- Brands in a niche should be checked for drop-ship viability before broadening. A niche dominated by household names is not viable, so broadening should not lead there. (older, 2021-22) [L0994.014]

### Niche Viability Checks

- The niche must be drop-ship friendly, meaning suppliers can ship orders directly to the end customer without the store holding stock. [L1080.002]
- Brands that sell through online-only stores are the ones that drop ship; brands that sell through distributors with warehouses usually do not. (2023) [L1094.010]
- Shipping is often the largest variable cost for heavy items, so shipping rates must be checked before choosing a niche. If shipping is charged out of a thin margin, such as a 35 percent margin on a large item, the product is generally not viable because no budget remains for ads. (older, 2018) [L1109.006]
- The best suppliers and brands are known to enthusiasts within the market but unknown to the general public. Markets dominated by household name brands are not viable for drop shipping. (older, 2021-22) [L0994.014]
- Televisions are not a viable drop shipping product, because the market is dominated by massive international brands such as Sony, Panasonic and LG. Those large companies do not need drop shippers to grow and rarely work with them. (older, 2021-22) [L0994.015]
- Seasonal niches: for a seasonal niche (for example in Texas with a short winter), start with suppliers for year-round products that sell in all four seasons. Add seasonal products later, as the relevant season approaches. (2025) [L0472.026]
- Most industries see sales spikes around Black Friday, Cyber Monday and Christmas, so a holiday spike alone is not a reason to reject a niche. Plan around it by confirming with suppliers that stock will last through the peak. (older, 2019) [L0357.069]
- Heavy or freight-shipped products priced around $1,500 to $3,000 can be a sound niche if they pass all niche criteria. (2020) [L0664.018]
- Before testing a niche's competition, source at least 20 suppliers. [L0971.001]
- The worksheet's Estimated Suppliers number counts brands, not competitor sites. [L0459.014]

### Single-Supplier Dependency Risk

- Do not depend on one supplier. A single supplier can stop selling to you and remove your whole catalog. Upload the first supplier's products and start ads, but keep contacting new suppliers continuously. (older, 2019) [L1086.030]
- Do not start traffic with only one brand. Get at least two brands live before turning traffic on, and use the free time to get the rest of the suppliers approved. (older, 2021) [L0671.173]
- Working with few suppliers raises business risk sharply. If two of three suppliers close or one raises prices, the store stays open but becomes far less profitable. A large supplier base protects margins and continuity against losing individual partners. (2023) [L0935.005]
- Multiple supply sources reduce risk. A supplier can close, have an owner stroke, or move on to another project, so you need several brands and several suppliers in each niche. (older, 2021) [L0801.035]
- Supplier depth is one of the two primary levers for growing revenue, so secure as many suppliers as possible within the niche. [L0512.003]
- Put paid ad budget only on products from suppliers that have real margins, and send MAP suppliers' products into paid ads while selling the rest through free traffic only. (older, 2021-22) [L0671.004, L0429.037]
- Where a supplier enforces MAP, route its products to paid ads. Use free Google product listings for the rest. (older, 2022) [L0429.037]
- Set up Google Merchant Center and free Google product listings for the products already approved. (older, 2022) [L0429.037]
- Build a store with a large supplier base from the start; the larger the base, the less any single partner's exit can damage revenue. [L0935.005]
- Keep the master supplier list continuously worked, since the long-term goal is authorization with as close to 100 percent of it as possible. [L0938.002]
- Supplier approval is a numbers game. Expect many rejections, and do not treat any single supplier as the business. [L0834.012]
- Expect the 80/20 pattern: about 80 percent of sales come from about 20 percent of suppliers, so keep approaching many suppliers and following up with them over time. (older, 2022) [L1113.017]
- Some operators have built stores with few suppliers, but this carries more risk and should be an exception. [L0935.004]

#### Further details

- 1. Get approved by at least two suppliers. 2. Upload their products to the store. 3. Enable the payment gateway. 4. Set up Google Ads. 5. Keep submitting approvals and uploading products in the background. [L1111.043]

## Finding Suppliers: Competitor Research and Brand Lists

### Set up the working sheets

Work inside the Niche Research Worksheet, in the tab "Niche Master Supplier List." [L0056.002]

To create the niche's own list, follow these steps:

1. Right-click the "Niche Master Supplier List" tab.
2. Select Duplicate.
3. Rename the tab with your niche name, for example "Outdoor Kitchens, Master Supplier List."
4. Position the new tab next to your Future Competitors sheet. [L0056.003]

A second build procedure gives the same sequence in more detail:

1. Open the niche's master supplier list template, a sheet named "niche master supplier list."
2. Right-click it, choose Duplicate, then right-click the copy and choose Rename.
3. Name the copy after the niche, for example "Chandelier Master Supplier List."
4. Drag each copy next to the matching future competitor list so the sheets stay in order.
5. Work only on column A (supplier names) in this step.
6. Open the first future competitor website in a split-screen view next to the spreadsheet.
7. Find the brand list: search the niche category, then open the filters and select Brand. On some sites the brand list is in the footer under a "shop all brands" link.
8. Before copying a brand, open its brand page and confirm it sells products in your niche.
9. Type each qualifying brand name into column A, skipping brands already on the list.
10. Repeat for every future competitor website, then repeat the full process for every other niche that is still a winner. [L0930.001]

Both build procedures are kept in full. The fuller one is in the section on collecting brands below. [L0930.001, L0931.001]

Which columns to fill depends on the stage:

- **Name collection stage:** Only column A (supplier names) is filled. URL, phone, email, date of contact, contact person and notes stay empty until the supplier-contact stage. [L0930.002]
- **Full build:** Columns A to D are completed in one pass: Supplier Name, Supplier Website, Supplier Phone Number, and Supplier Email or Contact Form. Columns E, F and G are covered in later modules. [L0056.004, L0931.001]
- **Other template:** A Master Supplier List spreadsheet can use four required columns: Supplier Name, Supplier URL, Supplier #, and Supplier Email. [L0116.001]

A Future Competitors sheet is created in the Niche Research Worksheet. It has these columns: [L0062.001]

- Supplier Name
- Supplier URL
- Supplier Phone Number
- Supplier Email
- Date of Contact
- Contact Person
- Notes

To fill it:

1. Open your Niche Research Worksheet.
2. Navigate to the [Niche] Future Competitors sheet.
3. Examine all products your future competitors sell.
4. Identify which suppliers provide each product.
5. Enter each supplier with all available information in the tracking columns.
6. Ensure you record every supplier you discover from your competitors. [L0062.002]

A Source B tracker (2021-22, older advice) uses different columns. Its name-stage fields are Supplier Name, Product Type, Email, Phone Number, Website and Online Application. Phone Call + Date?, Call Comments and Paused come later, in the outreach phase. [L0932.001]

Before outreach begins, keep the list ready. For each supplier, gather the website URL, phone number and email address, and put all of it in one document or spreadsheet. Aim for 20 or more potential suppliers in the niche. [L0278.043]

### Collect brands from future competitor websites

Before you start, keep in mind that the approach of this section is to take brands from competitors, not from supplier directories. Build from online-only competitors and from competitors known to dropship. [L0930.005, L0459.032]

Use this procedure for a single competitor:

1. Open the category page for the niche, for example CNC machines or outdoor kitchens.
2. Find the brand list in the sidebar filter.
3. Click show more to expand the full list.
4. Count every brand. This total is the estimated supplier number that competitor contributes. [L0459.013]

Use the building procedure to add brands to the list:

1. Duplicate the master supplier list template, rename the copy "<niche> master supplier list," and place it next to the niche's future competitor sheet.
2. Open the research sheet on one side of the screen and the competitor's website on the other (split screen).
3. On the competitor's site, open the exact niche category, not a parent or neighbouring category.
4. Scroll to the brand section, click "show more," and copy every brand name into column A.
5. Repeat for each future competitor, adding new brands to the bottom of column A.
6. Sort column A from A to Z and delete repeated brand rows.
7. If fewer than 20 brands remain, go back, find more future competitors, and extract their brands.
8. Google each brand name; add a niche keyword (for example "kitchen") if the results are unclear.
9. If the top result is the competitor itself, the brand is a private label: delete its row.
10. Copy the supplier website URL, remove any trailing code after it, and paste it into column B.
11. Find the phone number, reformat it to hyphenated US style, and paste it into column C.
12. Find the email address on the supplier's contact page and paste it into column D. If there is none, paste the contact form link instead.
13. If a supplier's site does not load, leave its fields blank and continue with the next brand.
14. Write any add-on or accessory products in the notes column.
15. Validate the niche: at least one competitor needs a traffic rank under 100,000, and at least 20 brands need contact information. [L0931.001]

Brand lookup on competitor sites:

- **Where to look:** category page, then Filters (or More filters), then Brand. [L0930.006]
- **Where there is no brand filter:** check the footer for a "Shop all brands" or brand page link and open that list instead. [L0930.006, L0930.001]
- **Brand section:** the brands sit in the 'brand' section of the category page, which appears after clicking show more. The competitor's category item count (for example 407 items) shows how big the section is. Zooming out in the browser makes the list easier to read while copying. [L0931.005]
- **Where to look for headings:** brand lists may sit under headings such as "Brands," "Brands we stock" or "Our Supplier Brands," or in the main navigation, footer or a small finder widget. If none exists, brand names can still appear in the sidebar or in product category links, so keep scanning before deciding a site has no brand list. [L1484.013]
- **Menu first:** if the site has a Brands item in the main menu, use it. If not, open the main product category page and scroll down for a "best brands" section, a brand list in the sidebar, or a brand filter. [L0937.005, L0932.001]
- **Other categories:** browse the other product categories the site sells (for example dehumidifiers next to air purifiers), add brands not yet listed, and record each supplier's primary product type. A brand covering two product types can stay in one row with both types listed. [L0937.005, L0932.001]
- **Site search:** use a competitor's own site search with the niche keyword, such as CNC or outdoor kitchens, to see the full range. Brands that the category menus do not show can be found this way and added to the supplier count. [L0459.029]
- **Best sellers first:** use the brands that top the Best Sellers or Recommended sorting on future competitor category pages first, because stores that know what they are doing place their best sellers at the top. [L1113.016]
- **Default sort:** to find the most popular brands on a large marketplace, open the category and read the brand names in the default sort, which is bestsellers first. [L0803.003]

Brand selection rules:

- Add a supplier only if it actually makes products in your target niche. A brand that sells mainly furniture or decor should be skipped even when it appears on a competitor's brand filter, because a competitor selling a mixed catalogue does not mean the brand makes the niche product. [L0930.003]
- Use the exact niche category on a competitor's site, because competitors often sell in several niches, and brands from other niches would be wrong suppliers. Clicking back to the parent category by mistake leads to products outside the target niche. Always confirm the category name before copying brands. [L0931.004]
- Before copying a brand, open its brand page and confirm it sells products in your niche. [L0930.001]
- Each brand a reseller lists is usually a manufacturer or company that makes its own product, so every brand you count is a possible direct supplier to contact later. The brand still counts even if the reseller does not carry its full range. [L1484.014]
- Competitor brand filters are a practical way to discover suppliers, because competitors have already vetted which brands they sell. Verifying the category on the brand's own page before adding it keeps the list relevant. [L0930.010]
- Retailers and e-commerce sites that sell several brands are the source of supplier names, not suppliers themselves. A retailer is a competitor and is not added as a supplier. An own-brand site that sells only its own products can be added to the list but is not the primary target. [L0932.002, L0937.004]
- Do not enter a retailer's own website as a supplier, and do not confuse the retailer's product list with a brand list. A combined listing, such as two brand names in one entry, should be split by checking each brand separately. [L0932.010]
- Add each brand name as its own row, noting its primary product type. Record names only at this stage. [L0932.001, L0937.005]
- Older example (Source B, 2021-22): on an allergy-focused retailer's air purifier category, the category page had no brand menu. Brands were found by scrolling below the collection links to a "best brands" section and a brand list in the sidebar filter. That single site yielded about 16 to 17 manufacturer brands, which were added one per row: Airfree, Aerus, Airmega, Airpura, Alen, Austin Air, Blueair, Coway, Germ Guardian, LiteAir, PowerPure, QuietPure, Winix, and others. Checking the same retailer's dehumidifier category added further brands that were not on the air purifier list, so both categories were recorded. [L0937.006, L0932.011]

Use the following procedure for a retailer-based build (older advice, Source B, 2021-22):

1. Build a tracking spreadsheet (Google Sheets: make your own copy of the template; Excel version also available) with columns Supplier Name, Product Type, Email, Phone Number, Website, Online Application? and, later, Phone Call + Date?, Call Comments, Paused. For this phase only fill the first six: Supplier Name, Product Type, Email, Phone Number, Website, Online Application.
2. Search Google for your main product type (for example "air purifiers") and open retailer or e-commerce sites that sell many brands in your market, not only their own label.
3. On each retailer site, look for a brands filter or brand list in the main menu or sidebar. If none exists, open the category pages and scroll for a "best brands" section or a brand list in the sidebar navigation. Record each brand name as its own row in the Supplier Name column, noting its primary product type.
4. Check several retailer sites (at least 4 to 6) and across other product categories in the niche (for example humidifiers, dehumidifiers), since the same brand often appears on many sites and new brands appear in other categories.
5. Aim for at least 20 suppliers. Do not spend weeks on this step.
6. For each brand, search its name on Google (do not guess the URL) and open its official website.
7. Record the brand website URL, a public email address if available, and the phone number (often in the footer).
8. Look for a "contact us" page and footer links. If no email is public, record "Website Contact Form" in the Email field to show no email is known yet.
9. Look for dealer or retailer links such as "Become a dealer," "Become an [brand] dealer," or "Become a retailer" (often in the header or footer). If found, record the online application page URL in the Online Application column and follow that page's instructed contact method.
10. Do not submit applications yet. Only record the page. Contacting suppliers and seeking approval come in a later step, after the website is built. [L0932.001]

Older example (2021-22): a second site can add brands missing from the first. On an Australian 4x4 accessories reseller's brands page, the visible names included Eezi-Awn, Direction Plus, Thunder Auto, Harrop, GME, CTEK, Hema, Roadsafe, Tough Dog, Warn and Ultra-Vision, so that single site already showed at least 20 brands. Brand names such as Clearview, Boss and Bushranger also appeared in another reseller's product category links. [L1484.015]

Stop at one site only when you have a reason to. Do not stop at one competitor site when checking supplier counts, because a single site may show a list that is incomplete or only partly relevant. [L1484.011]

Start the brand count with the competitor e-commerce sites you already checked for average price and shipping, instead of starting a new search from scratch. Reuse those sites first, then add further stores found through Google results that sell products matching your market idea. [L1484.012]

Keep the online-only, multi-brand competitor websites open, because they will be reused later to find more brands and to track future competitors. Do not close a competitor site until the niche has been fully evaluated. [L0459.015]

### Clean and de-duplicate the list

Sort the list and remove repeats as follows:

1. Right-click Column A.
2. Sort A to Z.
3. Identify duplicate brand entries.
4. Delete duplicate rows.
5. Keep one entry per unique brand. [L0056.007]

Other rules for duplicates:

- Duplicate brand names across competitor websites are expected and normal. Do not delete duplicates until you have finished collecting data from all competitor websites. [L0056.006]
- Brands sold by several competitors appear more than once, so duplicates are expected and must be removed before counting. Sorting column A alphabetically makes repeated names easy to spot. Counting only unique brands keeps the number of real suppliers accurate. [L0931.007]
- Do not add a supplier twice. Brands that appear on several competitor websites should be entered once, so before adding a name, check whether it is already in the list. Overlap between competitors is normal. In one example, three or four brands from the first competitor also appeared on the second. [L0930.004]
- Cross-check across four to six competitor sites. Many brands appear on several sites, so add each brand only once. [L0937.005]

### Verify each brand before it stays on the list

- **Private label check:** if a brand's top Google result is the competitor's own site, it is that competitor's private label and cannot be sourced directly, so its row is deleted. Marketplaces such as Wayfair private-label their own brands while still dropshipping them. Private label brands are excluded from the supplier list. [L0931.008]
- **Rare case:** if a brand's own website cannot be found, the product may be a private label owned by a retailer such as Wayfair. This is rare and should not be the default assumption. [L0967.021]
- **Verify the company:** when you search for a supplier's website, make sure you reach the brand's own company site, not a retailer page that only contains the brand name in its URL. A master brand may sell under several sub-brands in the same niche, so verify the company before outreach and avoid contacting a competitor that already sells for that brand. [L0834.117]
- **Parent companies:** a brand that appears only on one large retailer may be a white-label product made for that retailer. If the same brand shows up on other sites, it likely belongs to a parent company with sister or daughter brands, and approval with the parent can open those brands too. [L0803.001]
- **Search each brand:** search each brand name on Google and check its website to qualify it before you reach out. [L0942.029]
- **Judging brand quality (older advice, 2022):** when no quality signal is given, use this procedure:
  1. List the brands on the first page of competitor listings.
  2. If price is used as a quality proxy, sort by price.
  3. Check that at least about 20 brands exist at the chosen price level.
  4. Read the reviews for each brand.
  5. Search each brand name together with problem-related words to see whether a recurring complaint shows up. [L0969.110]
- **Brand with an unlabelled product:** to identify the real brand behind a product with no brand name, use this procedure (2022, older):
  1. Search the product name and any SKU or identifier on Google.
  2. In Google Chrome, right-click the product image and choose Search image with Google Lens.
  3. Check the other sites that list the product for a brand name or identifier. [L0803.002]
- **Nothing published stays current:** no published list of drop-ship suppliers stays accurate for long, because brands and companies appear and close constantly. The most current supplier list for a niche comes from researching that niche yourself with the same method each time. [L0801.180]
- **Directories do not replace research:** supplier directories and lists are not a substitute for researching brands yourself. Direct research uncovers more current and higher-quality suppliers than any directory. [L1279.035]

### Identify the brand behind a competitor's product

Reverse image search is a common way to find a supplier behind a competitor's product. Use the following procedures.

Procedure 1 (2020, older):

1. Use Google Chrome.
2. Right-click the product image on the competitor site.
3. Select Search Google for image.
4. Review the sites that show the same image. If only the competitor appears, the product may be exclusive to them.
5. Open other retailers that list the same product and check whether they show the brand name. [L0967.020]

Procedure 2 (2020, older), for finding the product name and brand:

1. Find product name and description from the competitor site.
2. Copy the product name into Google search to find which other retailers stock it.
3. Check which retailers display the brand name on the product page.
4. Right-click on the competitor product image, select "Search Google for image," and check visually similar results for brand names and other retailers selling the same product. [L0268.015]

Setting (2021, older): in Google Chrome, right-click the product image on a competitor's product page and select Search Google for image. This returns every web page where the image appears, which can reveal the supplier's website. [L0801.033]

Image search on AliExpress: use the AliExpress image search to find the exact product, or the closest match seen on a competitor's store, and check whether it is listed there. This is a verification step, not a sourcing recommendation. For the status of AliExpress as a supplier source, see the channel section below. [L0549.017]

Products from Wayfair: many Wayfair products are dropshipped, so suppliers can be sourced from Wayfair product listings by identifying the brand behind them. [L0967.022]

### Channel fit: online-only versus brick-and-mortar

Core principle: suppliers that already sell to online-only retailers are more likely to approve a store without a physical location. Source from online-only competitors first. [L0664.009, L0854.001, L0969.013, L0808.013]

- **Online-only competitors:** a competitor that sells online only, with no warehouse, store or showroom, is a source of suppliers that already work with online-only retailers. [L0854.001]
- **Future competitors:** an online-only store that sells in your niche is a future competitor, and the brands it sells are suppliers you may want. A manufacturer that sells many other brands online is probably focused on e-commerce, so its products are usually not a fit to list. [L0470.084]
- **Brick-and-mortar niches:** when a niche has brick-and-mortar competitors, build the supplier list from brands sold by online-only competitors. Those are your true future competitors. [L0803.013]
- **Preferred route:** finding suppliers through the websites of online-only future competitors is preferred, because those competitors already work with online-only retailers. Brands that only work with brick-and-mortar stores are not worth pursuing when the goal is an online-only store. [L0666.021]
- **Grandfathering:** suppliers that have online-only competitor stores, or brick-and-mortar stores with long-held brands, may still be worth contacting, as they can be grandfathered into brands. Do not build the initial list of 20 brands from them. [L0666.032]
- **Online-only sellers:** suppliers that do not require a physical storefront are the best supplier source. Find online-only competitors and reach out to the brands they carry, rather than sources that own physical stores. [L0969.013]
- **Find through online-only stores:** a supplier that also sells in physical stores is acceptable if you first found it through an online-only store. [L0808.013]
- **Online supplier that also sells in stores:** still reach out, get the price list and consider approval. Do not remove them from the master list automatically. [L0665.020]
- **Showroom competitors:** competitors that take a showroom approach can still be checked for new brands. Get approved and look at their price lists before deciding if they belong in the store. [L1281.121]
- **Physical retailers add markup:** buying from physical retailers adds their markup, which leaves small margins. Find online-only retailers in your country that sell the brands, then contact those brands directly, since their distribution already exists. [L0471.057]
- **Brands with a dominant chain channel:** to source from a brand dominated by large chains:
  1. Find online-only stores that sell new products in the niche.
  2. Note which brands they carry.
  3. Contact those brands directly to become an authorized retailer, not through a distributor or middleman. [L0857.032]
- **Partner pages:** a brand's partner or "our partners" page can name the online retailers that already stock it. This helps you identify the brand's distribution channels. [L0940.028]
- **Big box stores:** brands sold at big box stores such as Home Depot or Lowe's should still be added and contacted. A brand may list only a small share of its range there (for example 10 of 300 products), leaving the rest open for your channel. [L0940.027]
- **Big brands:** big brand suppliers generally do not drop ship, so do not contact them. [L1088.008]
- **Older conflict on big brands (2020 against 2024):** L1088.008 (2020) says big brand suppliers generally do not drop ship, while L0940.027 says big box brands should still be added and contacted, since they can open a share of the range. Both apply at different levels: the first describes the brand as a whole, the second the part of its range that is not in the big box channel. [L1088.008, L0940.027]
- **Mixed cases:** sources that sell both online and in stores are not automatically excluded, as described in the rules above. [L0808.013, L0665.020]

Older (2022): suppliers whose own distribution is online-only are the highest-yield starting point. Also, from 2020: suppliers that already sell to online-only stores are much more likely to approve you than suppliers that only work with brick-and-mortar retailers. [L0664.009]

Older (2019): suppliers that sell only online already work with online retailers. If a supplier refuses new stores or asks for a brick-and-mortar location, set it aside, first get approved by others, and return to it once you have traction. [L0663.048]

### Wayfair, Overstock and other large online retailers

Large online retailers such as Wayfair and Overstock are not treated as competitors. Their catalogs can be used to find brands to contact. Searching for those brands on Google usually reveals other online stores selling them. [L0468.188]

The share of dropship items on these platforms is reported differently by source. The figures are:

| Source | Year | Value | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Wayfair drop-ships | 2021 (older) | more than 95% of what it sells | suppliers found there are strong candidates [L0469.112] |
| Large drop-shipping retailers such as Wayfair | 2024 | about 90 percent sourced directly from brands | a useful source of supplier leads in niches they already sell in; their pop-up stores hold only a small part of their range [L0806.104] |

Use the 2021 figure for Wayfair specifically and the 2024 figure for large drop-ship retailers in general. Both show that Wayfair's brand list is a strong source of leads. [L0469.112, L0806.104]

Build the supplier list from the following:

- Wayfair suppliers.
- Two to three other popular online retailers that have no physical stores or warehouses and publicly list the brands they sell for. [L0469.112]

Wayfair sellers are almost all dropshippers, so treat them as dropshipping competitors. Source supplier brands from Wayfair listings where prices are equal to or lower than Amazon, and do not source suppliers from Amazon (2020, older). [L0551.016]

Amazon and eBay are the wrong places to find dropshipping suppliers. Look for brands and approved suppliers who sell directly (2018, older). [L0466.041]

Walmart: large US retailers such as Walmart also use drop shipping and allow third-party sellers to list products on their platform. The fact that a competitor drop ships does not decide whether it counts as a direct competitor. What matters is that it resells other brands in your niche. [L0896.026]

### Google Shopping and search results

Google Shopping (2021-22, older advice), local procedure:

1. Build a list of brands and suppliers that sell the products in the chosen market, using Google Shopping listings and searches such as "electric bike manufacturers," "electric bike suppliers" or "electric bike distributors."
2. For each brand, find the website, the phone number, the email address and any online dealer or retailer application form.
3. Record each supplier in a tracker with the product type and contact details.
4. Call each supplier and introduce your business, then ask whether you can sell their products through your website.
5. If the call does not produce a clear result, send an email or submit the online dealer application, and record the date.
6. Log each call with the date, the person spoken to and the outcome.
7. After approval, the supplier shares product data, pricing and images, which you upload to your website so the products go live. [L0771.024]

Contacting suppliers from the most visible Google Shopping results is a valid starting point (2022, older). Starting with the least popular suppliers can reduce fear of rejection and make early outreach easier. [L0803.004]

### Trade shows and exhibitor lists

Trade shows let you see many products in person in a short time and connect with brands face to face. They can also help get approved by brands that had put you on a waiting list (2019, older). [L0467.034]

Trade-show sources:

- **TSNN.com (Trade Show News Network):** lists trade shows by industry keyword. It is a worldwide trade-show directory and lets you find upcoming shows by country and industry keyword. [L0804.019, L0935.007]
- **Trade Show Network:** lists trade shows for almost any industry. Open each show's website, go to its exhibitors list, and extract the brand names, and where available the URL, contact name, phone and email, for a supplier list (2019, older). [L1068.038]
- **Search options:** you can search TSNN.com by industry keyword or by geographic location to find in-person events for your industry or a group of related industries. [L0942.004]
