- **Exhibitors:** exhibitors are the companies that pay to show their products at trade shows, and they are often looking for new retailers. Their exhibitor lists are therefore a direct source of supplier leads. [L0935.009]
- **Directory of shows (2019, older):** a trade show directory lists trade shows across industries. Each show's exhibitor list gives companies you can contact as potential suppliers. [L0663.049]
- **Source link:** the source for the directory address reads tsn.com in one unit and tsnn.com in others. The name used most often in the course is TSNN.com. [L0666.018, L0804.019]

Setup procedure, for finding suppliers through trade shows:

1. Open tsnn.com and use the search box.
2. Select the country where you plan to do business.
3. Enter a basic industry keyword, such as furniture.
4. Review the list of upcoming trade shows in that country.
5. Check whether each show is actually taking place, since events can be cancelled or postponed.
6. Open the website of a relevant show and find its exhibitor list.
7. Contact the exhibitors directly to build your list of at least 20 suppliers in the niche. [L0935.008]

Sidebar procedure (2021, older), when only a few suppliers approve:

1. Open the trade show news network website (tsnn.com).
2. Use the sidebar to enter your niche and find trade shows.
3. Open the exhibitor lists, which show companies exhibiting their products.
4. Contact those companies as potential suppliers. [L0834.014]

Trade show attendance in person (procedure at the event):

1. Search TSNN.com by industry keyword or location to find upcoming shows.
2. Attend the show, which is typically free or cheap, and walk the hall, where there are often hundreds of companies you could sell for.
3. Bring business cards, introduce yourself to exhibitors, collect their cards, and tell them you will email them.
4. A few days later, follow up by email and reference the expo where you met. [L0942.005]

Meeting a supplier in person makes approval easier than cold emails alone, because the contact is a face and a name and the supplier sees you as more serious. Personal contact also builds trust faster than a mass email. [L0942.006]

Trade shows typically take place several times a year, often coast to coast in the USA, so a new supplier search can be timed to upcoming events. [L0942.007]

Trade shows take place every year and new brands appear regularly. Repeat exhibitor list research regularly, even for established stores (2019, older). [L1068.039]

Procedure without an upcoming trade show:

1. Open the trade show's own website and go to its Exhibitors section, which lists every company attending.
2. Copy the exhibitor names.
3. Search each name on Google and open their websites.
4. Contact them directly, the same way you do normal supplier research. [L0942.008]

Filtering: trade show exhibitor lists can be filtered for companies that offer drop shipping and used as an extra source of supplier leads when your normal supplier search runs short (2021, older). [L0962.045]

Backup source: TSNN.com lists trade shows by niche and country. Open the exhibitors list of relevant shows in your country of business to find additional brands to contact, and use it as a backup source when suppliers are out of stock or not onboarding (2021, older). [L1279.021]

Stock shortages: when suppliers in a niche stop taking new retailers because of stock shortages, the TSNN.com trade show network can reveal suppliers that are still approving retailers and have inventory. Start with those approvals and add the shortage-hit suppliers later, once they restock (2021, older). [L1112.078]

Slow supplier: when a popular supplier has delayed inventory or long lead times, look for similar alternatives that can ship fast. Trade show exhibitor lists from TSNN.com are a source for new brands (2022, older). [L0803.028]

Doubling the product count: TSNN.com is a source for finding suppliers. Also search for your niche with "trade show" and look at exhibitors, then contact those brands. The aim is to double the product count so more products can ship quickly (2022, older). [L0854.019]

Trade shows as a later stage: advanced supplier strategies can require attending trade shows. Treat them as a later-stage option, not a starting step. [L0544.006]

Trade shows as optional: attending trade shows in person is optional. Trade-show directories and exhibitor lists should be used as a supplementary way to find suppliers alongside other sourcing methods. [L0935.010]

Note on the trade show sequence: the course gives three positions on trade shows. L0544.006 treats them as a later-stage option, L0935.010 as supplementary, and L0942.005 describes attending as typically free or cheap. Use the later-stage position when the list already has enough suppliers, and use exhibitor lists earlier if few suppliers approve or stock is short. [L0544.006, L0935.010, L0942.005, L0663.048, L1112.078]

Older (2019): if you are very limited, look up trade shows in your niche in a trade show directory and contact exhibitors from the exhibitor lists. Return to the suppliers who declined once you have traction. [L0663.048]

Older (2022): exhibitor lists keep giving new brands. Repeat the search with a new trade show keyword in another year. [L1068.039]

### Competitor directories, Thomasnet and other external sources

- **Thomasnet:** lists many brands useful for finding extra suppliers. Its listings do not show whether a brand works with dropshipping, so each brand must be contacted to confirm (2022, older). [L1092.035]
- **Supplier directory blogs:** these list suppliers by country and niche. Use them to build a shortlist of non-China suppliers before testing each one (Source C). [L1214.010]
- **Online supplier research tools:** these can show which suppliers larger brands in a niche use, mainly in the US. This is a faster route than a blind search for reverse sourcing (Source E, 2025-26). [L1225.077]
- **AI-generated lists:** supplier lists generated by AI prompts are acceptable only after you check that each brand is drop-ship friendly. Use them to expand a list over time after the main sourcing methods have been used up, not as the starting list (2024). [L0965.024]
- **Middlemen platforms:** build supplier lists mainly from websites that already drop ship, and avoid middlemen platforms such as Doba. Middlemen mark up products and leave almost no profit margin (2024). [L0965.023]
- **Alibaba and AliExpress:** these are not recommended as supplier sources for these stores (2022, older). For more brands, search trade show exhibitor lists on TSNN.com for your niche in the country where you do business, then contact the exhibitors directly. [L0429.039]
- **Community forum (2019, older):** to find suppliers in your own country, ask experienced sellers in a community forum for your region which suppliers they have approved and how they got them. [L0663.011]
- **Crowdfunding (2022, older):** search Kickstarter and Indiegogo for projects from five years ago that became legitimate brands. Many crowdfunded projects that succeeded are now offering wholesale to retailers. Never pre-sell products that are currently in crowdfunding development. [L0249.090]
- **Competitor brand pages:** competitor brand pages on sites in your niche show which suppliers they sell, which is an alternative source of supplier leads to trade show exhibitor lists. [L0942.029]
- **Suppliers from many sources:** suppliers can be sourced from many different types of lists and products. Before accepting a supplier, check the margin and shipping cost, because both determine profitability (2020, older). [L0967.042]

### Local, international and special-case suppliers

Local suppliers (Source B, 2021-22, older) are approved using the Google Shopping procedure above. For a local market, use the same steps to find and approve local suppliers. [L0771.024]

International suppliers found through competitor stores often have stock or fulfilment in the target country. Calling them via Google Voice or Skype, or emailing them, is fine (2021, older). [L1112.048]

Free bonus product (2021, older): first decide which product to give away, then source suppliers for it the same way as for the main products. Get their wholesale price and use them to fulfil the bonus orders. [L1112.053]

Outside the standard criteria, in order:

1. Exhaust standard supplier research first.
2. In a niche with many product variations, check whether the top two or three suppliers make most of the sales, since deeper research often reveals more sellers.
3. Search marketplaces such as Etsy for unique products from sellers with many reviews, which shows they have proven sales.
4. Contact those sellers and ask to become an authorized retailer for their products.
5. Ask for wholesale pricing and confirm the numbers make sense before committing.
6. Look for independent craftspeople who sell through eBay, Facebook Marketplace, or Etsy and can make custom designs, colours, or patterns. [L1125.012]

In niches with very high product variety, such as wallpaper or horse saddles, the number of patterns, colours and textures is nearly unlimited. Buyers often search for exact names of patterns or materials. Small craftspeople and niche sellers can add many products and long-tail keyword traffic beyond what the top brands offer. [L1125.013]

Note on the US spelling in the course text: the outside-criteria unit uses "colors" and "patterns" in the same way. The source text is kept in full. [L1125.012]

### Prioritisation and choosing which brands to contact first

Use this order:

- **Gold, silver and bronze (2020, older):** classify each brand as gold, silver or bronze based on its reputation and reseller stance. Contact gold and silver brands first and log every response in the notes column. Follow up on each brand according to the reason and timing given. [L0468.031]
- **Gold suppliers on the list:** gold suppliers are often already on your research list from the start. Keep these names in your list, because they become realistic partners once you have traffic and sales. [L0486.018]
- **Best sellers first:** use the brands that top the Best Sellers or Recommended sorting on future competitor category pages first, because stores that know what they are doing place their best sellers at the top (2022, older). [L1113.016]
- **Lesser-known suppliers:** large, well-known suppliers often have thin margins, while lesser-known suppliers with less competition can be far more profitable (2021, older). Look past the familiar names when choosing suppliers. [L0671.006]
- **Newer brands in a competitive niche:** in a competitive niche, break in by approaching newer or up-and-coming brands that the largest competitors are not focusing on. Still apply to the established brands, but do not rely on them to get started (2022, older). [L0803.012]
- **Margin and shipping check:** before accepting a supplier, check the margin and shipping cost, because both determine profitability (2020, older). [L0967.042]
- **Start with approvals:** start sales and traffic with suppliers who approve you (2019, older). [L0663.048]
- **Online-only sources first:** source from companies that sell only online, since they already work with online retailers (2019, older). [L0663.048]
- **Competitor count versus quality:** a site having 20 brands is not proof of a market, unless the brands are actually distinct and sell products in your niche. [L1484.011]
- **Demand validation:** nearly 3,000 ratings with four stars on Amazon for a comparable product type indicate proven demand and can be used as a validation signal before testing (Source C). This is a demand signal, not a supplier source. [L1186.011]
- **Completeness versus shortlist:** the master supplier list should contain every supplier in the niche that works on the dropship model, not a selected top group. Stopping at a handful of suppliers limits later sourcing and comparison options. [L0930.009]
- **Earlier sourcing and the quality check:** the list must hold all qualifying suppliers, while outreach order is set by the classes above. These are two different stages. [L0930.009, L0468.031]

Older note (2020 and 2022): a supplier's large size does not mean good margin, and a list of 20 brands at a chosen price level remains the threshold for the quality check. [L0671.006, L0969.110]

### Size, validation and source mix

The number of suppliers the course asks for is stated in several places:

| Source | Year | Rule |
|---|---|---|
| Source A, supplier-contact step | 2025 | identify 20 or more potential suppliers in your niche [L0278.043] |
| Source A, master list validation | current | at least 20 brands need contact information [L0931.001] |
| Source A, source mix | 2022 (older) | a list should contain two or three sources; a single source with 20 or more brands plus a second with 10 or more brands is enough [L0665.009] |
| Source B, retailer build | 2021-22 (older) | at least 20 suppliers, do not spend weeks on this step [L0932.001] |
| Source B, competitor build | 2021-22 (older) | keep going until the list holds at least 20 supplier names [L0937.005] |
| Source A, trade show step | current | at least 20 suppliers in the niche [L0935.008] |

These figures apply at different stages:

- 20 is the threshold for completing the first list.
- The 2022 two-source rule is the sufficiency rule for a list that is thin.
- The 2025 step is the minimum for the supplier-contact stage.

[L0278.043, L0931.001, L0665.009, L0932.001, L0937.005, L0935.008]

Size depends on the niche. A niche like chandeliers may hold 500 to 1,000 suppliers across all competitors. A smaller niche may hold 10, 20 or even 2,000. The aim is to find every supplier in the niche that works on dropship terms, not a shortlist of the best ones. [L0930.008]

Example counts from the course:

- **Chandeliers:** the first competitor, a dropshipping store, yielded 32 supplier names. The second added about 20 more after duplicates were removed. [L0930.007]
- **Outdoor kitchens:** starting point was three future competitors: Wayfair, Zorro and Home Outlet Direct.
  - Wayfair's brand list in the outdoor kitchen category gave 29 brands in about 2 minutes.
  - Zorro's category, with 407 items, raised the total to 46 in about 1 minute.
  - Home Outlet Direct brought it to 54 in about 7 to 8 minutes overall.
  - After sorting and deleting duplicates, 52 brands remained. [L0931.006]
- **Air purifiers:** about 16 to 17 manufacturer brands from one retailer, as listed in the research section above. [L0937.006]
- **Australian 4x4:** at least 20 brands from one reseller's brands page. [L1484.015]

A single site with 20 brands is a starting point. It does not prove a market on its own, so check the brands across several sites. [L1484.011, L1484.015]

Validation checks for the niche:

- At least one competitor needs a traffic rank under 100,000.
- At least 20 brands need contact information. [L0931.001]

Do not build the initial list of 20 brands from grandfathered suppliers, as described in the channel section above. [L0666.032]

Keep the list large enough before building the store. Make sure the list holds enough brands before the store is built, since many brands may refuse online retailers. [L0460.030]

Two to three sources (2022, older): a master supplier list should contain two or three sources; a single source with 20+ brands plus a second with 10+ brands is enough. To grow a thin list, look at non-competing sites that sell similar products and check which brands they carry in the niche's subcategory. [L0665.009]

Keep the research list as a source of future options: each source counts as a source for more brands. Track future competitors and add every brand found to the master list, and continue beyond the first few brands, because the list grows as more sellers are checked. [L0459.032]

### Ongoing tracking and refresh

- **Keep tracking:** after a niche passes the dropship friendly test, keep tracking every future competitor and add every brand found to a master supplier list. Tracking should continue beyond the first few brands, because the list grows as more sellers are checked. [L0459.032]
- **Revisit the competitor list:** revisit the list of future competitors and check their current brand lists for new brands to contact. [L0666.018]
- **Repeat regularly:** trade shows take place every year and new brands appear regularly. Repeat exhibitor list research regularly, even for established stores (2019, older). [L1068.039]
- **Subscribe to newsletters:** subscribe to the email lists of competitors, suppliers and industry trade shows to see which publications and newsletters curate content. Watch what they cover so you know which outlets are relevant for your content. This serves content research, not supplier sourcing. [L0899.011]
- **Competitor pages stay open:** do not close a competitor site until the niche has been fully evaluated. [L0459.015]

### Handover to outreach

Order of steps before outreach (2025):

1. Add websites, phone numbers and emails of potential suppliers to the Master Supplier List.
2. Finalize the Master Supplier List.
3. Set up a business email address, phone number and mailing address.
4. Get approved with the first 5 suppliers. [L0532.008]

Build the master supplier list with brand names and contact details before the store is delivered, so suppliers can be contacted as soon as the store is ready (2025). [L1116.038]

Research stage only: at the research stage you only identify suppliers and record their details. Asking suppliers for approval to sell through your store comes later, so do not submit applications yet (Source B, 2021-22, older). [L0937.001]

Do not submit applications yet. Only record the online application page. Contacting suppliers and seeking approval come in a later step, after the website is built. [L0932.001]

Outreach procedure (2021, older), including the approval email and follow-up:

1. Research suppliers using store directories.
2. Send initial approval email.
3. If no response, follow up via phone.
4. Build relationships by managing communication professionally.
5. Take notes on interactions.
6. Continue adding suppliers even after initial launches. [L0270.030]

If only a few suppliers approve, use the trade show sidebar procedure above, then contact the exhibitor companies. [L0834.014]

Keep the list in a structure that is ready for outreach. Gather the website URL, phone number and email for each supplier, and keep the list ready before starting supplier outreach. [L0278.043]

#### Further details

1. Open a future competitor's website
2. Navigate to the specific product category for your niche (avoid unrelated categories)
3. Scroll to the brand or manufacturer section
4. Click 'Show More' if available
5. Copy each brand name
6. Paste brand names into Column A only
7. Repeat for all future competitor websites
[L0056.005]

- TSNN.com lists trade shows by industry and keyword. Exhibitor lists on each event website show companies that may become suppliers, and contacting them is often productive. [L0247.031]

- Find suppliers by first identifying future competitors in the niche, then extracting the brands they carry. The suppliers are the brands behind the competitors' product ranges. [L1097.017]

## Researching Supplier Contact Details

### Where contact research fits in the workflow

- Contact research comes after market research. Once the supplier list is built, the next step is to find each supplier's contact details before reaching out for approval. [L0590.004]
- The supplier list holds brands found while studying future competitors. Collecting contact details in advance saves time at the approval stage. [L0940.004]
- Contact research only collects contact data. Do not submit applications, sell-with-us forms or sales inquiries during this phase. Approval requests come later. [L0940.003]
- At this stage, fill only the URL, phone and email columns. Contact date, contact person and notes are completed in a later step. [L0940.002]

### Building the first list

- Give each niche its own master supplier list, named after that niche, so suppliers from different niches never mix. [L0931.003]
- Fill columns A to D first. Columns E to G stay empty at this stage and are completed later. [L0931.003, L0931.002]
- Stage-one layout:

| Column | Content |
|---|---|
| A | Supplier / brand name |
| B | Supplier website URL |
| C | Supplier phone number |
| D | Supplier email address, or contact form link if no email exists |
| Notes | Add-on or accessory products useful for cross-selling (usually blank for core-product suppliers) |

[L0931.002]

- The Notes column is optional. Use it for add-on products, upsell and cross-sell opportunities, or links to dealer or reseller application pages. [L0056.013]
- Record cross-sell items in Notes, for example a supplier selling knife sets for add-on or upsell offers. Most Notes stay blank for suppliers of core products, since those are the suppliers you would order the main products from. Add-on sellers are noted but are not treated as core suppliers. [L0931.015]
- Brands that do not sell the core product can still be recorded in Notes, but they are not treated as core suppliers. [L0931.015]
- A brand with no working contact information does not count toward the 20-brand minimum. [L0931.014]

### Per-supplier research routine

**Source A procedure, per supplier, in this order:** [L0940.001]

1. Open the master supplier list for your chosen niche and place it side by side with a browser window (split screen or a second tab).
2. Search Google for the exact supplier or brand name, starting at the top of your list.
3. Open the brand's own website (not a reseller or article) and copy its URL into column B (Supplier URL).
4. Open the site's Contact page (usually via the header menu, or the footer if missing there), copy the phone number and paste it into column C (Supplier Phone).
5. Copy a direct email into column D (Supplier Email); if only a contact form exists, copy the form's link instead.
6. If no site or contact channel appears, run the alternative searches (brand + niche + website; brand + niche; brand + customer service; brand + contact number; brand + Zoominfo).
7. If still nothing is found, enter N-A (not available) in the missing cells.

**Source B procedure (older, 2021-22):** [L0937.009]

1. Search the brand name on Google and open the official website from the organic results, not from ads. Do not rely on a guessed domain, and check the exact spelling, since similar names can belong to different companies.
2. Confirm that the site sells your product type and that the company is the manufacturer or brand owner, not a retailer.
3. Open the Contact Us page from the top navigation first, since it is the most likely place for contact details.
4. Check the footer for a phone number and any support or customer service page.
5. Copy the website URL, the email address (if one is published) and the phone number into the spreadsheet.
6. If there is no public email address, type 'Website contact form' in the Email column and still record the phone number.
7. Look in the header and footer for links such as 'Become a Dealer', 'Become a Retailer' or 'Become a [brand] dealer'. If one exists, note its page URL in the Online Application column and do not submit anything yet.
8. If a dealer page gives specific contact instructions (phone, form, mailing address), record that channel as the way to reach them later.
9. Move on to the next brand.

**Source B 2021-22 (older):** the research routine above is a copy-and-record process, so the research is done in a single pass per brand: search, verify, record, then move on. [L0937.009]

### Finding the official website

- For an exact brand name, the first organic Google result is often the official website. Always verify that it is the brand's own domain before copying it. [L0940.007]
- A Google search for a brand name returns its official site and often identifies its real domain, which is more reliable than guessing the URL. Skip ads at the top of results. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0932.008]
- Choose the brand's own website over marketplaces and retail sites such as Walmart, Amazon, Houzz, Target or Wayfair. Those sites show who sells the products, not how to contact the supplier. [L0940.008]
- A marketplace or retailer listing is not the brand's website. A brand that only appears on marketplaces may still be reachable through other search variants. [L0940.020]
- Do not guess supplier website addresses from the brand name. Search the brand on Google first, because some brands use unexpected domains or share a name with unrelated companies. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0932.009]
- Do not record a retailer's page instead of the brand's own site. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.016]
- Confirm the company is the manufacturer or brand owner, not a retailer. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.009]
- If a brand search returns many unrelated websites, add the niche keyword (for example 'kitchen') to the brand name and check the products section of the result. This narrows the results to the brand's own product website. [L0931.009]
- For a generic or shared brand name, add a niche word such as lighting, health or furniture. Adding a product word such as chandelier can also help separate the right result. [L0940.006]
- Do not dig deep into Google results pages. After the first few results they are mostly unrelated. Try a different search variant instead. [L0940.009]
- Copy the official website URL into the Supplier Website column and remove any tracking parameters or unnecessary URL elements. [L0056.010]
- If a supplier's website does not load, leave its row blank for now and move on. Come back to it later rather than spending time on a broken site. [L0931.014]

### Finding the phone number

- Phone numbers are usually under a Contact link in the header menu. If the header has none, check the footer menu. [L0940.010]
- If a brand's header and footer have no Contact link, check the sidebar menu of its website for a Contact entry before concluding that no contact page exists. [L0940.032]
- Phone numbers can appear in odd formats or as international numbers. Check that the number has enough digits and reformat it to US style with hyphens (3-3-4 digits, e.g. 555-010-0199). [L0931.010]
- If the number shown is international, look for the contact page number instead. [L0931.010]
- If several phone numbers are listed (for example toll-free and local), they usually ring the same place, so any one of them can be used. [L0940.017]
- Record the supplier's main contact phone number in the Supplier Phone Number column. [L0056.011]
- For a toll-free number, call it and ask for the person in charge of eCommerce accounts. Request their direct email address for future communication if you prefer to follow up that way. [L0133.009]
- A supplier can have an email but no phone number. In that case enter N-A in the phone column and keep the email. [L0940.016]
- Blank versus N-A for a missing phone: the Source A column rule says leave the cell blank if no number is listed [L0056.011], while the step-by-step routine says enter N-A once all search variants have failed [L0940.001, L0940.022]. Use blank when the supplier lists no number at all and you are filling the column; use N-A only after the alternative searches have also failed.

### Finding the email address or contact form

- Take the supplier's email address from its contact page. [L0931.011]
- When both a direct email and a contact form are offered, prefer the direct email. [L0940.015]
- When a contact page lists several emails for different distribution centres (for example USA and Canada) or offices and they all share one address, use that address. Otherwise choose the entry that matches the market you sell in. [L0940.012]
- When a brand lists both a sales inquiries email and a parts inquiries email, use the sales inquiries address. [L0940.013]
- When a contact page lists several locations or departments, choose the e-commerce or online sales entry, since that is the contact point for online sellers. [L0940.018]
- Enter the published email address in the Supplier Email column. If none is published, enter the URL of the contact form instead. [L0056.012]
- If a supplier has only a contact form and no direct email, paste the URL of the contact form into the email column. [L0940.014, L0931.016]
- Some suppliers have only a contact form for drop-shipping requests and no direct email. Enter that form in the email column so the supplier can still be contacted later. Record every supplier with a form this way. [L0931.016]
- Brand inbox addresses often follow the pattern info@[brand].com. Treat this only as a hint and confirm the address on the brand's website before recording it. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.012]
- Do not guess generic dealer or vendor addresses such as dealers@ or vendors@ and assume they exist. The lesson's test with this kind of address did not work for the brand checked. Look for named dealer or vendor contacts on the contact page instead. [L0931.012]
- Many brands sell only through resellers and publish no public email address, because they do not want consumers emailing them or receiving spam. A website contact form or a phone number is then a valid route, so record it as the contact method. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.010]
- Brands often do not sell directly to the public and prefer to supply resellers, so most will not publish a public email address. Missing public email is normal and is not a reason to drop a supplier. A phone number or contact form is enough to start. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0932.003]
- Brand contact forms often ask for name, relation to the company, street address, city, state, ZIP code, email, whether the company may contact you, preferred contact method, phone number, topic, subject and message, usually with a CAPTCHA check. Fill these in only when you actually contact the supplier, not during research. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.014]
- Form-label convention differs by source. Source B's sheet uses the text 'Website contact form' in the Email column when there is no public email [L0937.009, L0932.012]. Source A's sheet uses the form's URL [L0931.016, L0940.014]. Use the URL when you need to reach the supplier through that form later; use the label when the form is the only route and the URL is not needed.

**Examples (Source B 2021-22, older):**

- **Airfree:** the brand was found on Google, its official site showed a phone number in the footer, and no public email existed. The row got the phone number, the URL, and 'Website Contact Form' in the Email column, since the brand's contact form was its only public email route. [L0932.012]
- **Aerus:** the official site had a contact form and a phone number at the bottom, so the row was completed with both, and the form was noted as the contact route. [L0932.013]
- **Coway/Airmega:** the contact page listed a support email, a toll-free number and opening hours, plus a separate business and retail contact section. The business and retail contact was recorded. [L0932.013, L0937.013]
- Some manufacturers publish a separate Business and Retail contact path next to their consumer support. Use that business channel for supplier and wholesale inquiries and record the business contact rather than the consumer support line. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.013]

### Contact labels and routes

- Contact pages may use labels such as Contact Us, Customer Support, Customer Service, Dealer Contact or Get in Touch. Use the label that matches your goal, for example dealer or vendor pages when you want to become a dealer or vendor. [L0940.011]
- Brand websites often split contact routes by purpose, such as General Inquiry, Government and Volume orders, and Become a Dealer. Choose the route that matches your request. A dealer or wholesale request should go through the dealer page rather than a general contact form. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0932.017]

### Dealer portals and application pages

- A footer link to a dealer portal with a 'request access' option means the brand has a dealer program. Speak to the supplier first, by phone or email, before filling in the request form. Record the phone number and email you find for it in the usual columns. [L0931.013]
- When a brand publishes a dealer or retailer application page, the brand is signalling the first step it wants resellers to take. Follow the process it describes when contacting it later. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0932.004]
- If a brand offers an online application, use it as the first contact step, since putting it on the site signals that this is the route they want. If no application exists, that is normal and not a problem. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.011]
- Dealer pages often name a specific route for applicants, such as a phone number for quick help, a form that a sales representative answers, and a postal address marked for dealer inquiries. Follow the route the page specifies rather than a generic contact method. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.015]
- Example (Austin Air, Source B 2021-22, older): the site had a 'Become an Austin Air Dealer' page with a phone line for immediate assistance and an online dealer application form. The application page URL was recorded in the Online Application column, and the phone number was noted for contact. [L0932.014]
- A wholesale-only online store that hides prices behind a dealer login is still a valid supplier. Use its vendor or dealer contact page, because prices only become visible after approval. [L0940.024]

**Conflict on first contact:** Source B (2021-22, older) says to use the online application first if one exists [L0937.011]. Source A says to speak to the supplier by phone or email before filling in a request form [L0931.013]. Both fit the research rule not to submit anything during research [L0940.003]. Use the application first where the page clearly names it as the route (Source B); use a phone or email call first where the supplier has a dealer-portal request form (Source A).

### Verifying the address and the seller

- Verify a listed address before you rule a seller out or in: [L0459.011]
  1. Copy the address from the footer or contact page.
  2. Search it on Google.
  3. Open Street View in Google Maps to see whether the building is a store, a warehouse, an office or a training site.
  - An office or a learning centre does not count as a physical store, but it also does not prove the seller is online only. [L0459.011]
- When a brand name is searched with the word ZoomInfo, the results can show a company profile for a different business with a similar name. Check that the profile's industry and location match the brand before copying any phone number or email from it. [L0940.033]
- Unrelated businesses can share a brand name, such as a bicycle shop, a home care company or a Facebook page. Do not accept the first search result for an ambiguous name without checking it. [L0940.021]

### When the first search fails

**Alternative search sequence, Source A (when the brand's own site does not appear):** [L0940.019]

1. Brand name + niche + 'website' (for example Brand Name Furniture Website).
2. Brand name + niche only (for example Brand Name Lighting).
3. Brand name + customer service.
4. Brand name + contact number.
5. Brand name + Zoominfo (written as one word).

**Alternative search sequence, Source A (order of combinations):** [L0116.002]

1. [Brand Name] + Niche
2. [Brand Name] + Website
3. [Brand Name] + Customer Service
4. [Brand Name] + Phone Number
5. [Brand Name] + zoominfo

Both sequences cover the same search terms. Run them in the order given in the list you are working from; L0940.019 is the sequence used in the per-supplier routine [L0940.001].

- Do not mark a supplier N-A after a single failed search. Enter N-A only after the alternative search variants have also failed. [L0940.022]
- Reverse image search (older, 2021): [L0801.032]
  1. Search the exact supplier company name on Google, putting the full name in quotation marks so only exact matches appear.
  2. If that fails, open a competitor's product page in Google Chrome.
  3. Right-click the product image and choose the option to search Google for the image.
  4. Review the pages that contain the same image, since they may show the supplier's website or identifying information.
  5. Use that information to find the supplier.
- A brand without its own website may be a sister brand of another supplier. If the parent supplier is approved later, it may allow you to sell the sister brand too, so keep the entry and recheck it later. [L0940.023]

**N/A and blank handling**

| Situation | Entry | Source |
|---|---|---|
| All search variants failed for a field | N-A (not available) in the missing cell | [L0940.001, L0940.022] |
| No phone number listed (Source A column rule) | Leave blank | [L0056.011] |
| Email found, no phone | N-A in the phone column; keep the email | [L0940.016] |
| Only a contact form, no email | Form URL in the email column | [L0940.014, L0931.016] |
| Website does not load | Leave the row blank for now; return later | [L0931.014] |
| Brand has no working contact information | Does not count toward the 20-brand minimum | [L0931.014] |
| Brand has no own website but a parent supplier exists | Keep the entry; recheck if the parent is approved | [L0940.023] |
| Wholesale-only store with hidden prices | Use its vendor or dealer contact page | [L0940.024] |

### Reaching a person

**Outreach sequence, Source A (2024):** [L0836.035]

1. Call suppliers by phone whenever a number is available, since this is the fastest way to reach the right person and get a yes or no.
2. If no phone number is listed, find contacts through LinkedIn by searching the company name.
3. If nothing works, message the company's Facebook page or Instagram account if it is active.
4. Start with the less well-known suppliers that rank lower on future competitor stores. They are easier to get approved with and help build phone confidence before calling the key brands.

- Avoid sending physical mail to suppliers to get contacts, because mail is unlikely to reach the right person. [L0836.036]
- Email is acceptable as a first contact method only when a supplier has not listed a phone number on their website. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0235.008]
- When a supplier asks you to email them, ask for the name of the right person, their direct email and a phone number. Writing that person down lets you follow up with a specific contact instead of a general inbox. [L1285.022]
- If the first contact has outdated information: [L1287.017]
  1. Ask the supplier to confirm or provide a different contact person.
  2. If calls keep reaching the same person, look up the contact on LinkedIn and send a message.
  3. If the contact has a Facebook profile, send a Facebook message.
  4. Try email as well, and keep following up with the new contact.

### Tracker and supplier list layouts

**Working rules**

- Keep one tracking sheet for every supplier you contact, recording each outreach date, response, approval status and notes. This makes it possible to work down the supplier list in order without losing track of follow-ups. [L0804.117]
- Store every supplier contact in one sheet with the contact date, the person spoken to and notes, so all outreach stays traceable. [L0542.003]
- Track suppliers in a spreadsheet: Google Sheets with a supplier tracking template, with an Excel version as backup. Always make your own copy of the template before entering data so the original stays unedited. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.002]
- Use Google Sheets with a copied template (never edit the original template) or the Excel equivalent. The later columns for call tracking (Phone Call + Date?, Call Comments, Paused) are filled only when suppliers are contacted. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0932.015]
- During research only the first six columns are filled in: Supplier Name, Product Type, Email, Phone Number, Website and Online Application?. The remaining columns are used later, when you start contacting suppliers. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.003]

**Master supplier list, Source A 2020 (older), one row per brand:** [L0468.032]

| Column | What to record |
|---|---|
| Brand | Brand name |
| Tier | Gold, silver or bronze |
| Point of contact | Name and role |
| Email | Main email |
| Phone | Main phone |
| Backup contact | Second person or email |
| Approval status | Not contacted, contacted, approved, rejected |
| Notes | Reason for rejection, follow-up date, restock date, conditions for approval |
| Inventory updates | Weekly, biweekly or monthly email, members area, or none |
| Order processing | How orders are sent to the supplier |
| Average shipping time | Days from order to dispatch |
| Ship-from location | Warehouse city and state or country |

**Niche master supplier list, one row per supplier, with these columns in this order** (the same seven columns appear in three source units, so they are merged here): [L0540.004, L0542.003, L0543.004]

| Supplier Name | Supplier URL | Supplier Phone | Supplier Email | Date of Contact | Contact Person | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [supplier name] | [supplier website] | [phone number] | [email address] | [date of first contact] | [person you spoke with] | [terms, stock, shipping or other notes] |

**Supplier tracker, Source B 2021-22 (older):** [L0458.036]

| Column | Content |
|---|---|
| Supplier Name | brand or distributor |
| Product Type | category sold |
| Email | contact email |
| Phone Number | contact phone |
| Website | supplier website |
| Online Application Submitted + Date | date of the dealer application |
| Phone Call + Date | date of the call |
| Call Comments | outcome and next step, such as needs an advertising strategy or will pass on details |
| Approved? | yes or no |

**Supplier tracker with contact person, Source B 2021-22 (older), one row per supplier:** [L0771.025]

| Supplier Name | Product Type | Email | Phone Number | Website | Online Application Submitted + Date? | Phone Call + Date? | Contact Person | Call Comments |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

The Call Comments column records the date, the person spoken to and the outcome of each contact. [L0771.025]

**Supplier list layout, Source B 2021-22 (older), one row per supplier:** [L0929.001]

| Column | What to enter |
|---|---|
| Name | Supplier, brand or distributor name |
| Products | What they sell |
| Email | General email address |
| Phone | Number you call first |
| Website | Supplier website |
| Call date | Date you made the request call |
| Call comments | Anything specific they said about supplying, pricing or other notable points |
| Contact person | Name of the person you spoke to, who becomes your point of contact |
| Contact details | Direct email, direct line or mobile number that contact gave you |
| Approved | Yes or No |

**Supplier tracking spreadsheet layout, Source B 2021-22 (older), visible columns:** [L0937.003]

| Supplier Name | Product Type | Email | Phone Number | Website | Online Application? | Phone Call + Date? | Call Comments | Contact P... (cut off in the video) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

**Per-manufacturer tab, Source B 2021-22 (older):** [L1396.004]

1. Manufacturer name, phone and email.
2. A separate contact person for orders, one for shipping, and one for warranties and claims, if the manufacturer has several departments.
3. Discount codes and the discounts each team member may apply.
4. The current price list.
5. The payment method, such as a card on file or a phone payment.
6. Shipping requirements for the brand.

Discounts, shipping policies and price lists often differ by brand, so keep each brand's information fully separate. Staff who prepare quotes need the item price, shipping requirements and bulk-order discount rules to work without asking the owner. [L1396.005]

**Approval status in the tracker, Source B 2021-22 (older):** [L0458.035]

1. Google the product type with words such as supplier, distributor or manufacturer, or check the brands sold on competitor sites. (Older.)
2. Find each supplier's website and record its name, email address, phone number and website URL in a spreadsheet. (Older.)
3. Add columns for the online application submitted (with date), the phone call (with date) and call comments. (Older.)
4. Track every contact attempt, including email, phone and online form, and the response. (Older.)
5. Mark each supplier approved only when the supplier has confirmed you can sell its products. (Older.)

Ids for this procedure: [L0458.035]

**Supplier type and product type**

- Record the primary product type of each supplier, because you can then speak to the product the supplier actually makes. A brand can make more than one product type, so one supplier row may list several. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0932.007]

### Common mistakes

- Guessing a domain, recording a retailer's page instead of the brand's own site, or confusing similarly named brands (for example Aeris and Aerus). Check each brand's official site and product match before writing anything into the spreadsheet. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0937.016]
- Guessing generic dealer or vendor addresses such as dealers@ or vendors@. [L0931.012]
- Accepting the first result for an ambiguous name. [L0940.021]
- Marking N-A after one failed search. [L0940.022]
- Digging deep into Google results pages instead of trying a different search variant. [L0940.009]
- Copying phone numbers or emails from a ZoomInfo profile without checking industry and location. [L0940.033]

### Planning the time

- Finding phone, email and form details for over 40 suppliers took about 20 to 25 minutes in the worked example. Plan a comparable time block for a niche's supplier research. The brand extraction step itself is faster, at about 1 to 2 minutes per competitor. [L0931.017]

## Preparing the Business and Store Before Outreach

### Why suppliers screen before they approve

- Trust is the main factor in supplier approval. Suppliers share their full catalog with authorized retailers and depend on the retailer to represent the brand to customers, so approval is a judgment about whether you are legitimate and will act responsibly. Brands put their reputation in the retailer's hands and need confidence that the retailer will look after customers and is not a scam. [L0108.001, L0109.001, L1063.002]
- The suppliers' worst case is a retailer who takes orders but never fulfils them, or who takes the supplier's full catalog, photos, names and descriptions, collects payments, and never forwards orders or ships products. Customers then see the brand as a scam. Suppliers therefore vet retailers carefully and avoid those who show this risk. [L0109.003, L1063.003]
- Suppliers do not refuse retailers in principle, since retailer sales are how they earn revenue. Each supplier you contact needs proof that you are a real business that will treat customers properly. [L1063.004]
- Good suppliers screen applicants and approve only professional sellers who can explain their business clearly. Presenting the store professionally during the application improves approval chances. (Older, 2019.) [L1069.011]
- Brand owners want resellers who protect brand value by presenting products professionally on a functional, credible site, so a finished store is evidence that makes approval more likely. (Older, 2021-22.) [L1060.003]
- Suppliers expect an applicant to have already invested time and some money in the business. A visible setup, such as a working site and business emails, signals commitment before any approval. (Older, 2021-22.) [L1060.010]
- Established brands can be worth millions, so brand owners screen applicants to avoid scammers and unvetted resellers. A working website, business emails and consistent branding give them evidence that you can be trusted with their products. (Older, 2021-22.) [L1060.011]
- Cold outreach from someone with no website, brand or email looks unprofessional and wastes the supplier's time. Contact suppliers for reseller approval only after a real business presence exists. (Older, 2021-22.) [L1060.002]
- Removing red flags before you contact suppliers significantly increases approval rates, because it shows you are a professional, trustworthy business. [L0109.002]
- Suppliers judge store quality and professionalism when deciding on a partnership. A polished, complete store signals a legitimate business. [L0123.001]
- Suppliers check a dropshipping store for trust and completeness before approving it. [L0767.010]

### Sequencing: store, supplier list and outreach

- Build and launch the store before contacting suppliers. No legitimate supplier will approve you or accept orders without first reviewing a completed, professional storefront. [L0070.001]
- Suppliers are more likely to approve a retailer that shows a professional, operational storefront rather than an early-stage idea. A finished demo store means design and technical work is complete before outreach, which shortens approval timelines and reduces back-and-forth. [L0069.002]
- The store must exist before approaching preferred suppliers, because they usually will not approve a retailer with no store yet. [L0506.006]
- Build the website first, then apply for supplier approvals, because suppliers approve stores that already exist. [L1224.013]
- Before a supplier discusses approval, it usually asks where you sell, so have a live, high-quality website ready to show. The site lets the supplier picture its own products on your store. [L1422.021]
- Silver-tier suppliers approve new retailers through their own approval form and review the applicant's live store, so the store must be fully built and online before you apply. [L0711.022]
- Build the store before contacting suppliers, because suppliers review the site and it must look sufficient for your approval rate. (Older, 2020.) [L0852.027]
- A finished website shows suppliers how their products will look and that you are serious. Lesson figures state that approval rates are significantly higher when you have a website to show. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0458.030]
- Before the supplier review, the Shopify demo store should be fully set up and look finished, since suppliers review it during the approval conversation. A store that is not ready weakens the approval request. [L0590.003]
- A professional store and business setup make suppliers respond more positively, so prepare both before the first outreach. [L0590.005]
- Store approvals are easier when the store looks live, even with a few demo products. (Older, 2021.) [L0853.010]

**Timing conflict: store first or minimum essentials first**

The sources give three positions. The store-first position is the one repeated across most units above. Two other positions appear:

- Minimum essentials (older, 2020): do not wait for the demo store to be perfect. Complete the minimum essential elements by end of day, then immediately email the supplier with your store link and request next steps, because timing matters more than completeness. [L0245.070]
- Start early (2023): do not wait until everything on the store is perfect. Supplier approval takes time and delays add up, so start outreach as soon as the niche is chosen. [L1094.014]
- Split sequence (older, Source B 2021-22): in a high-ticket dropshipping model, the supplier list is built before the website is finished. The list is parked until the store is built and set up, and approval to sell the products on your site is sought in a later stage. Finish the supplier research now, set the list aside until the website is built, and contact suppliers only after the store is set up. [L0932.016, L0937.017]

When each applies: store first is required where the supplier reviews a live store, as with silver-tier and preferred suppliers. [L0711.022, L0506.006] Minimum-essentials outreach is the 2020 approach, which depends on the basics being in place before you send the store link. [L0245.070] Early outreach from the 2023 source starts from niche selection, so research and list building can begin immediately while the first approval request waits for the store to be presentable. [L1094.014]

**Supplier list and target**

- Add each supplier's website, phone number and email to a master supplier list. [L0393.007]
- Keep all supplier contact details in one Master Supplier List. [L0532.010]
- The target for the first store is approval from five suppliers. [L0393.008]

**Sourcing and approval order**

1. Add each supplier's website, phone number and email to a master supplier list. [L0393.007]
2. Finalize the master supplier list. [L0393.007]
3. Set up a business email address, business phone number and mailing address. [L0393.007]
4. Get approved by your first five suppliers. [L0393.007]

**Legitimacy build order**

1. Register the business as an official business entity. [L1063.005]
2. Set up official business email addresses. [L1063.005]
3. Set up a business phone number separate from your personal phone. [L1063.005]
4. Prepare a supplier contact plan. [L1063.005]
5. After approval, upload the live products you are authorized to sell. [L1063.005]

### Business entity and tax identity

- Register the business entity before contacting suppliers if you are committed to the venture. Switching from a sole proprietorship to an LLC or corporation later requires resubmitting all documents to each supplier. [L1283.014]
- Before approaching silver-tier suppliers, have a registered business entity, a business email address and a business phone number in place. Suppliers check these during review. [L0486.007]
- A registered entity, business email, dedicated business phone, and polished demo store signal trustworthiness during silver approval review. [L0110.009]
- Roughly 90% of suppliers ask for a sales tax certificate, and suppliers expect a business EIN rather than a personal Social Security number. An LLC name, EIN and sales tax certificate together are what is needed to start securing suppliers. [L0695.016]
- Many suppliers ask for your EIN when you start working with them, so have it ready before you contact suppliers. [L1344.011]
- Some suppliers require an EIN before approval and others do not. If the EIN is still pending, tell the supplier it is pending and keep reaching out to suppliers that do not require it. (Older, 2020.) [L0967.034]
- Suppliers usually ask for the business tax number during account approval, such as the ABN or ACN in Australia, so have it ready before contacting them. (Older, Source B 2021-22.) [L0702.022]

**Tax and certificate reference**

| Item | What the sources say | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Business entity | Register before contacting suppliers. Changing structure later means resubmitting documents to every supplier. | L1283.014 |
| EIN | Required before approval by some suppliers, not by others. Many ask for it once you start working together. Pending EIN: say so and continue with suppliers that do not require it. | L0695.016, L0967.034, L1344.011 |
| Sales tax certificate | About 90% of suppliers ask for one. Some require a state sales tax registration certificate for approval, usually obtained online as a PDF and emailed. (Older, 2020.) | L0695.016, L1111.046 |
| Reseller or state retail certificate | Same thing as the state sales tax certificate. Obtain it before buying from suppliers that require it. | L1115.017 |
| Resale certificate scope | State-specific. A Delaware certificate does not cover Connecticut. | L0274.004 |
| Supplier demands certificates in all 50 states | Unusual. Many states do not issue them, and the paperwork takes time. Consider not selling for a supplier that demands it. | L0806.020 |
| Obtaining a resale certificate | Through the state government website, usually a simple online form. Search for the state name plus resale certificate. A fee may apply through a third-party service. | L0806.021 |
| W-9 or resale certificate for a Canadian business | A Canadian-registered business generally does not need a US state sales tax ID. Explain that the business is Canadian or ask the supplier to clarify. (Older, 2021.) | L0834.032 |
| Liability insurance clause | When a supplier requires a commercial or liability insurance clause, ask your current home or car insurance provider for a general liability quote. Such policies are typically low-cost for new businesses. | L1115.013 |

**Conflict on certificates by state**

- Source A says not to apply for new certificates per state, because suppliers pushing this are using outdated practices. [L0274.004]
- Source B says that when a supplier requires certificates in all 50 states, consider not selling for that supplier. [L0806.020]
- Source C says a certificate must be obtained before buying from any supplier that requires it. [L1115.017]

These apply to different cases. Obtain the certificate for your own state through the state website. [L0806.021, L1115.017] Decline a supplier whose demand covers many states, since this is time-consuming. [L0806.020] Source A applies to supplier demands for certificates from states where you do not operate. [L0274.004]

### Business address

- Use a dedicated business address instead of your home address on supplier applications, your store website and other business accounts, so private information is not published online. [L0696.001]
- Publishing a home address on a store website exposes private information to anyone online and should be avoided from the start. [L0696.003]
- Using a virtual or mailing address for public business information is the approach. The speaker saw no evidence that a home address on a Google account causes suspension. (Older, 2020.) [L1088.019]
- Get the business address set up before contacting suppliers, because supplier approval forms ask for a business name and address. [L0696.002]
- A virtual business address is optional and separates your home address from business documents. A mail service holds the mail and either forwards it on a schedule or waits for pickup. [L0964.040]
- A supplier that asks for a business address can be given the virtual address, which is used for bank statements, credit cards and suppliers. [L0964.041]
- The virtual address matters only if the supplier requires one. A standard business address is the usual choice. [L0964.043]
- If a supplier needs a US mailing address and the seller is based abroad, use a US mailing address such as a UPS Store location in the seller's US shipping area. (Older, 2020.) [L1088.018]
- A P.O. Box looks less professional than a business address with a suite number. In the United States, a USPS P.O. Box must be listed as a P.O. Box, which signals a small box inside a post office. A UPS Store address does not require the P.O. Box label. [L0696.004]
- The source's speaker prefers a suite or unit style address, because the address is printed on the store. A P.O. Box can also serve as a virtual address. [L0964.039]
- UPS Store locations are widespread in the United States. A New York City ZIP code search returned more than 28 locations. [L0696.006]
- The cheapest UPS Store option is a folder in a cabinet behind the counter, where mail is stored for pickup or forwarding. Prices vary by location because UPS Store locations are franchises. [L0696.005]
- Some UPS Store locations offer a mailbox you open with a key. Others offer the folder-in-cabinet option. Ask the specific location which option it offers before you sign up. [L0696.016]
- Request the smallest size available and ask for pricing to keep monthly costs low for a business address. (Older, 2022.) [L0249.092]
- Post Scan Mail receives your post at an address you choose a plan for. For an extra fee it can open and scan paper mail and email you a copy. Its starter plan costs $15 per month in the United States. [L0696.008]
- Where possible, have business correspondence delivered electronically by email. Only mail that must arrive on paper needs a physical address or a scanning service. [L0696.009]
- Keep the exact same business address across the website, Google Merchant Center and Google Ads, and resubmit after any correction. Even a small mismatch can trigger a suspension or delay the account's reinstatement. [L0964.042]

**Procedure: set up a UPS Store business address in the United States**

1. Open the UPS Store website. [L0696.007]
2. Click Find a Store. [L0696.007]
3. Enter the ZIP code where you want your business address. [L0696.007]
4. Review the listed locations and click on the stores to check them. [L0696.007]
5. Visit the store in that area or call it. [L0696.007]
6. Ask what the mailbox costs, request the smallest size, and ask for the folder-in-cabinet option, which is the cheapest. [L0696.007]
7. Choose whether you pick up your mail in person or pay the store to forward it to you. [L0696.007]

### Business email

- Set up a business email address, phone number and mailing address before applying to suppliers, since suppliers are approached as a business. [L0532.010]
- Contacting suppliers without a business email address, business phone number and mailing address can lead to rejection, because suppliers expect a verifiable business. [L0393.024]
- The target for the first store is approval from five suppliers, and the business email, phone number and mailing address are needed before contacting them, since they need a professional business identity to approve you. [L0393.008]
- Contact suppliers from the store's own email address rather than an email tied to the business entity. (Older, 2020.) [L1088.016]
- Send supplier approval emails from a business address on your own domain, not a free personal webmail account such as Gmail or Yahoo. Free addresses look unprofessional and can slow supplier approval. [L1196.001]
- Use the same business address on the website, for customer replies and for supplier communication in the long term. [L1196.001]
- Use one primary login address, such as sales@yourdomain.com, for the Google Workspace account, and add further addresses as aliases. Aliases such as returns@ and a personal first-name address deliver to the same inbox, so one login manages all of them. Adding an alias does not require a separate paid account. [L1196.002]
- Use a personal alias with first and last name for supplier approval emails, so the supplier sees a real person from the business. Use the sales alias for general sales contact and the returns alias for return or refund communication. Separating addresses by purpose makes replies easier to sort. [L1196.013]
- Suppliers see the sender's name and address before approving a business account. A professional address with the first and last name builds more trust than a personal email. Use the firstname@ alias with the full name for supplier contact. [L1197.020]
- In supplier forms' marketing, purchasing, accounts payable or sales contact fields, a generic support or email address at the store's domain is acceptable when no real contact exists. [L0964.038]

### Business phone number

- Create a dedicated business phone number before contacting suppliers. [L0127.001]
- A store's public phone number should not be a personal line, because supplier callbacks then get mixed up with spam and unknown calls. A separate business number lets you answer supplier and customer calls without guessing who is calling. [L0338.001]
- OpenPhone and Google Voice are available tools for creating business phone numbers. [L0127.002]
- Use a US business phone number even if you run the business from outside the US, because suppliers ask for phone numbers and US-based stores usually need one anyway. Many members operate from abroad without problems. (2023.) [L0934.040]
- Purchase a local Skype phone number for your target market, for example a US number if you are calling from overseas. Suppliers see your local number rather than an international one, which improves answer rates. (Older, Source B 2021-22.) [L0235.006]
- Avoid collecting customer phone numbers before launch unless the supplier asks for them, because pre-launch sign-ups cannot find you before the store goes live. [L0934.151]

**Procedure: business phone setup**

1. Decide whether the personal phone number is acceptable for now, or sign up for Quo's Starter plan to get a business number. [L0338.011]
2. If using Quo, install the app and log in so calls work from the phone. [L0338.011]
3. Add the business phone number to the header and to the Contact us section of the footer in the Shopify theme. [L0338.011]
4. Click Save. [L0338.011]
5. Mark the lesson complete. The app must be ready before supplier calls begin. [L0338.011]

### Demo store setup

- Use 5 demo products, which is sufficient to show suppliers what the store looks like and how their products would be presented. (Older, 2020.) [L0268.013]
- A working homepage, one collection page and product pages are enough to show the supplier what their products will look like. (Older, 2021.) [L0853.010]
- A newer store can list a few products from a supplier at a higher markup to build credibility when approaching more brands. Choose the products with the best margins and do not include them in paid ads. Uploading the full catalog is not required. (2025.) [L0837.039]
- Do not use Oberlo for demo products. Build them manually following blueprint module 3 guidance. Demo products do not need real specs, materials or dimensions, since generic descriptions are sufficient when the products are not for sale. (Older, 2020.) [L0268.014]
- Do not use dropshipping apps to fill the store before supplier approval. Use stock images to build the site for suppliers to review. (Older, 2021.) [L0834.026]
- Build the demo store with stock or generic products so the supplier can picture how its products would look on your site. The demo is not meant to mislead them. (Older, 2022.) [L1113.025]
- Do not display one supplier's branded products on a demo store to pitch a different supplier. The first brand may find them through Google alerts and state that the store is not authorized to sell its products. [L0623.014]
- A good-looking live store that already sells other brands makes suppliers more willing to work with you. Show your site when you approach a supplier. [L0832.032]
- Well-organized filters and tags on the storefront show future suppliers how the website functions. Additional tag lists can be added over time as more suppliers are onboarded. [L0890.009]
- The store must show a logo, favicon, consistent colors, clean collections with images, working links and a visible email signup. [L1309.061]
- A buyer's guide on the store is optional before contacting suppliers. It adds credibility and trust. [L1285.018]
- Link the domain to Shopify before suppliers review the store. [L1283.015]
- Before you contact suppliers, a few days of study of store-building and traffic basics makes it easier to explain your business and tends to increase approvals. You do not need to implement those lessons yet. (Older, 2022.) [L0854.024]

**Password removal and purchase blocking**

- The storefront must be publicly viewable before you contact suppliers, because they check the URL themselves. Removing the password protection makes this possible. [L1294.002]
- The site must be supplier ready: the domain is linked to Shopify and the storefront password has been removed. Suppliers should see the store only once it is fully ready. [L1283.015]
- Do not set a store password when showing demos to suppliers. [L0856.058]
- To prevent purchases on a demo store shown to suppliers, disable all payment options, or leave the shipping profile with no selected zones so no one can check out. [L0856.058]

### Demo-mode disclosure

- If a supplier sees its products on your store before launch, explain that they are only demonstration placements, so the supplier can see how its products will look, and that you are not selling them yet. This also shows you can build and maintain a website. (Older, Source B 2021-22.) [L0929.027]
- Do not present a demo as a live shop. The demo is for showing layout and presentation, not for selling, and it is not meant to mislead the supplier. [L1113.025]

### Trust signals and pre-outreach checks

- Before contacting suppliers, the store must look trustworthy: links must work, there must be no typos or errors, and trust signals must be in place. Conversion optimization can wait until after the supplier approves you. (2024.) [L0806.016]
- Pre-outreach checks before contacting suppliers:
  1. Make sure the website is not locked behind a password. [L1114.025]
  2. Click every link to confirm it works. [L1114.025]
  3. Spell-check the whole site, including collection names and the About Us page, for obvious errors. [L1114.025]
  4. Confirm the basic structure is complete before sending supplier applications. [L1114.025]

### Credibility building

- A social media presence is not make-or-break for supplier approval, but suppliers often check that a company is legitimate by looking for active pages under the company name. (Older, 2021.) [L1112.050]
- A newer store can build credibility by listing a few supplier products at a higher markup, as described above, rather than by uploading a full catalog. [L0837.039]
- Presenting the store and business setup professionally is part of the approval case, together with the entity, email and phone already in place. [L0110.009, L1069.011]

### Readiness checklist

Before supplier calls, prepare the following:

1. A live, high-quality demo store that suppliers can review. [L1422.018]
2. A clear plan for generating additional sales for their products. [L1422.018]
3. An explanation that your focus as a business owner is exceptional customer service. [L1422.018]

Before the first supplier contact, confirm:

- Registered business entity, business email, business phone and business address are in place. [L0486.007, L0393.007, L1063.005]
- EIN, sales tax certificate and any state certificate that suppliers require are ready to send, and the EIN status is known if it is still pending. [L0695.016, L0967.034, L1115.017]
- The store is live without a password, every link works, the text is checked, and the demo products are in place. [L1114.025, L1294.002, L0268.013]
- The master supplier list is finalized. [L0393.007]
- The first outreach aims at approval from five suppliers. [L0393.008]

## Approaching Suppliers: Scripts, Calls and Positioning

### Why Suppliers Approve Retailers

- Suppliers evaluate every potential retailer on three traits: a professional, functioning website already operating; a clear, defined strategy for generating sales; and demonstrated exceptional customer service. [L0133.004]
- Show the three capabilities: a quality website that represents their brand well, a clear strategy for generating additional sales through your channel, and exceptional customer service that protects their reputation. [L0121.004]
- Approval is not guaranteed, regardless of website quality, traffic level or business experience. Not every supplier will approve you even with professional preparation. Success depends on the right communication strategy, not external credentials alone. [L0133.001]
- Suppliers accept a new retailer only when they believe it is a real, qualified business that will bring extra sales to their brand. Your job in the first contact is to make that belief clear. [L0927.002]
- Suppliers want retailers who prove themselves before approval, because a retailer represents their brand. They expect the retailer to honor policies such as MAP and to support customers after the sale. [L1094.008]
- Suppliers approve retailers they expect to care about customer service, run a lasting business and bring traffic that adds value to their brand. Show that intention in the approach call and in the store. [L0806.017]
- Suppliers generally want to work with new stores that are excited about their products. Clear enthusiasm and specific requests are usually well received. [L0804.037]
- Suppliers sell business to business, to physical and online retailers, so an online store is simply another retail customer for them. Approval is usually easy because you offer a sales channel that fits what they already do. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0458.039]
- High-ticket suppliers usually do not sell to the public. They earn from selling to retailers, so they wait for serious businesses that will manage sales to customers. The approval call is a request for a partnership, not a favour. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0771.029]
- The products already exist on the market and demand already exists. The store does not need to create demand; it taps into existing demand for these products. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0771.030]
- Suppliers evaluate outreach from their side and accept a retailer only when they believe it is a real, qualified business. [L0927.002]
- Suppliers often ask the same questions when reviewing an approval request. Prepare answers in advance so the first conversation is not stalled. [L0590.006]
- Dropshipping brands usually require approval before a store may sell their products. Copying a product list from the web and listing it directly is not a valid route. Plan a contact and approval step for every supplier. [L0590.001]
- Suppliers are approached directly, and the retailer becomes an authorized retailer through approval, not by paying for supplier access or joining supplier directories. [L0506.007]
- Ready-made phone and email scripts are used to ask suppliers for approval as a retailer, so that approval is obtained without spending money. [L0506.008]
- Contacting suppliers and getting authorized to sell their products costs nothing. The first real spending starts with ads, so supplier approval should be requested before any budget is committed to traffic. [L0360.004]

### Positioning: How You Come Across

- Establish yourself as an attractive opportunity they would regret losing, not as a retailer who needs them to succeed. Make them wish they had accepted you sooner. [L0133.002]
- Sounding desperate, or signaling that you need the supplier to succeed, damages your position. They should believe they would be making a mistake by passing on you, not that you depend on them. [L0133.014]
- Position the relationship as the start of a long-term partnership, not a transaction. Suppliers want retailers who grow with them and generate reliable repeat revenue over time, not one-off buyers. [L0133.006]
- The quality and content of each supplier conversation matter far more than call frequency. Make each call count rather than contacting suppliers repeatedly. [L0133.003]
- Suppliers receive many calls about the same products, so they ask what sets you apart. Show that your business is long term, that customer service is your priority, and that you understand their brand and why it fits your store, rather than only asking to list their products. [L0933.026]
- Approach suppliers owner to owner, because suppliers need sellers who promote their products. Frame your outreach as giving them an opportunity to be sold through your store. [L0832.031]
- Describing your launch plan works best when you have actually prepared it. Position yourself as someone about to take a market share, and show that you understand the subject well enough to be believed. [L0927.008]
- Do not call a supplier with a generic request, such as asking whether you can drop ship their products. Research their brand first and explain specifically why your store is a good fit for them. [L0933.028]
- Suppliers judge retail partners by whether they will last. Show that you treat the relationship as lasting, not as a quick resale opportunity. [L0933.026]
- Some suppliers want to see your marketing plan before they approve you as a retailer. Before calling, prepare a short outline of how you will sell their products, such as through Google Shopping ads and retargeting. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0771.065]
- Nervousness about calling is normal, since most suppliers are professional and welcome the contact. The discomfort decreases with practice, so use it as energy rather than trying to eliminate it before calling. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0235.012]
- If you feel nervous, start with smaller, less visible suppliers to build confidence and practice the pitch. Approach the larger brands after you are comfortable with the conversation and have a few approvals behind you. [L0941.106]

### Phone First

- Always call suppliers first rather than emailing. Phone contact is the most efficient way to reach the right decision maker (the eCommerce account person) on the first attempt. [L0133.007]
- Call every supplier with a phone number first. Use email or an online form as a follow-up or fallback. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0771.027]
- Contact suppliers by phone first rather than email, because phone calls achieve higher success rates and lead to stronger business relationships. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0235.001]
- Email is unreliable for initial supplier contact. Many recipients never read emails: inboxes get cluttered, unfamiliar senders are often ignored, and mailboxes reach capacity limits, so your opportunity depends on chance. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0235.003]
- Contact suppliers by phone when a phone number is listed, because phone contact gets better results than email. Use email only when no phone number is available. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0962.014]
- Calling suppliers gets better responses than emails. Emails are easily ignored, and a phone call creates a direct connection and a sense of urgency. *(2023.)* [L0970.030]
- Phone calls are the recommended way to reach suppliers. Once you reach the right person and describe what you want, the application usually takes 5 to 10 minutes to complete, and approval often follows right after it is returned. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L1086.031]
- Call suppliers instead of sending a long letter, because letters are easy to set aside or miss. A phone call reaches the right person quickly and gives a faster answer on approval. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0960.026]
- Reseller approval requests to brand suppliers can be sent by phone, by email, or through the supplier's online application form. The first contact states that you want to sell their products through your business and asks for their approval. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L1060.009]
- Call suppliers rather than submitting only the trade or dealer intake forms. A supplier listing a trade or designer program does not stop you from getting approved, since those programs mainly serve larger corporate or B2B buyers. [L0941.019]
- Phone supplier contacts early in the morning, around 8:00 to 8:30 AM in the supplier's time zone, before their workday fills up. Choose the suppliers you call by time zone so the morning call makes sense for each. *(2023.)* [L0835.017]
- Calling suppliers early in the morning, around 8:30 to 9:00 AM, can get you through to decision makers before their workload builds up. If responses are poor at other times, test this window for a few days. *(2023.)* [L0970.031]
- Before calling, check the business or retail contact hours on the supplier's contact page, which may be listed in a specific time zone, and schedule calls within that window. Some manufacturers run a separate business line from their consumer support number. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0937.032]
- Whether a supplier needs a personal meeting is rare, under 1% of supplier interactions. When a supplier wants to fly in, politely decline and suggest meeting at a trade show or local event instead, unless they are already nearby. *(2023.)* [L0964.019]

**Call timing: sources differ**

| Source | Recommended window | Year | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source A | 8:00-8:30 AM, supplier's time zone | 2023 | L0835.017 |
