| Source A | 8:30-9:00 AM; test this window for a few days if responses are poor at other times | 2023 | L0970.031 |
| Source B | Within the business or retail hours on the supplier's contact page | 2021-22 (older) | L0937.032 |

Both 2023 windows are early-morning calls. Use the supplier's time zone for the window, and test 8:30-9:00 if the 8:00-8:30 window gets poor responses.

### Preparing Before You Call

**Research the supplier**

- Research each supplier's website and company positioning before calling to identify specific connection points. Reference these findings in the pitch to show genuine interest in the brand, not generic outreach to every supplier in the category. [L0133.005]
- Research each supplier before calling, as you would prepare for a job interview. Suppliers approve retailers who show they understand and value the supplier's brand, and this improves approval rates. [L0928.007]
- Before a supplier call, learn its story: read its About and home pages, then open the conversation by saying what you like about the company and why you want to sell its products. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0962.015]
- Pitch with something specific from their website, such as better materials or a virtual showroom, and explain how it fits your customers' expectations. Generic praise weakens the approval chance. [L0928.009]
- Evaluate the supplier before calling: its company background, history, products and how it sells. [L0927.004]

Supplier research method (2024 version, as written):

1. Google the brand or product category. [L0806.015]
2. Check that the brand sells through online stores and is not a dropshipper. [L0806.015]
3. Open the brand's About us page. [L0806.015]
4. Spend about three to five minutes reading what the brand is proud of and wants customers to know. [L0806.015]
5. Use those points in the supplier conversation, for example customer service focus, long history of the brand, or shared location. [L0806.015]

Supplier research method (undated version, 5 to 10 minutes per supplier):

1. Open the supplier's website from your research worksheet. [L0928.008]
2. Read the About Us section and homepage to find what the brand says about itself, such as its philosophy, materials, quality or differentiators. [L0928.008]
3. Check for extra content such as a virtual showroom or 360-degree tour, and browse it. [L0928.008]
4. Note what stands out and what the brand wants customers to know. [L0928.008]
5. Plan to reference one or two of these points on the call and explain why this brand is a better fit than other suppliers. Spend about 5 to 10 minutes per supplier. [L0928.008]

*Both research methods are current. Use the 3-5 minute reading of the About page (2024) for a quick first pass, and the full 5-10 minute method when you prepare a supplier you plan to call.* [L0806.015, L0928.008]

**Know what you bring**

- Know what you bring to the table: your store, your audience, your marketing channels and your sales plan. [L0927.004]
- Prepare answers to likely questions so you do not depend on improvising under pressure. [L0927.004]

**Prepare the list**

- Open your completed master supplier list. Make sure each supplier has a URL, phone number and email. [L0928.001]
- Have at least 20 suppliers researched and filled in before starting supplier calls. Work in batches, for example researching 20 suppliers one day and calling them the next. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L1068.027]
- Batch order: 1. Research the contact information for the first 20 suppliers. 2. On the next day, call those 20 suppliers. 3. On the following day, research the next 20 suppliers. 4. Repeat the cycle. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L1068.028]
- Calling one supplier and then researching the next one slows the process down. Expect voicemails and callbacks, so a prepared list should be ready before calling begins. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L1068.029]
- The scripts, emails and day-by-day timing in the supplier contact plan are starting points you can edit to fit your store and wording. Keep the overall order of calls and follow-ups intact, because the sequence is built to keep outreach going until a supplier gives a definite answer. [L0936.020]
- Prepare for the most likely supplier questions in advance. *(see L0590.006 above)*

### Order and Pacing of Outreach

- Contact suppliers you expect to approve you first. Reach out to top-tier suppliers only after your business information is complete. Keep adding suppliers over time rather than waiting for every approval before uploading products. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0968.004]
- Start at the top of the supplier list. Once approved, sign the agreement, upload the products, then move on to the next supplier. Have products from the first two approved brands live with traffic before you proceed. *(2023.)* [L0804.026]
- Silver suppliers come first: they are the majority of the list, and gold suppliers are left for later. Call each supplier, even when its website has a dealer form. Introduce yourself and your store, and say you are about to submit the form. When a dealer application arrives, fill it out and return it immediately. Stop contacting new suppliers while several applications are pending, so each is completed and approved before you move on. Once all silver suppliers are handled, contact the gold suppliers. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0801.020]
- Order in a niche with many suppliers (older advice, 2021):
  1. On competitor category pages, note which suppliers' products appear first, since these pages sort by best-selling products. [L1090.143]
  2. Do not contact the top-ranked suppliers first. [L1090.143]
  3. Contact about 10 suppliers that rank lower but still carry products in the niche. [L1090.143]
  4. Get the first 5 to 10 approved and build comfort with the approval process. [L1090.143]
  5. Return to the top-ranked suppliers and seek approval from them. [L1090.143]
  6. Contact all remaining suppliers. [L1090.143]
- Credibility approach when a brand accepts a new reseller (2025): become an authorized seller, upload the supplier's products to the store, price them above the supplier's minimum price if margins are thin, and exclude them from paid campaigns. They can still be found through organic search and free Google Shopping listings, while paid ads go to proven sellers. Then reach out to more suppliers and mention the existing brand. [L0472.024]

**Pacing: how many approvals before you go live (sources differ)**

| Source | Rule | Year | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Approval workflow | Keep applying until at least two approvals | 2023 | L1094.012 |
| Outreach order | Do not go live until at least two are approved | 2023 | L1094.117 |
| Source A (contact steps) | Repeat until approval from five suppliers, then build silver and gold tiers over time | undated | L0126.008 |
| Onboarding | Repeat until at least five suppliers are approved and all their products are live | undated | L0495.002 |
| Product count | About five products per category, or about five products in total, is generally enough when applying | 2020 | L1088.010 |

Use two approvals as the minimum before going live (2023 sources), and five as the target that the undated contact plan and onboarding procedure set. Keep the 2020 product count in mind when you build the application.

## Call Sequence and the First Conversation

**Goal of the first call**

- The goal of the first contact is only to reach the decision maker, not to close the deal. Keep the early conversation short and focused on introduction, your planned launch and your reasons for choosing them. [L0928.004]
- Keep calls short, maintain professionalism and focus on clarity and confidence. [L0126.004]

**Full call procedure (17 steps)**

1. Research the supplier's website and company positioning to identify why their products fit your niche. [L0133.015]
2. Find the supplier's phone number via Google search or directly on their website, looking for a toll-free number. [L0133.015]
3. Call and ask for the person in charge of eCommerce accounts. If unavailable, request their email and/or direct phone number for a callback. [L0133.015]
4. When you reach the eCommerce decision maker, begin by briefly explaining that you own an eCommerce store focused on their niche and want to add quality suppliers to your catalog. [L0133.015]
5. Give a brief overview of your company without referring to any other websites you operate. [L0133.015]
6. Explain that your team is finishing a major project in their niche that you are about to launch. [L0133.015]
7. Share your excitement, noting that your team has extensively researched the niche and is confident you can dominate it with your marketing approach. [L0133.015]
8. State that you are building out your catalog before launching a major marketing push on [specific date: 1-2 months from today]. [L0133.015]
9. Reference specific findings from their website that show why their products are a perfect fit for your customers. [L0133.015]
10. Express confidence that your company will become one of their top retailers within weeks of launching. [L0133.015]
11. Emphasize building a long-term relationship, similar to partnerships you have developed with other suppliers in other niches. [L0133.015]
12. Clearly state you are not looking for special payment terms, only a solid business relationship. [L0133.015]
13. Ask directly how to apply for an eCommerce account. [L0133.015]
14. If they say no or that they are not taking new retailers, explain how it is a shame they will miss out on the significant launch you are planning. [L0133.015]
15. Ask who specifically needs to be involved to make the partnership happen. [L0133.015]
16. Even if declined, understand they will remember this call and who you are, which positions you for future contact. [L0133.015]
17. Document the conversation completely: the person's name, the date and all details discussed. [L0133.015]

**Eight-step workflow (decision maker focus)**

1. Open the master supplier list and make sure each supplier has a URL, phone number and email. [L0928.001]
2. Call first whenever a phone number exists. Use email only when no phone is available. [L0928.001]
3. On the call, introduce yourself and your store, state the planned launch and why you like their products, and ask how to apply for a supplier account. [L0928.001]
4. Ask to be connected to the decision maker, who is the person who sends the approval form or grants access. [L0928.001]
5. Speak with the decision maker, reference something specific you learned from their website, and ask for the dealer application form to be emailed. [L0928.001]
6. As soon as they promise the form, give your email address, thank them and confirm you will return it right away. [L0928.001]
7. Fill out and return the form the same day you receive it. [L0928.001]
8. Log every contact in the tracking columns, then follow up on the agreed date. [L0928.001]

- When a supplier agrees to send an approval or dealer application form by email, give them your email address during the call, thank them, and confirm you will send it back right away. Then check your inbox, fill out the form and return it immediately. [L0928.015]
- Call the supplier and ask to reach the right decision maker during that same call. Ask them to email the approval form right after the call. If a promised callback or email does not arrive within a few days, follow up. Return the completed form as soon as possible. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0853.012]
- Do not ask a supplier many detailed questions before they send their approval form, because many answers are in that form. Ask follow-up questions after the form is returned. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0853.013]
- Trade account contact (2022): 1) Call and ask for the person in charge of opening new accounts. 2) If it is a small company, that person may answer directly; if larger, ask who handles it. 3) For email outreach, a short intro works: state you are interested in selling their products and ask who opens new accounts for internet retailers. *(Older advice, 2022.)* [L0969.012]
- Getting a supplier approved by phone (older advice, 2021):
  1. Call the supplier rather than relying on email, since phone gives a faster answer. [L1090.023]
  2. Say you want to open an account as an online retailer and ask who handles new retailer accounts. [L1090.023]
  3. If nobody answers, ask how to email or whom to contact for a new account. [L1090.023]
  4. Follow up by email if no one calls back, because follow-up is part of the process. [L1090.023]
  5. If no phone number is listed, use supplier research tools to find other contact numbers. [L1090.023]
- Calling a supplier to ask about dropshipping stores (undated):
  1. Call the supplier and ask whether they work with dropshipping stores. [L0495.002]
  2. If they agree, ask them to send you the supplier application, and give them your email address so they can send it. [L0495.002]
  3. Fill in the application and return it to the supplier as soon as possible. [L0495.002]
  4. When the supplier sends you their product information, upload their products to your store right away. [L0495.002]
  5. Repeat these steps with more suppliers until at least five suppliers are approved and all their products are live on your store. [L0495.002]
- Trade account procedure (2020):
  1. Contact the brand by phone or email and ask for an account. [L0468.033]
  2. Ask which format they use for inventory updates and whether they can send a regular report. [L0468.033]
  3. Ask for the top three best-selling products with their weight and packaging size. [L0468.033]
  4. Record every answer, the reason for any rejection, and the date to follow up. [L0468.033]
  5. If the brand does not respond, follow up every few days. If it rejected you, follow up when the stated condition is met. [L0468.033]
- Approval workflow (2023):
  1. Call the supplier first and ask for the name and direct email of the person responsible for retailer relationships, even if they say to email. [L1094.012]
  2. Send a short, direct email to that contact that mentions the phone call, the store link and a request for the next steps. [L1094.012]
  3. If there is no reply after a few days, reply to your own first email with a short follow-up. [L1094.012]
  4. Keep applying to more suppliers until you have at least two approvals. [L1094.012]
- Outreach for approval (2023), in short form: call the supplier and ask for the name and direct email of the person who handles retailer relationships, even if they first say to email. Send a very short email to that contact that names the person you spoke with, includes the store link and asks for the next steps. If there is no reply after a few days, answer your own first email with a brief follow-up. Keep contacting more suppliers until at least two are approved before going live. [L1094.117]

**Call scripts and rules**

- Use phone scripts as conversation frameworks rather than reading them verbatim. Before calling, review the script and prepare to speak naturally. [L0126.004]
- Do not present your business as established. Keep to what is true, and never claim anything you do not have. [L0365.006] *(See the honesty section below.)*
- Send Day 1 emails only if the supplier requests them. [L0126.005]
- Do not bring up dropshipping, and do not say that you only want to resell their products (the opening pitch). [L0928.002]
- Note the conflict: L0928.002 says not to bring up dropshipping in the pitch, while L0495.002 has you ask whether the supplier works with dropshipping stores. Use the wording of the first pitch from L0928.002. Ask the dropshipping question in the step of L0495.002 only if the supplier raises the subject or the step requires it. [L0928.002, L0495.002]

#### Opening script, phone (2023 version)

```
Hi, [name of person who answers], this is [your name] calling from [your company name]. We run a group of boutique e-commerce shops across several niches, and recently we spotted an opening in the [supplier's product niche] niche. We have just launched [store name, URL] and are looking for dependable suppliers who want to work with our shop. Based on our early research, my team and I believe our marketing will make this new venture our strongest yet. We are not asking for credit terms or anything similar; we simply want a sound working relationship. Could you tell me how we would go about applying for an account with [supplier name]? Once you have said this, stop and listen to their reply. Do they offer to help directly, or do they refer you to someone else? Write down the name of anyone they mention. Do not bring up dropshipping, and do not say that you only want to resell their products.
```
[L0928.002]

#### Pre-launch script, phone

```
Hi, [name of person who answers], this is [your name] calling from [your company name]. We are only a few [time frame, e.g. weeks or months] away from opening [store URL]. We are seeking reputable brands that want to supply our customers with the finest niche products, with the best [product type]. Given our research, we think your range would be a strong addition to our catalog, and we would be delighted to have you on board for our big launch. We are not requesting payment terms or anything of that nature; we just want to build a solid business relationship. Could you let me know how we can apply for an account with [supplier name]? Never claim to have existing stores or sales if you do not have them. Describe only the store you are actually building.
```
[L0928.003]

#### Opening script, phone (2019 version)

```
Hi, this is [Your name] from [Store name]. We have been researching brands in [niche], and your [product] stands out for its quality. We believe our customers would love it. Could you connect me with the person who sets up new trade accounts, so we can get started?
```
[L0960.028] *(Older advice, 2019.)*

#### Phone script for a supplier who wants a dealer application (older advice, 2021)

```
Hi, this is [your name] calling from [your store name]. We would love to sell your products because of [reason 1], [reason 2] and [reason 3]. Could you tell me the best way to get our dealer application approved?

If the supplier says they are not opening new e-commerce accounts right now, respond with:

That's too bad, because I know your products would fit well into our collection, and I know we would be able to promote them to our audience. Could you let me know when would be the best time to follow up?

When the supplier says it is not possible at this time, respond with:

Okay, we are moving forward with our opening date, and we would love to work with you in the future. When would be the best time to follow up?

Add the follow-up date and the supplier's answer to your notes list, and follow up on that date with your results.
```
[L0801.021] *(Older advice, 2021.)*

#### Phone script for suppliers who accept only a website form (older advice, 2021)

```
Okay, great. My name is [your name] from [your store name]. We are going to get that form submitted right away.

Then submit the form immediately and follow up by phone until the dealer approval is confirmed.
```
[L0801.022] *(Older advice, 2021.)*

## Email Outreach

- Copy email templates directly from the tool and customize them only slightly if needed. Send Day 1 emails only if the supplier requests them. Follow-up emails from Day 2 onward keep momentum without pressure; use them if the initial contact receives no response. [L0126.005]
- Email procedure: 1. Email the supplier and introduce your store, including the store link. 2. Ask the supplier for product images for the description and for the prices. 3. Upload the products once the images and prices arrive; many suppliers reply yes right away. [L0832.030]

#### Email template, wholesale account

```
Hi [Support Person Name],

I'm [Name] from [MyCompany Inc]. We run a collection of focused eCommerce stores across different market segments and have recently identified strong potential in the [Suppliers Products] category. We've just launched [newnichewebsite.com] and are seeking reliable suppliers interested in working with our business.

Our detailed market analysis of this niche gives us confidence that our promotional approach will make this store one of our strongest performers. We're currently building out our product inventory before we execute our primary marketing push around [specific date 1-2 months from today].

We're not looking for any special payment arrangements-our focus is establishing a solid, trustworthy partnership.

What's the process for us to apply for a wholesale account with [Supplier Name]?
```
[L0133.018]

#### Email template, distributor account

```
Hi [Support Person Name],

I'm [Name] with [MyCompany Inc]. We're planning to launch [newnichewebsite.com] within [Time Frame: 1-2 months] and are actively seeking quality brands to supply our customers with premium [Niche Products].

After analyzing both the [Niche Products] market landscape and your company directly, we're convinced your products would be a valuable addition to our catalog and want you involved from our official launch.

We're not seeking preferential payment terms-we're interested in building a reliable, professional working relationship.

How do we go about opening a distributor account with [Supplier Name]?
```
[L0133.019]

#### Phone script, retailer enquiry

```
Hi, my name is [Your Name] and I'm calling on behalf of [Company Name]. I own an eCommerce store and I'm interested in becoming a retailer for [Supplier Name]. Can you please direct me to the person who handles setting up eCommerce accounts?
```
[L0133.020]

#### Email template, trade account enquiry (older advice, 2019)

```
Hi, I am looking to speak with someone at your company about becoming a retailer. Could you reply to this email or send me your phone number so we can talk?
```
[L0356.031] *(Older advice, 2019.)*

- For email outreach to a trade account, a short intro is enough: state that you want to sell their products and ask who opens new accounts for internet retailers. *(Older advice, 2022.)* [L0969.012]

## Presenting Your Business Honestly

- Stay honest when contacting suppliers. There is no need to lie about who you are or what you have done to get a good supplier relationship. [L0365.006]
- Supplier sourcing templates and Q&A forms are available in the sourcing section to structure supplier outreach. Use them to present your business accurately. [L0365.007]
- Never claim a longer history than you have. If your store is not live yet, point to any earlier online or offline business experience you can honestly name. [L0933.008]
- When a supplier asks how long you have been in business, the question is about whether they can trust you to stay around and treat their brand with respect. [L0933.008]
- Established suppliers judge whether a new store will last mainly by its track record. A store that is not live yet should say so and point only to real earlier online or offline business experience, with no inflated history. [L0933.034]
- Our store is a new venture, and we are launching it now as part of our launch strategy. [If you have prior experience: Before this, we ran [previous business, for example an eBay store or an offline business] for [number of years].] [L0933.009]

#### Template: new venture with optional prior experience

```
Our store is a new venture, and we are launching it now as part of our launch strategy. [If you have prior experience: Before this, we ran [previous business, for example an eBay store or an offline business] for [number of years].]
```
[L0933.009]

**Using "I" and "we"**

- In outreach emails from a one-person store, writing "I" instead of "we" is acceptable, and a small mismatch like this is unlikely to end a supplier deal. Using the word "niche" in supplier communication is also fine. *(2023.)* [L0470.101]
- When speaking to a supplier, using "we" to refer to your business is acceptable as long as you do not claim anything untrue. The "we" refers to your company, not to a false claim about size or history. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0960.027]

**Expertise in a technical niche (older advice, 2020)**

1. Do not claim expertise you do not have. [L0967.100]
2. Study the niche and its product types at a surface level, for example through a few hours of video covering the basics. [L0967.100]
3. Tell the supplier honestly that you have a working knowledge and aim to become an expert. [L0967.100]
4. State that you want a good working relationship so you can educate customers and build a resource section on the store. [L0967.100]

## Demo Store, Launch Date and Existing Stores

**Demo store**

- When contacting suppliers, always have a demo store ready and mention it during approval conversations. Show it as a living example of how their products will appear on your store. *(Older advice, 2022.)* [L0249.014]
- Always inform a supplier in advance that your store is in demo mode. This frame prevents suppliers from misinterpreting a limited product catalog or demo placeholder items as evidence of an unfinished or fraudulent operation. [L0294.005]
- Sample wording for demo mode: "My name is [Your Name], I've been building e-commerce stores for [your years of experience], please visit this site to see how your products would look. The store is launching on [specific launch date]." [L0294.005]
- During supplier review you can share your store template with a stated launch date, so the supplier can see how their products would look on it. Do not present the unfinished store as an active shop with products for sale. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0834.120]
- Do not start with cold calls to request authorization, even when a demo store already exists. First determine why a supplier would choose you, and set up legitimacy signals beforehand. [L1063.006]
- Note the sources: L0249.014 says always have the demo store ready and mention it during approval conversations. L1063.006 says a demo store does not justify a cold call that asks for authorization. Use the demo store in the approval conversation, after you have established why the supplier should choose you. [L0249.014, L1063.006]

**Existing stores**

- If you already have successful online stores (dropshipping or otherwise), mention this when contacting suppliers. It helps with approval regardless of whether the stores are in the same niche. *(Older advice, 2022.)* [L0249.015]
- Never claim to have existing stores or sales if you do not have them. Describe only the store you are actually building. [L0928.003]

**Launch date**

- When calling suppliers, specify a launch date 1-2 months in the future. Use an actual date you have set for your marketing launch, not a hypothetical timeline, so you can reference real results in follow-up calls and strengthen credibility. [L0133.010]
- State a real planned launch date when speaking to a supplier, and keep it no more than two months in the future. A date further out makes the supplier treat you as not relevant now and ask you to come back later. [L0928.005]
- When giving a supplier a launch date, a window of about three to four weeks is typical, though a fixed date such as January 1 can also be given. [L1094.015]
- Describing your launch plan to a supplier works best when you have actually prepared it. [L0927.008]
- Use the launch strategy, not previous store history, to build excitement. Reference the launch date to set expectations. Include scripts from module 4.6 or Drop Ship Weekly training. *(Older advice, 2020.)* [L0268.016]

**Launch date: sources differ**

| Source | Rule | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Default | 1-2 months ahead, an actual date you have set | L0133.010 |
| Limit | Never more than two months ahead | L0928.005 |
| Typical window | About three to four weeks, or a fixed date such as January 1 | L1094.015 |

Use the 1-2 month window as the default. Keep the date within two months, and use the 3-4 week window when you need a short, realistic timeline.

- Use the launch date in the supplier pitch to ground the conversation. [L0133.010, L0928.005]

## Traffic and Sales Questions

- During a launch, if you have no traffic yet, tell suppliers you plan to send hundreds of qualified leads to your website every day. Do not present current traffic numbers you do not have. [L0933.010]
- Suppliers want partners who add value, mainly through orders. Projected sales are essentially a guess, so do not invent a sales forecast. State a baseline instead: a brand must be able to reach at least 10,000 USD per month in sales through your store, otherwise it is not a good fit. [L0933.011]

#### Template: traffic and baseline sales answer

```
Right now we do not have traffic yet, but we are planning to send hundreds of qualified leads to our website every single day. I do not want to make up a sales number. What I can tell you is that if I cannot get you at least 10,000 USD a month in sales, I do not think it would be a good fit for us. So our baseline goal is at least 10,000 USD a month in sales for your brand on [your store domain].
```
[L0933.012]

- For traffic, use the statement from L0933.010 and avoid any figure you cannot support. [L0933.010, L0933.012]

#### Template: sales revenue and credit references (2023)

```
I'm not comfortable sharing specific revenue numbers. What I can tell you is we're getting X unique visitors each month and seeing profitable conversions with our existing supplier partners X, Y, and Z. I'm confident we can do the same with your products.
```
[L0274.063]

## Warehouse and Stock Questions

- Suppliers ask about your warehouse to learn whether you are an online-only retailer who needs their products stocked and shipped by them. Your answer should explain your focus, not present the lack of a warehouse as a gap. [L0933.001]
- Do not answer the warehouse question by saying you are new and may have a warehouse one day. That sounds like a weakness and invites doubt, so state it as a deliberate business focus. [L0933.003]

#### Template: warehouse answer

```
That is not something we do. Our focus is on marketing, customer service and retail. We are an online retailer and would be a non-stocking dealer, which means you keep the products in stock and ship orders for us. Our focus is on being the best online retailer, bringing awareness to brands like yours, fulfilling orders and providing exceptional customer service. So we do not handle warehousing, and that is by design.
```
[L0933.002]

#### Template: how the model works (2022)

```
Here is how we operate. Each week our suppliers send us an inventory report listing what they hold in their warehouse, and we add their products to our store based on what is available right now. We pay for ads that promote these products. When a customer places an order, we forward it to the supplier, usually by email, and the supplier ships it through its own shipping account. If you cannot ship through your account, we can provide shipping labels for every future order.
```
[L0854.009] *(Older advice, 2022.)*

## Competing Brands and Other Suppliers

- Suppliers ask which other companies you work with because they want to know whether others have already approved you and whether competitors are already selling on your site. They do not want to lose sales to competing brands, so they want to be on your site too. Answer with the right information for that reason. [L0933.005]
- If you have not been approved by any supplier yet, name the two or three biggest players in your niche that you are reaching out to. If you are already approved with one or two suppliers, name them and say you will work with them for your launch. If you are in discussions with other companies, say you are in discussions. [L0933.006]

#### Template: competing brands answer

```
Right now, as you know, we have not launched yet, but we are reaching out to [top brand 1], [top brand 2] and [top brand 3], which we see as major players in this industry. [If approved with other suppliers: We have already spoken with [approved supplier A] and [approved supplier B], and we will be working with them for our launch.] [If in discussions with other companies: We are currently in discussions with other companies in this space.]
```
[L0933.007]

#### Template: why you, not just a listing

```
We are not just looking to list products. We are focused on the long term, and customer service is our priority. When we researched the market, we saw [number or description of competing brands], and what we love about your [specific product attribute, for example design, quality or uniqueness] is [why it stands out]. We will treat your customers with care and bring people to your store who want what you sell because of [specific benefits of their products].
```
[L0933.027]

- A broad approval email avoids over-explaining (older advice, 2021): When a supplier asks which companies you compete with or what your business does, keep the initial approval email broad and vague. Do not over-explain your business model, customer base or marketing strategy in early communications. Let the supplier focus on whether they can work with online retailers, not on your specific market positioning. [L0270.066] *(Older advice, 2021.)*

## Credit References, Prepay and Bank Details

**Credit terms**

- Make clear you are not asking for credit terms. Suppliers usually give credit terms of 30 or 60 days to physical retailers that buy in bulk, and a dropshipping business should not request them. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0929.007]
- State that you pay upfront for each order, before the product leaves the supplier's warehouse. Because the supplier is paid before shipping, it carries no financial risk, which makes approval easier. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0929.008]
- Use prepay terms with suppliers, meaning you pay for orders before they ship. (2018.) [L1085.016]
- If a supplier asks for trade references, you may use references from another niche. Tell the supplier you are not asking for credit terms, since references are usually requested to check payment history for credit. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0853.062]

**Credit references on applications (2018 and 2023)**

- If a supplier application asks for three credit references, you can leave that section empty when you use prepay terms, because it only matters for credit terms. *(Older advice, 2018.)* [L1085.016]
- When a supplier asks for bank details or a location, this is usually a credit check. If you are prepaying orders, you do not need to provide bank information. On the form, cross out the bank section and write prepay terms. *(2023.)* [L0970.047]

#### Template: revenue and credit references (2023)

The template for revenue and credit references is in the section "Traffic and Sales Questions" above. [L0274.063]

## Revenue, Marketplaces and International Distribution

#### Template: marketplaces and annual revenue (2022)

```
We sell exclusively through our own store, [store domain]. We are launching on [launch date], and we are excited to include your brand because [reason, e.g. your product quality or demand in our niche]. Our annual revenue is currently at the launch stage, and we plan to grow it with paid advertising and organic search.
```
[L0854.010] *(Older advice, 2022.)* Replace the brackets and keep the launch wording, which focuses on excitement rather than the zero revenue.

#### Template: supplier in another country with possible US distribution (2022)

```
Hello, I'm [your name] from [store name]. I know your product line and noticed your products are listed on many websites that sell in the US. Do you have distribution here, or do you work with a fulfillment center that ships directly to US customers? Could you explain how that works?
```
[L0854.021] *(Older advice, 2022.)* Fill in the brackets.

## Follow-Up and the Contact Plan Tool

**The tool**

- The Supplier Contact Plan Tool generates a personalized 7-day outreach schedule with phone and email scripts, daily action guidance and follow-up timing. Access it by entering your business details (name, company name, store name, store URL, business phone and email address in first.name@domain format) and supplier information (company name, website, phone if available, and tier; select Silver for most suppliers). [L0126.001]
- Day 1 outreach starts with a phone call asking about the supplier's application process and identifying the decision maker. If the supplier requests email instead, use the Day 1 email template. The 7-day schedule continues with follow-ups through Day 7 and stops when you receive approval, rejection or "not right now." [L0126.003]
- Download and save each contact plan for your records. For multiple suppliers, generate a separate plan for each one by replacing the supplier details, and work through your list sequentially. [L0126.009]

**Contact plan procedure**

1. Open your completed master supplier list and generate a contact plan for the first supplier. [L0126.008]
2. Initiate Day 1 outreach with a phone call asking about their application process and identifying the decision maker (use the email template only if they request it). [L0126.008]
3. Record the date, contact person's name and conversation notes. [L0126.008]
4. Follow the 7-day contact schedule with the mix of phone calls and emails provided. [L0126.008]
5. Stop contacting when you receive approval, rejection or "not right now." [L0126.008]
6. Upon your first approval, proceed to the next lesson and upload that supplier's live products. [L0126.008]
7. Return to this lesson and generate a new contact plan for your second supplier. [L0126.008]
8. Repeat steps 1-7 until you have approval from five suppliers. [L0126.008]
9. Continue this process with additional suppliers from your list to build silver and gold tier approvals over time. [L0126.008]

**Follow-up timing (older advice, 2020, and sources)**

- If there is no response, follow up every 2-3 days. If they say "we'll follow up," follow up with them after 1 week if they have not responded. *(Older advice, 2020.)* [L0268.016]
- Follow up with a promised callback or email after a few days. [L0853.012, L1094.012]
- Follow up on the agreed date and log the result. [L0928.001]
- Trade account follow-up procedure: if the brand does not respond, follow up every few days; if it rejected you, follow up when the stated condition is met. *(Older advice, 2020.)* [L0468.033]
- Note the sources: the 2-3 day cycle (L0268.016) and "every few days" (L0468.033) give the same rhythm. The contact plan tool (L0126.008) sets the schedule within seven days.

**Onboarding after approval**

1. Call the supplier and ask to be approved. [L0671.032, L0671.171]
2. Once approved, request their product CSV or price list. [L0671.032, L0671.171]
3. Upload their products to the store the same day or the next day. [L0671.032, L0671.171]
4. Keep calling new suppliers every day. [L0671.032, L0671.171]

*(Older advice, 2021. Both ids describe the same procedure.)*

## Trade Shows, Meetings and Territory

**Trade show approach (2025)**

1. Remove the password from the store before the event so suppliers can see it live. [L1097.015]
2. Print about 200 business cards with your logo, name, store email and cell number (Vistaprint is one option). [L1097.015]
3. At each booth, introduce yourself and your company, and say you want to sell the brand's products online. [L1097.015]
4. Ask whether they work with online retailers and who to speak to if they do not. [L1097.015]
5. Collect each contact's business card. [L1097.015]
6. That evening, email each contact a short note asking them to get in touch once they are back home. [L1097.015]
7. If there is no reply after about a week, send one more follow-up. [L1097.015]

**Territory (older advice, 2019)**

- When suppliers ask "What is your territory?", they are routing you to the correct regional sales representative. Simply state your location (city and state or geographic region). They will assign you to the territory rep responsible for your area. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0243.046]

**Documentation**

- Document every conversation: the person's name, the date and all details discussed. Even if declined, the supplier will remember the call, which positions you for future contact. [L0133.015]
- Record each answer, the reason for any rejection, and the date to follow up. [L0468.033]

### Phone-first outreach sequence

- Build a supplier list first, then contact each supplier by phone and by email. Once a supplier approves, upload its products to your Shopify store so customers can see them. In theory this is simple; the difficulty is carrying out the calls and emails. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L1248.012]
- Follow this order for each product in the target country:
  1. Find the suppliers of the product in the target country.
  2. Call each supplier and ask for approval to retail their products through your website.
  3. After approval, list the products on the store. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L1481.016]
- If calling feels uncomfortable, start with brands you care less about. This builds confidence before you call the brands that matter most. (Older advice, 2022.) [L1113.015]

### Your position with suppliers

- Approach suppliers as a mutually beneficial business opportunity, not as someone asking for a favor or begging for approval to sell their products. Both sides can make money from the arrangement. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L1248.003]
- Suppliers benefit because you bring them sales and improve their cash flow. This win-win basis is what makes the relationship work; it is not a favor you ask for. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L1480.013]
- Suppliers do not have to pay to have their products listed on your website. You handle marketing, customer service and payment, and you pay them only for the products you order. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L1248.004]
- Suppliers already prepare and ship orders for their existing retail clients, so dropshipping orders add little new work for them. Their risk is minimal. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L1248.005]
- In the worst case you sell nothing for a supplier, which leaves their net result at zero. They cannot lose money from the relationship, but they can gain. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L1248.006]
- Suppliers compete with other manufacturers and distributors for the most prominent placement on retailer websites. This is why they want their products sold through a new store. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L1248.007]
- Suppliers judge a seller by professionalism and business seriousness, not by where the seller lives. A non-US seller who holds a US reseller permit and an EIN can approach US suppliers. Follow the call script closely and stay calm. (Older advice, 2019.) [L1110.002]

### Preparing before the call

- Fix your website launch date before you contact any supplier, so you can give it without hesitation. (2024.) [L1115.005]
- Do not reveal your exact location in early conversations. In traditional industries this can reduce trust or cause resentment. Set privacy settings so suppliers who connect with you on Facebook cannot see personal photos, such as work from the beach. (2024.) [L1441.006]
- Prepare your own traffic ideas and a basic business plan. Suppliers need to believe you will add sales that require little work from them. (2024.) [L1422.016]
- Prepare a short explanation of how you will add sales to the brand and how you will provide excellent customer service. Brands approve a reseller when it can show both. (Older advice, 2021.) [L1279.019]

### Presenting your business

- Suppliers unfamiliar with dropshipping need a pitch that explains the benefits and how the model works for them. Covering that education is a key part of getting them on board. (Older advice, 2018.) [L1274.038]
- Present your business in this order (older advice, 2019):
  1. Show the supplier the value you will bring to their brand.
  2. Explain how you will market their products.
  3. Describe your business.
  4. Explain why you want to work with this specific supplier.

  [L1275.020]
- Explain that you run a long-term business with customer support as your top priority, and that you are not a short-term or fly-by-night operation. (2024.) [L1422.013]
- Approval depends on the supplier believing you will benefit its brand. Suppliers mostly want to know that you will look after customers and run a real business. (2023.) [L1283.005]

### Scripts and email templates

**First message to a supplier.** Keep it short, say you want a long-term partnership, and ask concrete questions. Questions on dropshipping, delivery time to each target market, shipping fee and method, and custom packaging or logo options let you qualify a supplier quickly before deeper negotiation. (Older advice, 2024 source.) [L1353.002]

```
Hi, I'm [YOUR NAME], a seasoned dropshipper. I'm already selling this product and want to find a supplier for a lasting partnership. If that interests you, could you help me with a few questions?

1. Do you provide dropshipping?
2. What is the typical delivery time to the USA, Canada, Australia, New Zealand, and Europe?
3. What would shipping cost, and which carriers or methods would you use?
4. Can I add custom packaging and my own logo to the product?

Thanks in advance. I look forward to hearing from you.
```
[L1353.001]

- Use the sentence "I'm already selling this product" only if it is true. (2024 source; honesty principle.) [L1283.016, L1111.050]

**Shipping and fulfilment offer.** Use this to offer the supplier a choice of how orders are shipped.

```
Hi [Supplier name], we work in a few different ways depending on what suits you best. If you prefer, we can take care of shipping: your team picks and packs the order and has it ready for dispatch, and we arrange our courier to collect it, and our virtual assistant can be trained on the exact steps. If you would rather ship it yourselves, that works too, and we are happy to go with whichever option is easier for you. We have had strong demand for your [product or brand] and have turned down some sales recently, so if you decide to work with us, let us know and we can set up the account.
```
[L1441.014]

- Only include the demand claim if it is true. Do not overstate the store's performance. [L1111.050]

**Monthly sales before the store is live.** Send this as written after filling in the placeholders. Do not invent sales figures. (Older advice, 2018.)

```
Hi [Supplier name], our store is still being built and goes live on [launch date]. We do not have sales history yet, so I cannot give you reliable figures today. Once your products are live and traffic is running, I will share real numbers with you. If you need an estimate for planning, our goal is [target amount] per month for your brand, but the actual result will depend on traffic and listing performance.
```
[L1274.039]

**Company name and EIN before LLC paperwork is finished.** Fill in the placeholders before sending. (Older advice, 2018.)

```
Hi [Supplier name], I am forming a new company for this business, and the paperwork should be complete in about [number] weeks. Could you send me the product files now so I can start preparing the uploads? I [have / will have] my EIN [and the date it was issued, if you have it].
```
[L1274.040]

### Answering supplier questions

**Yearly purchase budget.** Do not give a fixed number. Explain that your budget is effectively unlimited as long as products convert at a 10x ROAS, and that spending starts at a small daily test budget. (2024.) [L1115.003]

**Monthly sales estimate before products are live.** A usable answer is a rough target, such as $10,000 per month for the brand. Be clear that there are no real visitor data yet and that the actual result depends on traffic and listing performance. Never state zero sales as if it were a real figure. (2018.) [L1274.085]

- Use the wording in the monthly sales template above. [L1274.039]

**Website launch date.** Give the date fixed in advance, without hesitation. [L1115.005]

**Shipping and warehousing.** Ask for shipping on the supplier's account if possible. Ship on your own account only if the supplier requires it. For warehousing, ask the supplier to stock the products, provide inventory reports, and ship on its account. (2024.) [L1115.004]

| Option | Use when | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Supplier ships on its own account | Your first request, if possible | L1115.004 |
| You ship on your own account | Only if the supplier requires it | L1115.004 |
| Supplier stocks, reports inventory and ships on its account | Your warehousing request | L1115.004 |
| You offer to take care of shipping (supplier picks, packs, and your courier collects), or the supplier ships itself | An offer in the reply template; the supplier chooses | L1441.014 |

- The two sources fit together: ask for the supplier's account first, and offer the courier option when the supplier prefers it or requires it. [L1115.004, L1441.014]

**Traffic.** Have your own traffic ideas ready, because suppliers need to see the extra sales you will bring. [L1422.016]

**Company name and EIN.** Use the template above if your LLC paperwork is not finished. [L1274.040]

### Application forms and terms

- When a supplier application asks what collaboration type you want, choose retailer, because you sell as a retailer on your own website. If asked about other sales platforms or physical stores, answer no when you only sell on your own website. (2024.) [L1115.097]
- You usually do not need to send your own terms. The supplier reviews the agreements it provides. (2024.) [L1115.097]

### What suppliers weigh

- Suppliers weigh approval by downside risk. A retailer that brings no sales costs them little time. A retailer that damages their brand is their worst outcome, so your pitch should show you will protect their reputation. (2022 source; the same point appears in two units.) [L1422.019, L1422.022]
- Suppliers are approving a future, not current results. Approvals are about the store's future potential and the niche, not current store results. (2020.) [L1111.050]

### Honest positioning

- State clearly during approval talks that you will never list a supplier's products below the MAP or price range it specifies. Suppliers need to believe this before they approve you. (2024 source.) [L1422.010]
- Present the plan honestly and do not overstate the store's performance. (2020.) [L1111.050]
- If the store is still in development and has only a few sales, describe it honestly as a store that is launching and plans to scale traffic and bring in the supplier's brand. Avoid sharing specific sales figures that would not impress a supplier. (2023.) [L1283.016]

## Follow-Up, Rejections and Re-Contact

### Tracking and records

- Record three things for every supplier: the date of first contact, the contact person's name, and detailed notes from each conversation with its outcome. This prevents accidental duplicate outreach, coordinates several supplier processes at once, and keeps follow-ups on time. [L0126.007]
- Keep a Master Supplier list that tracks all outreach and contact attempts. For each supplier, record the contact person's name, the conversation date, the details discussed, and the outcome. [L0133.008]
- Record the contact person and the date of first contact in your notes. A later follow-up can then address that person by name and refer to the earlier conversation. [L0938.015, L0936.015]
- Detailed notes matter because with many brands it is easy to forget what happened on an earlier call. Notes show exactly where you stand with each supplier. [L0936.016]
- Failing to log notes across a large list leads to repeated or inconsistent calls and emails. Record the outcome of every contact immediately. [L0936.017]
- Log every supplier response in the notes, including reasons for refusal and conditions such as "contact again in three months" or visitor and revenue thresholds. Schedule follow-ups from these notes instead of relying on memory. [L0938.007]
- Record every supplier response, including rejections, in a supplier tracking sheet and schedule a later follow-up date. [L0471.038]
- Log exact requirements, such as a monthly visitor minimum or a monthly sales level, together with a re-contact date such as 3-6 months. These notes decide when and how to follow up. [L0191.011]
- Typical conditions to record, and the trigger each one sets:
  - "contact again in 3-6 months" [L0191.012]
  - "requires 10,000 monthly visitors" [L0191.012]
  - "only works with stores doing $20,000+ per month" [L0191.012]
- Keep a supplier tracking spreadsheet with the exact date you must contact each supplier again. A supplier that is out of stock or asks you to come back later gets a dated entry, such as the end of Q3. [L0968.003]
- Use a supplier spreadsheet to track every supplier you contact, with a notes column for minimum requirements and the reason for each rejection. The spreadsheet from the Drop Ship Blueprint can serve as the starting template. [L0927.011]
- Record every supplier conversation with the contact person, the date, and the planned follow-up date. Circle back later, because a rejection in a new niche often means the supplier wants proof that you are a real store. [L1113.014]
- Track each conversation in a sheet with the date and what was said, and follow up within a set period. [L0803.027]
- Note the contact date, the person and the outcome in the supplier sourcing worksheet. [L0665.022]
- Record the requirements for each supplier next to its name in your supplier spreadsheet. [L0927.010]
- Record a refusal and its date in your supplier spreadsheet. [L0358.007]
- Record the stated reason for a rejection and the follow-up plan that goes with it. [L0835.014, L0468.034]
- Do not list an out-of-stock supplier's products yet, and record its status so you can return to it later. [L1090.024]

### First contact and the first follow-up

**Sequence for each supplier on the list**

1. Start with the first supplier on your list and work down one supplier at a time. [L0936.019]
2. Day 1: if the supplier has a phone number, call first. Ask for their application process and for the decision maker who handles new accounts. [L0936.019]
3. Day 1: if the supplier asks you to email during the call, send the day-one email from the contact plan. [L0936.019]
4. Day 2: if the day-one email gets no reply, send the follow-up email. It restates your earlier request, gives a near launch window, and asks for the application steps. [L0936.019]
5. Day 3: make another phone call. [L0936.019]
6. Days 4 to 7: continue the mixed calls and emails in the plan until you get a clear yes or no. [L0936.019]
7. Stop the sequence early once you reach the right person and get a clear answer. [L0936.019]

**Email or form requested by the supplier**

1. Send a short, simple email to the dealer or retailer contact address on the supplier's website. Introduce yourself and your store, and include a condensed version of your pitch on why they should work with you. [L0470.019]
2. If there is no reply by the next day, call in the morning. [L0470.019]
3. On the call, introduce yourself and the store, mention the email sent the day before, and say you want to confirm it arrived. [L0470.019]

Before ending the call, ask when you should expect a reply. Submit the email or form right after the call, and if nothing arrives within a couple of days, call the same contact again. [L0970.032]

- Ask: "Okay, perfect, I'll do that. Right after this call I'll send it. When should I expect a reply? Is that something you'll handle today, later this week, or at another time?" [L0970.033]

```
Okay, perfect, I'll do that. Right after this call I'll send it. When should I expect a reply? Is that something you'll handle today, later this week, or at another time?
```
[L0970.033]

- When following up after an email or form was requested, reference the date of the earlier conversation and the exact email or form you submitted. Confirm it arrived and ask where your application stands. [L0970.032]

```
Hi, this is [your name] from [your brand]. We spoke on [day of the week], and you asked me to [email [address] / fill out the form on your website]. I did that right after our call on [day]. I'm excited to continue the conversation and get things moving, and I just wanted to make sure it went through so you can follow up on your end. Could you let me know where things stand and whether you received it?
```
[L0970.034]

**Phone first, then email**

1. Call the supplier first, since a phone call reaches the right person and creates a first contact. [L0429.006]
2. Follow up by email afterward, because the supplier then expects the message. [L0429.006]
3. If there is still no reply, call weekly and record each follow-up in a supplier tracking spreadsheet. [L0429.006]

- Call the supplier the next day, in a friendly way, to confirm the application was received. Follow up sooner only if the supplier asked you to expect a longer wait. [L0835.012]
- Confirmation template for after an application:

```
Hi [Supplier contact name], this is [Your name] from [Store name]. I am following up on the application we sent on [date]. I just wanted to make sure you received it. Please let me know if you need anything else from us.
Thank you,
[Your name]
[Company name]
[Phone number]
```
[L0835.015]

- Application status check:

```
Hi [Supplier contact name], I just want to touch base and make sure my application was received. It does not need to be long or pushy. Could you let me know where it stands and what the next steps are? Thank you, [your name]
```
[L0247.025] *(older advice, 2021)*

- Confirmation of online dealer application:

```
I'm checking in to confirm you received my online application. We're very interested in becoming a reseller of your products.
```
[L0235.011] *(older advice, 2021-22, Source B)*

- Suppliers with online dealer applications expect the form to be filled out. If there is no reply after a couple of days, call to follow up. [L1112.049] *(older advice, 2021)*
- If a supplier does not reply within about two days after a promised follow-up, call or email again and stay in regular contact. [L0358.049] *(older advice, 2020)*
- Follow up with a supplier about three days after a call or email if you have not heard back. Keep the follow-up short, for example by confirming they saw your message. [L0941.018]
- If there is no response to an email or contact form, wait one day, then follow up. Suppliers often have no phone number listed. [L1277.039] *(older advice, 2020)*
- Email visibility: two to three days after the initial email, reply in the same thread. This creates two emails from you in the recipient's inbox on different days. [L0276.050]
- Do not sort or prioritize suppliers when reaching out. Contact all of them, keep notes on those that decline, and circle back later. [L0934.037]
- Start supplier outreach early, even before the store is ready. A supplier that is low on stock can still add you to a list for later. [L0803.027] *(older advice, 2022)*
- Starting outreach early in the autumn avoids the busy and slow periods described in the holiday section. [L0360.012]

**Warm leads first**

When suppliers say "email me" or "fill out our form", prioritize them, because a dialogue has started. [L0251.025]

Procedure for a warm-lead batch: [L0251.025] *(2023)*

1. Reach out to 20 or more suppliers as your minimum starting pool. [L0251.025]
2. Prioritize suppliers that said "email me" or "fill out our form". [L0251.025]
3. Follow up on these warm leads daily or every other day before contacting the remaining suppliers. [L0251.025]
4. Once a supplier sends approval documents, drop other priorities and complete the paperwork immediately. [L0251.025]
5. Upload their products and integrate them into your store. [L0251.025]
6. Return to contacting the remaining suppliers on your list. [L0251.025]

**Old onboarding forms**

Do not simply answer an old onboarding form or email that has sat unanswered for a long time. Contact that supplier again as if the relationship were new, using the channel you originally used (phone, contact form or email), because contacts and forms may have changed. [L0360.007] *(2024)*

### Follow-up intervals

**Which interval applies**

| Situation | Interval | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Starting interval for the first follow-up call | 2-3 weeks after the launch date you gave in the first call | [L0133.011] |
| Voicemail or initial contact, no answer | 2-3 business days (weekends not counted), 2019 | [L0243.027] *(older)* |
| General follow-up until a clear yes or no | Every 2-3 days, 2024 | [L0276.001] |
| Supplier list of 300 or more | Every 2-3 days with each supplier, 2024 | [L0276.049] |
| Peak periods such as Black Friday and Cyber Monday | Every 2-3 days, with a longer grace period during peak | [L0469.037] *(2021)* |
| Warm leads and active supplier pool | Daily or every other day, 2023 | [L0251.012, L0251.025] |
| Email or form requested by supplier, no reply | Next day by phone, 2023 | [L0470.019] |
| Call without an answer | Next business day, 2025 | [L0807.013] |
| Unresponsive supplier, minimum | At least once a week, 2019 | [L0357.025] *(older)* |
| Voicemail with callback request, no callback | Call again after one week, 2019 | [L1002.053] *(older)* |
| Weekly phone follow-up, recorded in tracking sheet | Weekly, 2022 | [L0429.006] *(older)* |
| After an unprepared rejection | About one month later | [L0927.006] |
| Supplier with 4-8 week approval timeline | Reminder exactly 4 weeks after first contact | [L0294.003] |
| Supplier that did not reply after several emails and voicemails | Wait a few days, for example until the next Monday, before a final follow-up | [L0972.100] |
| Declined supplier, no criteria given | Every 3 months, with an update on growth | [L0835.014, L0971.044] |
| Declined supplier, reconsidering | 3-6 months after going live, or after traffic and sales are in place | [L0470.021, L0358.007] *(2020)* |
| Declined supplier, periodic cycle | Every 3-6 months, with a note on the reason for rejection | [L1279.032] *(2021)* |
| Supplier-stated window | The date or window the supplier gave (3 months, 6 months, or a year) | [L0927.014, L0801.023] |

- Conflict on follow-up frequency: 2019 advice set a weekly minimum and a 2-3 business-day gap after voicemail [L0357.025, L0243.027] *(older)*. Newer advice (2023-2025) uses every 2-3 days, every other day in active warm-lead phases, or the next business day after a missed call [L0276.001, L0251.012, L0807.013]. Use the tighter schedule while a supplier is active, and the longer intervals once a supplier has declined.
- Follow up with suppliers every two to three days until you get a clear yes or no. Persistence shows you are serious, and busy suppliers forget you if they are not reminded. [L0276.001]
- With a list of 300 or more, keep following up every 2-3 days. Persistent follow-up is not spam when it is backed by genuine business intent. [L0276.049]
- Keep following up until you receive a yes or a no. A clear no is better than no information. [L0357.025] *(older)*
- Keep following up by phone or email, because suppliers are busy and repeated contact often gets a response. Do not stop after one attempt. [L0835.018] *(2023)*
- Follow-ups are normal and expected. Suppliers receive many calls and do not treat a second attempt as a nuisance. [L0927.006]
- If a supplier does not answer within two to three days, follow up by phone. [L0471.040] *(2024)*
- Follow up after a supplier's stated timeline, not before. A supplier that says approval takes 4-8 weeks gets its reminder at 4 weeks. [L0294.003]

**Gold-tier suppliers**

- Gold suppliers can take 3 months, 6 months, or more than a year to approve a store. Plan follow-ups on that timeline, while many other suppliers can be added immediately. [L0191.005]
- You can contact gold suppliers early, but expect "not yet" until you reach a certain traffic or sales level. Use the time to build the store and track results so you can reapply later. [L0486.015]

**Procedure: supplier who said approval takes 4-8 weeks**

1. Document the contact person's name, the contact date, and the timeline in your supplier tracking spreadsheet. [L0294.003]
2. Set a calendar reminder for exactly 4 weeks after initial contact. [L0294.003]
3. Follow up with the supplier on the reminder date. [L0294.003]
4. For email-only suppliers that have not responded after nearly 2 weeks of follow-ups every 2 days, stop following up on Fridays before long weekends, when staff take extended time off. [L0294.003]
5. Resume follow-ups on Wednesday or Thursday of the following week, once decision makers are back in the office. [L0294.003]

### Unresponsive suppliers

**Basic checks first**

- If no supplier responds, check the basics: that your email address receives mail, that your contact details on voicemails are correct, and that the store is not password-protected when you point suppliers to it. [L1002.056] *(older, 2019)*
- If suppliers do not respond despite outreach, follow up persistently and check whether the outreach strategy itself is failing before changing anything else. [L0969.015] *(older, 2022)*
- When many suppliers reject your outreach, the root cause is usually the approach: the wording of your message, the way you contact them, or the time of day you reach out. It is rarely the number of suppliers or the niche itself. [L1254.018]
- Switching niches or contacting many more suppliers after repeated rejections wastes time if you have not first diagnosed why suppliers say no. Review your message, contact method and timing first. [L1254.019]
- If hundreds of supplier applications produce only one approval, the application approach or the way suppliers are contacted is likely broken. Approval rates that low are not normal and must be investigated. [L0429.019] *(older, 2022)*

**Silence is not rejection**

- Suppliers are typically busy, and a lack of immediate response does not indicate rejection. Consistent follow-up through the entire 7-day schedule significantly increases approval rates. [L0126.006]
- Do not interpret silence as rejection. Assume the supplier is busy with other matters and keep following up every day or every other day by email or phone. [L0251.012] *(2023)*
- Treat a silent supplier as a busy schedule. Follow up every few days rather than assuming rejection. [L0468.034] *(older, 2020)*
- Suppliers that go quiet while approval is pending are often busy, not hostile. Follow up on the same channel first. [L0243.028] *(older, 2019)*
- When suppliers stop responding after initial interest, it may mean they were not confident in the store's viability or have internal issues. Following up after getting the first two or three approvals from other suppliers often works better, because the store appears more established. [L0255.015] *(2025)*

**Procedure: unresponsive supplier, phone and email**

1. Send a follow-up email to the unresponsive supplier. [L0255.008]
2. Find a phone number on the supplier's website or through a Google search. [L0255.008]
3. Call the phone number. [L0255.008]
4. If the individual contact is unresponsive, email the main support address (support@, sales@, or info@ at the supplier's domain). [L0255.008]
5. Continue with other suppliers while you wait. You may retry after getting the first two or three approvals. [L0255.008]

**Procedure: follow-up after silence (2019 routine)**

1. Call the supplier rather than relying only on email. [L1002.053] *(older)*
2. If the call goes to voicemail, leave a message asking for a callback. [L1002.053] *(older)*
3. If nobody calls back within a week, call again. [L1002.053] *(older)*
4. Keep following up with calls and voicemails until you get a clear yes or no. [L1002.053] *(older)*

**Procedure: call at different times of day**

If suppliers consistently do not answer during your usual calling time, try other times of day: early morning (8:30 AM), mid-morning (10 AM), afternoon (2 PM), and late afternoon (4 PM). Small supplier companies often have aligned schedules, scheduled meetings, or lunch breaks that follow their time zone or company culture. [L0251.059] *(2023)*

- Call suppliers on business days. If a supplier does not answer, note it and call again on the next business day. [L0807.013]

**Procedure: LinkedIn fallback**

1. After following up by email on Wednesday or Thursday and receiving no response by Friday, switch to LinkedIn as an alternative contact method. [L0294.007]
2. Search for the supplier company on LinkedIn. [L0294.007]
3. Identify employees of that company and review their job titles. [L0294.007]
4. If you do not have a LinkedIn account, create one and list yourself as owner of your company. [L0294.007]
5. Send a direct message to an identified employee using this text: [L0294.007]

```
Hi [Contact Name], I've been following up for a few weeks now, and we're really excited about listing for your brand. I haven't heard back yet and wanted to make sure my emails are getting through. Are you the right person to contact? If not, could you let me know who I should reach out to? Really appreciate your help, and thanks in advance.
```
[L0294.007]

6. Keep a friendly, professional tone throughout, because friendliness increases the likelihood of a positive response. [L0294.007]

**Procedure: unresponsive large supplier**

1. Before cutting ties, reply to an email from the contact person. Say that messages seem unanswered and ask whether someone else at the company can be contacted. [L0805.026] *(2024)*
2. Call the company again, ask to be connected to another person, and voice your concerns. [L0805.026]
3. If the new contact is just as unresponsive, stop selling that supplier's products. [L0805.026]

- A supplier whose sales contact does not answer emails or calls for weeks is a serious warning sign for later order processing. Move on, and revisit only after a long time, when the contact may have changed. [L0854.014] *(older, 2022)*
- If a supplier does not answer a sourcing request within a few days, escalate through the account manager on WhatsApp or the public community channel. Public escalation often gets a faster reply than a private message. [L0524.033] *(Source C)*
- If an order is delayed or a supplier does not respond, ask the supplier directly through its contact channels and keep your own record of the communication. Do not assume a supplier is reachable just because it is listed. [L0562.037] *(Source C)*
- Approval timing from suppliers varies widely, from about an hour to many days. If a supplier does not respond, follow up consistently, since slow responders are often less reliable suppliers. [L0960.025] *(older, 2019)*

**Out-of-stock suppliers**

- If a supplier is out of stock, it will usually tell you during outreach. Do not list its products yet. Work with suppliers that have stock, and circle back to the out-of-stock supplier after a few months. [L1090.024] *(older, 2021)*
- A supplier that is out of stock or pressed by many inquiries: record the contact, ask when to follow up, and record that time. [L0665.022] *(older, 2022)*

### Holiday and peak-season timing

**Windows as stated by different sources**

| Period | What the source says | Applies when | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Oct to first half of November | Contact suppliers during this period, when they have the most capacity to approve new retailers (2024) | Planning a Q4 launch | [L1115.009] |
| Until about Thanksgiving | Contact suppliers until about Thanksgiving; the rush to ship runs from Thanksgiving to Christmas; responses slow from mid-December to New Year (2021) | Q4 outreach that continues into November | [L1112.051] *(older)* |
| Second or third week of November | Approvals should be done by then; applications are put on hold until after the holidays (2023). Suppliers have no fixed cutoff, they simply get busier | Approval target before peak | [L1114.018] |
| Late November through the holiday rush | Avoid cold outreach; suppliers are busiest, replies and approvals slow, and a short lull can occur in early December. Resume calls in January (2022) | Cold outreach in this window | [L0803.097] *(older)* |
| Roughly two weeks before Black Friday | Best window for a Black Friday launch outreach; suppliers are often unresponsive from Black Friday to about a week after Cyber Monday (2025) | Black Friday launch | [L1097.018] |
| Christmas to year-end | Response times often slow because of vacations (2024); Black Friday through Christmas and New Year see slower replies (2025) | Any outreach in late year | [L0360.012, L0807.013] |

- Suppliers do not have a fixed cutoff for accepting new dropshippers before the holidays. They become busier as the season approaches, so applications are put on hold until after the holidays. [L1114.018] *(2023)*
- Suppliers may slow approvals during peak holiday sales weeks. Record every response and follow up at a later date. [L0471.038] *(2024)*
- Around holiday peaks, wait a few extra days before following up. [L0471.040] *(2024)*
- During peak periods such as Black Friday and Cyber Monday, allow a longer grace period and reach out again once the supplier's business is back to normal. [L0469.037] *(older, 2021)*
- Supplier responses slowed during holidays because staff were away and then had a backlog. Follow up with every supplier that did not respond over the holiday period, and treat the earlier contact as a fresh start. [L0801.024] *(older, 2021)*
- Supplier approvals tend to come faster in quiet periods with no holidays or peak season, so use those times to get as many approvals as possible. [L0961.029] *(older, 2020)*
- When supplier onboarding runs through a slow period such as summer, follow up with each supplier daily because staff may respond more slowly. A store can launch on a fixed date with only one approved supplier, if more suppliers are still being onboarded. [L1069.095] *(older, 2019)*
- In Q4, supplier approvals are slower but still possible. [L1112.051] *(older, 2021)*
- Colder periods do not justify dropping suppliers. Use the quiet season to build the list and then the holiday peak to upload approved brands. [L1115.009]

**Procedure: Q4 outreach timing (2024)**

1. Contact suppliers during October and the first half of November, when they have the most capacity to approve new retailers. [L1115.009]
2. Expect slower replies as Black Friday promotions begin, which often start as early as the third week of November. [L1115.009]
3. Keep a steady pace of approvals until about the first half of November, then focus on uploading approved brands to the store. [L1115.009]

**Procedure: holiday closure plan (2024)**

1. Send your final round of outreach about a week before the holiday break. [L0471.041]
2. Pause during the holiday week. [L0471.041]
3. Restart outreach after the break. [L0471.041]

**Procedure: holiday follow-up (2019 routine)**

1. Contact the supplier again through the same channel you used first. [L0467.035] *(older)*
2. Refer to the holiday season and say you want to pick the conversation back up now that the busy period is over. [L0467.035] *(older)*
3. Ask for, or send, the application or form they need. [L0467.035] *(older)*
4. Once a supplier responds positively, act immediately: send the documents, sign the MAP agreement, and upload the products to the site. [L0467.035] *(older)*

```
Hi [Supplier contact name], I hope you had a great [holiday season, e.g. Thanksgiving, Black Friday and Cyber Monday]. Now that the holidays are over and the busiest week has calmed down, I would love to send you our [application or the form you asked for]. Please let me know if there is anything else you need from me to move forward, and I will get the documents back to you quickly. Thank you, [Your name], [Store name].
```
[L0467.036] *(older, 2019)*

**Procedure: approval during the post-holiday lull (2022)**

1. Call during a normal week to reach the person who can approve you. [L0803.025] *(older)*
2. Ask them to send the approval sheet. [L0803.025] *(older)*
3. Return the sheet, get approved, upload their products, and then move to the next supplier. [L0803.025] *(older)*

**Event follow-up**

```
Subject: Following up from [EVENT NAME]

Hi [CONTACT NAME],

I know you met a lot of people at [EVENT NAME], so I will keep this short. It was great to meet you at your booth. I am [YOUR NAME] from [STORE NAME] ([STORE URL]), an online store, and I would love to sell [BRAND NAME] products on it.

Is an online retailer like us something you can speak about, or could you point me to the right person at your company?

Thank you,
[YOUR NAME]
[YOUR EMAIL]
[YOUR PHONE]
```
[L1097.016] *(2025)*

**Offline periods**

- If you will be offline for a stretch, do not start an outreach round you cannot follow up on. Use the offline time to build a complete supplier contact list, so you can start calling the moment you are back online. [L0941.107]

### Objections and declines

**"We don't work with new online stores"**

- Suppliers often use "we don't work with new online stores" as a standard rejection to test whether you are serious and trustworthy. Push back by asking which thresholds, such as traffic, Alexa score, or revenue, would qualify you for approval. [L0245.007] *(older, 2020)*
- Procedure when a supplier says it does not work with new online stores:
  1. Respond: "I totally understand. What would it take for us to get approved?" [L0245.008] *(older)*
  2. The supplier may specify traffic per month, an Alexa score above or below a number, or a minimum revenue. [L0245.008] *(older)*
  3. Work toward those metrics rather than accepting "no" as final. [L0245.008] *(older)*
- Alexa Score (alexa.com) ranks website popularity. Some suppliers require a minimum Alexa score to approve new retailers, so research and improve yours before approaching premium suppliers. [L0245.009] *(older, 2020)*
- If a supplier says it does not currently work with online retailers, do not drop the outreach. Use a pitch built around how dropshipping increases the supplier's sales, online reach and revenue. Present the arrangement as a benefit for both sides. [L0935.011]
- Supplier unfamiliarity with dropshipping was a bigger obstacle in the past than it is now, because e-commerce has matured. Only spend extra time explaining the model when a supplier actually does not know it. [L0935.012]

**Suppliers that say they are already working with a retailer, or are not taking new retailers**

- Still start the conversation. Explain how selling with you benefits them, and ask to follow up three to six months after going live, once you have traffic and results. [L0470.021] *(2023)*
- Many suppliers approve retailers only in a second round, after they see proof of the store's performance. Plan for a group of suppliers that will ask you to prove yourself first, and keep them on your follow-up list. [L0470.022] *(2023)*
- When a supplier declines, end the conversation politely with a clear follow-up plan, such as saying you will get back in touch after launch once you have traffic and sales. Ending on a hard no closes off a future approval, whereas a polite follow-up keeps it open. [L0470.023] *(2023)*
- If a supplier turns you down because the store is new, you may briefly mention relevant past business experience if the conversation leaves room for it, without giving detailed information. If the answer is a firm no, end politely and do not push further. [L0470.102] *(2023)*
- Suppliers that are not accepting new e-commerce accounts are not a dead end. Note the date they gave for a follow-up, and return with sales results or traffic data at that time, since results raise the chance of approval. [L0801.023] *(older, 2021)*
