- When a supplier says it will not take new dealers, ask when to follow up and record that time. [L0665.022] *(older, 2022)*

**Capacity-based rejection**

Example: "we are not taking on more internet retailers right now".

1. Do not accept the rejection passively with a polite goodbye, because the supplier will not reach out to you on its own. [L0927.007]
2. Present a launch strategy that shows what you are doing to start the store: which suppliers you already work with or are onboarding, which marketing channels you will use, and which promotions you will run. [L0927.007]
3. Use fear of missing out (FOMO) by showing that you are about to build a meaningful position in the market, so the supplier does not want to be left out of your growth. [L0927.007]
4. Use this approach only if your launch strategy is real and well researched. A supplier that senses a bluff will not be persuaded. [L0927.007]

**Standards-based rejection**

A supplier may reject you by saying your store does not meet its standards. Treat this as a signal to improve the store and your pitch before calling again, not as a final answer. [L0927.015]

**MAP policy decline**

```
Hi [Supplier name], thanks for reaching out. Right now we only work with suppliers that enforce their minimum advertised price, so we will not be moving forward with your products at this time. If your MAP policy is enforced across retailers, please let us know and we can revisit this with you.
```
[L0941.021]

**Responding during a follow-up call**

1. If they decline, ask what you can do to make it happen, and show genuine interest in their concerns. [L0133.016]
2. Highlight how this project is already benefiting your company, and explain the growth potential of adding their product line. [L0133.016]
3. If they continue declining, ask for the real underlying reason, and state that you genuinely want to understand their hesitation. [L0133.016]
4. On your next follow-up call, address their concerns with specific solutions or workarounds. [L0133.016]

```
I totally understand where you're coming from. Here's what I want to make sure you're thinking about though: we're putting together something really significant in your product category, and your brands would be perfect for our customer base. If you decide to pass, you're going to be missing out on what could be really meaningful order volume coming your way.

I genuinely don't want us to lose this opportunity because of process issues or timing. Who do we need to get involved to make this partnership happen? Is there a different decision-maker, or is there a specific approval process we should follow?
```
[L0133.022] *(2024 Source A, undated template)*

- Expect rejections from high-ticket dropshipping suppliers, especially domestic manufacturers or distributors. Treat them as a routine part of outreach, not a judgment of you personally. Suppliers often refuse new online retailers for reasons unrelated to your store's quality, such as capacity or company policy. [L0927.001]
- Some suppliers refuse at first, and that is part of the normal process. Work through the list and keep following up. [L0469.033] *(older, 2021)*
- Some individuals cannot be reasoned with, regardless of your professionalism or preparation. If a supplier unfairly rejects you despite clear, professional communication (for example, dismissing a demo site as a "scam site" after you explained its launch timeline), continue with other suppliers and do not take it personally. This reflects their attitude, not your business model's viability. [L0294.006]
- When a supplier refuses a new or inexperienced dropshipper, accept it as normal and do not force the issue. [L0358.007] *(older, 2020)*
- Procedure for refusal by a new dropshipper:
  1. Record the refusal and the date in your supplier spreadsheet. [L0358.007] *(older)*
  2. Once you have traffic and sales from other brands, reapproach the supplier, which may take three to six months. [L0358.007] *(older)*
  3. Show your results and request approval again. [L0358.007] *(older)*
- Early refusals from brands to a brand-new store are normal. The minimum of 20 suppliers provides enough chances for approvals. [L0971.045]
- Handling a refusal by an online-only reseller: [L1116.037] *(2025)*
  1. Ask what would change their mind, such as a traffic level, a sales level, or a business history requirement. [L1116.037]
  2. Record the answer as a note on the supplier tracking sheet. [L1116.037]
  3. Ask whether you may follow up after a set period, for example three months after launch, to share results and check whether their policy has changed. [L1116.037]
  - Treat the refusal as "not yet" rather than "no". [L1116.037]
- Newer stores are often rejected or told to return once they have more traffic or more time in business. Keep applying to other suppliers, because approval rates vary and early rejection is normal. [L1094.009] *(2023)*
- A supplier may postpone approval until a new store reaches a set level of sales or traffic. This is a normal outcome. Ask for the exact threshold and keep pursuing approvals with other suppliers in the meantime. [L0359.115]
- Some suppliers approve only after the store has sales, traffic and business history. Follow those up later rather than dropping them, and ask for the reason for each rejection. [L0967.018] *(older, 2020)*
- Suppliers that decline early often approve later, once the store shows more traffic or revenue. Re-contact them with proof. [L0966.020] *(2025)*

### Reasons for rejection and the next step

**Next step by reason (2020 routine)**

1. If the supplier is busy (for example during the holiday season), call back after that period. [L0664.011] *(older)*
2. If the supplier requires proof of revenue or traffic, follow up once you reach those thresholds. [L0664.011] *(older)*
3. If the supplier says it is not opening new accounts, follow up every month, ask for another contact, restate your interest, and repeat the original pitch. [L0664.011] *(older)*
4. Keep detailed notes of every supplier conversation, so the follow-up is well timed and consistent. [L0664.011] *(older)*

**Minimum requirements**

- A common reason for rejection is a minimum requirement, such as an Alexa score above 100,000, or around $20,000 in expected monthly business. A new store usually cannot meet these, and a large company will rarely make exceptions, however well you pitch. [L0927.009]
- Some suppliers only authorize stores with at least 10,000 monthly visitors. Others require at least 20,000 euros in monthly revenue. Record these requirements in the supplier notes, so you can follow up when the store reaches them. [L0938.005]
- Reference point from 2025: about 20,000 monthly visitors and around 200,000 USD monthly revenue as proof when re-contacting. [L0966.020]
- If a supplier requires a minimum Alexa ranking under 100,000 or a marketing plan, note the requirement, respond that the threshold will be met, and reapply once the store reaches that traffic level. [L0967.027] *(older, 2020)*
- Some suppliers approve only at a set level of sales or traffic. For example, the approval threshold can be 10,000 monthly visitors or $50,000 in revenue. [L0278.024] *(2025)*
- A supplier that imposes minimums can still become a customer later. Mark it as a follow-up target and return once you have proof of the required numbers, rather than dropping it. [L0927.012]

**Procedure: handling minimum requirements**

1. Ask each supplier that gives a minimum requirement exactly what it needs: analytics access, screenshots, Alexa score, or monthly sales volume. [L0927.010]
2. Record these requirements in your supplier spreadsheet next to that supplier's name. [L0927.010]
3. Keep working on the other suppliers on your list while you build toward the stated numbers. [L0927.010]
4. When you reach the required numbers, contact the same person you spoke to before, remind them of the earlier conversation, send proof such as screenshots, and ask who to speak with about setting up your account. [L0927.010]

**Procedure: convert a rejection into an approval (2025)**

1. Prepare a list of 20 or more brand suppliers during niche selection, to account for rejections. [L0278.024]
2. When a supplier says no or claims it does not work with online stores, understand that this means it lacks trust in the store yet. [L0278.024]
3. Ask: "What would it take for us to eventually sell for you? Do you require a certain sales volume or monthly traffic?" [L0278.024]
4. Record the supplier's specific approval threshold, for example 10,000 monthly visitors or $50,000 in revenue. [L0278.024]
5. When that threshold is reached, follow up with the original contact person, referencing the previous conversation and the new metrics. [L0278.024]
6. Use this systematic follow-up to convert rejections into approvals. [L0278.024]

**Procedure: handling a first rejection caused by being unprepared**

1. Note the supplier in your supplier list, along with the reason for the rejection. [L0927.006]
2. Prepare properly, including research and a clear pitch. [L0927.006]
3. Call again roughly one month later. [L0927.006]

**Procedure: refusal with no reason given (2024)**

1. Note the reason for the refusal and when to follow up. [L0971.044]
2. Keep working the rest of the supplier list. [L0971.044]
3. Follow up after three months, and again three months later if the supplier still refuses. The goal is approval from every supplier. [L0971.044]

**Rejection ratios**

Different sources give different expectations. Use them as planning ranges, not targets.

| Source | Expectation | Source id |
|---|---|---|
| Undated (typical first-contact result) | Around 10 refusals out of 20 on day one; approvals come through repeated follow-up | [L0935.006] |
| 2022, first store, high-ticket brands found through US online retailers | Roughly 25 to 35 approvals out of 50 approached; the rest reject for now, wanting proof of traffic and track record | [L1113.013] *(older)* |
| 2025, new store | About 50 percent of brands may approve early; 20 contacted might yield about 10 approvals | [L0966.017] |
| 2022 | Hundreds of applications with one approval means the approach is broken; investigate | [L0429.019] *(older)* |

- Many suppliers reject at first contact, so a list of 20 will typically shrink on day one. Approvals come over time through repeated follow-up, so keep contacting suppliers rather than settling early. [L0935.006]
- A rejection in a new niche often just means the supplier wants to see proof that you are a real store. [L1113.014] *(older, 2022)*
- Most supplier rejections come from too little follow-up, not from the supplier's answer. Keep following up until a supplier gives a clear yes or no, or ask it to recontact you once you reach a set traffic or sales level. [L0808.019] *(2026)*
- A supplier may reject you by saying your store does not meet its standards. Improve the store and the pitch before calling again. [L0927.015]
- A supplier may name a window to reconsider a new retailer: three months, six months, or a year. Record it and schedule the follow-up for that date instead of leaving the supplier without a next step. [L0927.014]
- The first goal in outreach is approval from the first five suppliers. The real aim is approval from every supplier on the master list, including the gold-tier brands. [L0936.001]

### Recontacting after growth

**When to recontact**

- Suppliers who turned you down earlier can be contacted again once the store is live, real products are sold, other brands have approved you, and traffic systems are running. Name current supplier relationships when it fits the conversation. [L0191.010]
- Recontact timing differs by source. Use the number the supplier gave you first:
  - Supplier-stated window: follow up on that date [L0927.014, L0801.023]
  - 3-6 months after going live, once you have traffic and results [L0470.021] *(2023)*
  - Every 3 months after a refusal with no criteria, with an update on growth [L0835.014] *(2023)*
  - Every 3-6 months, with the reason noted and concrete progress shared [L1279.032] *(older, 2021)*
  - 3 months after refusal, then again 3 months later if still refused [L0971.044] *(2024)*
- Track each declined supplier with its reason and a dated follow-up. Each rejection has a re-contact date such as 3-6 months. [L0191.011]

**Procedure: recontact a supplier that declined the store (2020 routine)**

Procedure for a supplier that refuses a new dealer: [L0358.007] *(older)*

1. Accept the refusal as normal. [L0358.007]
2. Record the refusal and date in your supplier spreadsheet. [L0358.007]
3. Once you have traffic and sales from other brands, reapproach the supplier, which may take three to six months. [L0358.007]
4. Show your results and request approval again. [L0358.007]

**Procedure: reapproach an out-of-stock or busy supplier (2022)**

1. Note the contact date, person and outcome in the supplier sourcing worksheet. [L0665.022]
2. When they say they are not taking new dealers, ask when to follow up and record that time. [L0665.022]
3. On follow-up, call the same contact and state how the store launched, its sales progress, and the brands already sold, plus your desire to add them. [L0665.022]

**Follow-up call after launch (about 2-3 weeks after the date given)**

1. Plan the follow-up call for 2-3 weeks after your stated launch date. [L0133.016]
2. Call and attempt to reach the same person from your initial call, using their name to remind them of your conversation. [L0133.016]
3. Ask how their business has been, and bring up anything important from the first conversation. [L0133.016]
4. Tell them your launch exceeded your team's expectations and you are even more excited about the project. [L0133.016]
5. Mention that you wanted to call sooner but needed time to smooth out internal systems and processes. [L0133.016]
6. Share that your initial results beat your projections. [L0133.016]
7. Add that all your other supplier partners are equally thrilled with their product performance. [L0133.016]
8. Inform them you are scaling up your advertising campaigns significantly. [L0133.016]
9. Ask directly if they are ready to partner with you now. [L0133.016]
10. If they decline, ask what you can do to make it happen, and show genuine interest in their concerns. [L0133.016]
11. Highlight how this project is already benefiting your company, and explain the growth potential of adding their product line. [L0133.016]
12. If they continue declining, ask for the real underlying reason, and state that you genuinely want to understand their hesitation. [L0133.016]
13. On your next follow-up call, address their concerns with specific solutions or workarounds. [L0133.016]

```
Hi [Contact Name], this is [Your Name] from [Company Name]-we talked a few weeks ago about us launching [website.com] in the [niche] market.

I wanted to give you an update because things have developed really well. Our launch actually exceeded what we predicted, and the team is more enthusiastic than ever about what we're building here.

I wanted to reach out sooner but it's been intense getting all our internal systems and processes dialed in properly-that's why I'm calling now.

I'm glad I waited because I can tell you our actual results are significantly outperforming what we projected. Beyond that, all of our current supplier partners are seeing strong traction with their product lines on our site.

We're gearing up to substantially increase our advertising and marketing investments. I really think now is the time for us to move forward together. Are you ready to become a partner?
```
[L0133.021]

**Follow-up message for a declined supplier, once the store is live**

Send by email or messaging once the store is live with products and traffic.

```
Hi [contact first name],

This is [your name] from [store name] ([store website]). We spoke on [date of last contact], and at that time you mentioned you were not ready to work with us yet, which I completely understand.

Since then we have started selling products from [brand name 1], [brand name 2], and [brand name 3], and our traffic systems are now fully set up. I would really love to add your products to our store, and I believe our customers would love them.

Is this a good time to restart the process of becoming an authorized retailer for [brand name]? If there are any requirements we should meet first, such as a minimum number of monthly visitors or monthly revenue, please let me know where we currently stand.

Thank you, and I look forward to hearing from you.

Best regards,
[your name]
```
[L0938.012]

- Follow-up with declined suppliers is an ongoing process, not a one-time task. Reference the previous conversation, name the brands you already sell, and ask what must happen before they can authorize you. [L0938.013]
- If a gold supplier turns down a new store for lack of traffic or sales, accept it, tell them you will reconnect once the business is established, and move on to silver suppliers in the same niche. [L1085.033] *(2018)*
- Suppliers that decline a new store can be approached again with related niches. Get the first five to ten brands live, then return to the target suppliers, since live brands add credibility. [L0667.060] *(2025)*
- Build credibility with the first live brands before going back: [L0969.015, L0667.060] *(older, 2022)*, expand the supplier list through trade show networks and consider a broader niche if outreach keeps failing.
- Supplier follow-ups should happen every three to six months, with a note on the reason for rejection. Return with concrete progress, such as traffic growth, new channels, or email list size. [L1279.032] *(older, 2021)*
- A supplier that is not accepting new accounts: note the date, return with sales results or traffic data at that time. [L0801.023] *(older, 2021)*
- Proof of growth that raises approval chances includes sales results, traffic data, and the number of brands already live. [L0801.023, L1279.032]

### Restarting with former suppliers

- When restarting with former approved suppliers, check for new products and price changes first, since approved accounts usually receive those updates by email. A separate "I'm back" message is often unnecessary. [L0804.002] *(2023)*
- Restart template:

```
Subject: Current inventory, products and pricing

Hi [Supplier contact name],

I hope the past couple of years have been good for you and your team. The last time we spoke, inventory was a big issue on your side, so I'd like to get up to date. Could you send me your current inventory list, your current product offerings and your current pricing?

I'm excited about working together this year.

Thank you,
[Your name]
[Store name]
[Phone] | [Email]
```
[L0804.003] *(2023)*

- Old partner records: suppliers with an old onboarding form that sat unanswered should be contacted as new relationships, through the original channel. [L0360.007]

### Ongoing outreach routine

**Aim and pacing**

- The first goal is the first five suppliers to approve you. The real aim is approval from every supplier on your master list, including the gold-tier brands. [L0936.001]
- Do not sort or prioritize suppliers when reaching out. Contact all of them. [L0934.037]
- Upload a supplier's products to your store as soon as that supplier approves you, then return to outreach for the next supplier. Alternate between outreach and product uploads until every supplier on the list has been worked through. [L0936.002]
- When a supplier approves you, mark the lesson complete and move to the product upload lesson. Upload that supplier's live products to your store, then return to the contact plan and continue working down the list. [L0936.018]
- Approval rates differ by niche and store maturity; expect a partial approval rate with a new store. [L0966.017]
- Repeated contact with suppliers is part of the routine. Do not stop after one attempt. [L0835.018]

**Tools and planning**

- Use the Supplier Contact Plan tool to build a day-by-day outreach plan for each supplier. [L0808.018]
  1. Log in to the members area. [L0808.018]
  2. Enter your name, company name (including LLC or corporation if applicable), store URL, phone number, and business email. [L0808.018]
  3. Enter the supplier's details from your supplier tracking sheet. [L0808.018]
  4. Click "Generate my contact plan". [L0808.018]
  5. Download the plan with calls and email follow-ups for each supplier. [L0808.018]
- Before a Black Friday launch, plan supplier outreach for the roughly two weeks before Black Friday. [L1097.018] *(2025)*
- Plan supplier outreach around holiday closures using the closure plan in the holiday section. [L0471.041]
- After submitting a supplier application, call the next day in a friendly way to confirm receipt. [L0835.012] *(2023)*
- Supplier approvals: call during normal business weeks, since the decision maker is available then. [L0803.025] *(older, 2022)*
- Keep the tracking spreadsheet and contact plan in sync so each supplier has a next date. [L0968.003] *(older, 2021)*
- Use the supplier list to decide who you call next; follow up each supplier on its own schedule. [L0936.019]

**Routine sequence for each day**

1. Check the tracking sheet for each supplier's next date. [L0126.007, L0968.003]
2. Make the calls and send the emails scheduled for today, following the contact plan. [L0808.018, L0936.019]
3. Record every response and set the next follow-up date. [L0936.017, L0938.007]
4. If a supplier approves you, upload its products, mark the lesson complete, then return to the list. [L0936.018, L0936.002]
5. Follow up on warm leads before contacting the rest of the list. [L0251.025]
6. Keep the Master Supplier list current so no supplier falls out of the rotation. [L0133.008]

**Supplier lists and the offline period**

- Expect slower replies around Black Friday through Christmas and New Year, when many suppliers close for a break. [L0807.013] *(2025)*
- Build a complete supplier contact list while offline, so you can start calling the moment you are back online. [L0941.107]

### Follow-up cadence

- Follow up at a steady pace until the supplier gives a clear answer, rather than treating a single unanswered message as the end. [L1287.016]
- Supplier relationship calls should not be outsourced, because relationships are central to this business. If time is short, send emails during the day with a scheduling tool such as the Boomerang Gmail extension so they arrive at a business-hours time. (Older advice, 2021.) [L1279.034]
- Use this template to chase an application that is still pending (2024):

```
Hi, this is [Your name] from [Store name]. We spoke last week and you asked me to send an email, which I sent to the attention of [Contact name]. I have not heard back yet. Could you check with [Contact name] that it was received and let me know the status of our application?
```
[L1285.023]

### Unresponsive suppliers

- A supplier can go silent after an account setup or order, which is a form of ghosting. If a supplier does not respond, escalate to their sales or distribution contact early. [L1472.031]
- If a supplier stops answering after being responsive, keep following up instead of giving up. Silence often comes from busy periods, such as holiday weekends or back-to-school season. (2025.) [L1287.016]

### Holiday and seasonal timing

- Silence during holiday weekends and back-to-school season is often caused by busy periods, not by a refusal, so keep following up rather than closing the lead. [L1287.016]

### Objections and declines

- Expect that not every supplier will approve a new reseller account, so contact several suppliers in parallel. In one case, one supplier approved within two minutes while the next one refused. A refusal alone is not a reason to stop. [L1437.007]
- If one supplier declines, tell competing suppliers that demand for their products is high and that you have turned down sales for their brand. Once you have one supplier, word of mouth in the industry, especially in niche markets, often brings in other suppliers who reach out to you. [L1441.013]

### Paused supply and reasons for rejection

- When a supplier pauses new retailers because of inventory or supply chain problems, work through this procedure (older advice, 2021):
  1. Ask the supplier when its inventory is expected to stabilize and record the answer in your tracking sheet.
  2. Follow up on that date, or at the interval the supplier gives.
  3. Meanwhile, source backup suppliers from a trade show directory such as TSNN.com. [L1279.098]

### Re-contacting after growth

- A "we are not taking new retailers right now" answer usually means the supplier wants more authority, traffic, sales and a track record first. Return later with a live store and history, and ask again. (2023.) [L1283.006]
- Use this template when reopening a conversation that was declined earlier (older advice, 2021):

```
Hi [supplier contact name], this is [your name] from [store name]. We spoke on [date of last contact] and you mentioned you were not taking on new online retailers at that time. Since then we have [specific progress, e.g. grown traffic by X percent, added channel Y, reached Z email subscribers]. We are still very excited to list your products and would love to reopen the conversation. When would be a good time to talk, or when do you expect to take on new retailers?
```
[L1279.033]

### Tracking and ongoing outreach routine

- Record every supplier answer, including any expected restock or reopening date, in a tracking sheet, because the paused-supply procedure and the follow-up dates depend on it. (Older advice, 2021.) [L1279.098]
- Keep supplier outreach as a recurring routine: follow up on the recorded dates, escalate silent contacts early, and keep parallel contacts open. [L1472.031, L1437.007, L1279.098]

## Approval, Agreements and Account Setup

### Approval, Forms and First Contact

- Expect the supplier's account manager to reply within a few days after you submit the application forms, with approval, an account number and contact details, or a request for more information. [L0133.012]
- Delaying the return of supplier forms significantly lowers your approval rate, so complete and submit them as soon as they arrive. [L0133.013]
- (older, 2022) Fill out supplier authorized-retailer forms as soon as they arrive and return them right away, so products can go live without downtime. [L1092.038]
- Letting an application or product information sit unanswered slows the whole sourcing process. Return applications and upload product data as fast as possible so you do not lose time between suppliers. [L0495.004]
- A dealer or retailer application on a supplier's website is the same process as the formal approval paperwork, so fill it out the same way. [L1111.178]
- (older, 2021-22) A brand's dealer application typically asks for first and last name, email, phone number and a business street address, and a sales representative then calls back. Use the same details and phone number you plan to use when you call the supplier. [L0932.018]
- (older, 2020) To find the right person:
  1. Call the supplier's main contact numbers and ask who handles retailer approvals.
  2. Submit the approval paperwork.
  3. Ask whether they ship on your account or their own. [L1111.028]
- Approval reveals the supplier's actual price list, which shows your real margin and what competitors earn per sale, so you can skip any product with weak margins. Getting approved costs nothing and does not oblige you to list any product. [L1146.002]
- Some suppliers do not approve a new store immediately. Gold suppliers commonly reject or ask to re-review once a store has more products. Application fields asking for Facebook or Instagram accounts can be left blank until those accounts exist. [L0969.007]
- (older, 2020) You can contact suppliers and get approved while your business bank account or DBA is still pending. You can request price lists and CSVs and prepare the site, and simply hold orders until the account is ready. [L0961.028]
- (older, 2020) If a supplier that owns several brands approves the account, get approved with all of its brands rather than just the first one. [L0967.026]
- (older, 2019) If you have not been approved by a brand yet:
  1. Contact the brand and go through its normal approval process.
  2. Once approved, request its price lists.
  3. Compare the wholesale price, the retail price and the shipping cost for each item.
  4. Approval does not require listing every product the brand sells, so list only the items whose margin works. [L0357.066]
- (older, 2018) Some approved suppliers do not require a contract and only send specifications, prices and photos. This is enough to start, and products should be uploaded and traffic set up as soon as possible. [L0466.023]
- (older, 2021-22) Secure supplier approval before listing their products on your store, because suppliers must agree to let you sell their products through your website. [L0912.008]
- (older, 2019) Approval steps once a supplier moves toward approval:
  1. Stop other tasks and prioritize the supplier while the conversation is moving forward.
  2. Send the documentation the supplier requests and sign the MAP agreement.
  3. Get approved, then receive the product CSV and pricing sheets.
  4. Check that your selling price can be competitive and still profitable against the MSRP and MAP.
  5. Upload the supplier's products to the store. [L0467.077]
- Approval order (2025):
  1. After approval, the supplier sends a simple agreement to sign and return.
  2. After they acknowledge the signed agreement, they send product information.
  3. If product information does not arrive, follow up with every approved supplier and state your launch date. [L0972.007]
- Once authorized as a retailer, repeat this cycle for as long as you keep adding brands:
  1. When a supplier agrees to work with you, immediately send the authorized retailer application form it requests.
  2. Once authorized, upload that supplier's products to your store.
  3. Return to your master supplier list and contact the next brand.
  4. Repeat getting approved, uploading products and moving to the next supplier. [L0938.006]

### Product Data and First Uploads After Approval

- (older, 2018) After approval, ask the supplier for its top three selling products and how many units of each it sells per month. The monthly unit count gives you a baseline for how much of that demand you can capture. [L1274.037]
- (older, 2022) When a supplier approves you:
  1. Ask for the top three bestselling products and write them down, since these are the priority for early work and conversion optimization.
  2. Ask how many units of each sell per month.
  3. Upload the approved products, then move to the next supplier. [L0803.024]
- If a supplier agreement requires you to upload the whole catalog, ask the supplier by email to confirm that you only plan to list selected products before you rely on that. [L1113.023]
- Upload only the products you want. You do not need to upload everything. [L0253.024]
- (older, 2021) If a supplier requires a product purchase or deposit for a product list, a common workaround is to ask them to upload the catalog first, then pre-purchase only the proven bestsellers. For custom products, you can offer to forward each order to the supplier once it arrives. [L0801.027]

### Agreements and Terms to Check

- Supplier agreements are usually short and cover payment, business information, shipping method and the price policy (MAP). Review them carefully. (older, 2020) A lawyer review was not needed for typical supplier agreements in the lesson's experience. [L0468.051]
- Supplier agreements usually cover the MAP policy, brand representation terms, the contact details for both sides, payment terms and the return policy. (older, 2021) Use a signed agreement from a supplier you already work with as a template when a new supplier asks what terms you propose. [L1112.045]
- The authorized retailer agreement you sign with a brand contains its MAP policy and return policy. Read these terms before selling, because they decide whether discounts and returns are allowed. [L0667.058]
- (older, 2021) A supplier can require approval before you offer discount codes. When signing a MAP agreement, check whether the supplier prohibits discount codes. This is rare, around 5% of cases, and if it applies, do not offer discounts on that brand. [L1090.033]
- (older, 2022) Supplier agreements can require a supplier to contain the order process. Read each agreement and document how orders are processed, by email or through a dealer portal, so the process can later be handed to a team. [L0856.055]
- Supplier agreements typically describe how orders are processed, by email or through a dealer portal. Read each supplier's agreement and document its order process, so the process can later be handed to a team. [L0856.055]
- (older, 2021) If a supplier's application or contract contradicts itself, for example about who arranges freight, call them before submitting and ask which term applies. A professional supplier should be able to clarify the wording and send a corrected version. [L0853.115]
- If a supplier has no standard terms for online retailers, define them yourself:
  1. Expect that the supplier has no ready dropship terms and will need to be guided.
  2. Take the terms from your existing supplier agreements. These cover the MAP pricing formula (for example 2x or 2.5x wholesale), who ships and bills the order, and the length of the return window.
  3. Copy these terms into a Word or Google document and edit them to fit the new supplier.
  4. Save the document as a PDF and send it to the supplier, stating that these are the terms you are looking for. [L0626.006]
- (older, 2022) If a supplier has no drop shipping agreement, take the clearest existing supplier agreement from another brand and adapt it into your own template. [L1113.024]
- (older, 2021) When a supplier asks you for a contract, it often means they have little experience with online retailers. A contract for this purpose can fit on one page and list the basic terms as bullet points. [L0968.005]
- If a supplier has no dropship agreement, create a simple one-page agreement in Google Docs. Include how to place orders, the return policy, who to contact and any warranties. Base it on an agreement from another supplier, then save it as a PDF. Suppliers rarely lack such policies, so this should be uncommon. [L0666.030]

```
Create a simple one-page dropship agreement in Google Docs if a supplier has none. Include how to place orders, the return policy, who to contact, and any warranties. Base it on an agreement from another supplier, then save it as a PDF. Suppliers rarely lack such policies, so this should be uncommon.
```
[L0666.030]

- Before listing a supplier's products on another platform, check the supplier agreement. The agreement typically limits sales to the retailer's own store, so the usual rule is Shopify only. [L0806.026]
- (older, 2022) Supplier agreements typically forbid selling their products on marketplaces such as eBay and Amazon. Check the agreement before listing a supplier product there. [L0803.030]

### Payment on Card File and Prepay

- Most supplier relationships run on a credit card kept on file: the supplier charges it when an order is sent, so the item ships right away. A debit card on file can work too. [L0970.044]
- Many suppliers keep the operator's card on file so an order can be charged and shipped right away when it is submitted. The card is used for orders the operator places with that supplier and is part of the supplier's online vendor account setup. [L1114.016]
- When a supplier approves an online vendor account, it may ask for a card on file, a preferred courier and the store's account number so orders can be charged and shipped quickly. It may also send a spec sheet with dimensions. Use the supplier's own data file where it is usable, and fill in the missing details yourself before uploading. [L1114.069]
- (older, 2022) Standard supplier approval paperwork often includes a credit card authorization form with the card number and a signature, so the supplier can process orders quickly. Some sellers also send a card photo for verification. [L1113.021]
- Providing credit card details to approved suppliers is standard practice for pre-pay arrangements. Suppliers charge the card to fulfill orders after customer purchases. [L0270.017]
- (older, 2021) To pay suppliers:
  1. Keep your company credit card on file with the supplier.
  2. Send the order. The supplier charges the card and ships the product.
  3. You receive the tracking number and invoice by email.
  Some suppliers instead require payment through a dealer portal on their website. [L0247.027]
- (older, 2020) Keep a credit card on file with suppliers so they can charge it immediately when you submit an order, rather than asking for payment each time. Pay the card off the same or next day, and ask the card company to raise the limit as sales grow. [L0852.030]
- (older, 2020) A supplier can be paid per order by debit card or PayPal if no card is kept on file. A stored card lets the supplier charge immediately and dispatch sooner, so it is the faster option. [L0852.120]
- (older, 2020) Products from a supplier can be paid with a debit card, as suppliers generally accept it. [L1111.052]
- (older, 2021) Do not ask suppliers for credit terms. Instead, leave a credit or debit card on file that they charge when each order is submitted. [L0834.015]
- (2023) Keep all purchases on your card-on-file with a supplier as prepaid orders. When describing this to a supplier, use the term "prepay for orders", because you pay before they ship. [L0835.013]
- (2023) Prepay wording for a supplier application that asks for credit terms: write "prepay terms" over the request. Because the store pays the supplier before it ships, credit references are not needed. [L0964.037]
- (2021) Credit-application forms from suppliers often ask for credit and trade references. You can cross those sections out, mark the application as wanting prepaid terms, and send it back. [L1279.031]
- (undated) Suppliers may offer net payment terms such as 30, 60 or 90 days, which means they extend credit and ship inventory before being paid. Asking for prepay instead means you pay by card for each order before the supplier ships it. This takes the credit risk away from them and speeds up approval. [L0933.022]
- (undated) Prepay wording to use with a supplier:

```
We are not looking for any credit terms. We would like to prepay: we will keep our credit card on file, and when we send you an order, you can charge our card and ship the order to our customer. [If you have no credit yet: We will start with a debit card on file and switch to a credit card once our credit has built up.]
```
[L0933.023]

- (undated) With prepay terms, the supplier does not ship any item until you have confirmed a specific customer order and told them where to send it. The supplier never carries the cost of inventory you have not sold, which is why prepay makes approval easier. [L0933.033]
- (2023) Some suppliers accept bank transfers, but many prefer payment by credit or debit card for orders. A business card is therefore the more practical way to pay suppliers, even though transfers work with some of them. [L1405.038]
- (older, 2019) Avoid credit terms. If an application asks for business credit and bank references, prepay orders instead, so that you can pay with a credit card and collect card rewards. [L0960.031]
- (2023) A supplier that asks for bank information or references is usually checking your credit for credit terms. An alternative is to prepay each order by keeping a card on file that is charged before the order ships. [L0855.057]
- (2023) Payment timing differs per supplier. Most suppliers charge the card when the order is placed, but confirm this with each supplier before placing orders. [L0664.022]
- (2023) Most suppliers charge the card when an order is sent, so the item ships right away. Confirm the terms before the first order with each supplier. [L0970.044, L0664.022]

#### Card Fees

| Source | Fee | What to do |
|---|---|---|
| (older, 2020) Supplier card processing fee | About 2% when you pay by credit card | Ask to pay by bank wire or electronic transfer. If refused, weigh the fee against your margin. If unavoidable, consider raising prices on those products to absorb it. [L0358.046] |
| (older, 2022) Prepaying by card | About 3.5 percent in supplier fees | After about ten orders over two months, ask for net 30 terms, or switch to ACH transfer, which typically takes two to three days and gives a day or two of credit. [L1113.022] |

### Credit Limits and Building Credit

- (older, 2020) Minimum credit card limit: $1,000 covers a typical first order (COGS $500 + shipping $100). A debit card is an acceptable alternative. [L0268.007]
- (older, 2020) To grow the limit:
  1. Pay off the credit card balance daily after orders go through.
  2. Call the card issuer after the first few weeks and request a limit increase based on the demonstrated daily payoff history.
  3. Repeat monthly or bimonthly. Limit increases are granted based on usage pattern, not waiting period. [L0268.008]
- (older, 2023) Build credit limit by paying off the supplier card every day. This keeps it near its limit while showing the bank consistent use. Then ask the bank for limit increases, since the payment history supports larger limits over time. [L0970.046]
- (older, 2020) A new seller does not need a high credit card limit at the start. Limits can grow over time as you repay the card each day and request increases from the bank. [L0852.031]
- (older, 2021) Build credit in stages: low limit, then increased limit over time, then a secured credit card backed by a $5,000+ deposit. [L0270.064]

### Credit Terms and Net Payment

Sources differ on when net terms are possible. Use this order:

| Stage | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Start (new store) | Prepay each order by card on file. Net terms are not available at the start. | [L1480.036] (older, 2021-22); [L0963.083] (older, 2022) |
| Start, general | Net terms are uncommon and depend on the supplier. Most suppliers expect prepay. | [L1112.046] (older, 2021) |
| Start, preference | A supplier that offers credit card terms is preferred. Net terms and wire transfer per order are not recommended for a new dropshipper: net terms need many trade references, and wires slow each order. | [L0665.018] (older, 2022) |
| Later | After about ten orders over two months, ask for net 30 terms. | [L1113.022] (older, 2022) |

- (older, 2022) Suppliers often offer 30-day, 60-day or 90-day net terms. Avoid applying for credit terms and prepay each order by card instead. [L0963.083]
- (older, 2022) Suppliers offering credit terms may request credit references, bank references or a personal credit report. Prepaying by card on file avoids the credit check. [L0963.084]
- (older, 2022) Net 30 is available only after about ten orders over two months. Switch to ACH transfer at that point if needed, as described under card fees. [L1113.022]
- (older, 2021-22) Supplier credit accounts, which allow you to pay invoices later, are not available at the start. Expect to pay each order in full when it is placed. Treat a credit account as a later-stage option once the relationship is established. [L1480.036]

### Deposits, Stock and Pre-Purchase

- (older, 2020) Never pay a deposit to a supplier before a customer order exists, so the cash outlay is zero until sales come in. Refuse requests for large credit card deposits, such as $4,000, because they are uncommon and risky before you have a relationship with the supplier. [L0852.029]
- (older, 2021) Only pay a supplier deposit or opening order from profits your store has already earned with other suppliers. Do not pay a deposit or stock inventory before you know the best sellers. Commit to larger inventory only when the opportunity is clearly massive. [L0801.026]
- (older, 2020) Never pay a supplier upfront or buy inventory in advance to start a relationship. Propose a 30-day trial run of listing their products instead. [L1277.031]
- (older, 2018) Do not send stock to a fulfillment center to meet a supplier minimum or to handle a first order. It is not necessary in this situation. [L1109.004]
- (older, 2021) If a supplier requires a product purchase or deposit for a product list, ask them to upload the catalog first, then pre-purchase only the proven bestsellers. [L0801.027]

### Minimum Orders

#### Principles

- (2023) Minimum purchase requirements from suppliers are not recommended for most people. Only consider them if you have proven niche knowledge, existing cash flow and high confidence in sales velocity. New sellers should avoid them. [L0270.035]
- (older, 2021) Handle a supplier minimum purchase requirement by:
  1. Requesting that the supplier split the order into multiple charges if your credit card limit is low.
  2. Providing a debit card associated with your business checking account.
  3. Avoiding the supplier if you are new to business. [L0270.064]
- (older, 2019) If a supplier's minimum order roughly equals the MAP of one product, list the products and place the first order only after a customer buys. If the minimum is larger, ask the supplier to let you test the products on your site for one month without the minimum commitment. [L0961.026]
- (older, 2021) When a new account is held pending a first order of a set amount, check the terms and conditions for how the requirement is worded. If the supplier has already sent product information and images, upload the products and place the order. [L0853.031]

Email template for a new account held pending a first order:

```
Hi [Supplier contact name],

I saw your email about the [amount] minimum first order. We are happy to do business with you and send you many orders, but we are online-only and do not yet know which products will sell best. Could you send us your product information, product images, price sheet and descriptions? We will upload them, drive traffic to them, and expect orders within our first month.

Thank you,
[Your name]
[Store name]
```
[L0853.032]

#### Amounts Seen in the Sources

| Minimum | Source | Guidance |
|---|---|---|
| $1,000 first order before a trade account opens (older, 2018) | Uncommon. It becomes a real constraint only when average order value is low. With products selling for around $2,000, it is usually not an issue. [L1109.001] | Tell the supplier honestly that you do not yet know which products will sell. Propose listing their products on your store for 30 days. Aim for well over $1,000 in sales from their top sellers within those 30 days, then place an order above the minimum. [L1109.002] If they still insist, list the top products, run traffic, and place the first order once the sales money is available. Suppliers often accept a smaller order once the money is in hand, for example $500 against a $1,000 minimum. [L1109.002] |
| $1,000 with very low wholesale costs (older, 2018) | Reaching $1,000 in 30 days is much harder when wholesale costs are very low, such as around $100 per item. Expensive products make this route realistic. [L1109.003] |, |
| $2,500 first order before single-order shipping (older, 2021-22) | Ask why they require it and how much they want. Decide whether you can afford it. It is a financial commitment, and you may choose not to work with that supplier. [L0929.029] The minimum also filters out sellers who are not committed, so it tests your seriousness as well as costing you money. [L0929.035] | If you accept, ask for their best-selling products and buy those, so you know they will sell. The supplier holds the stock reserved and ships each customer order to the customer one by one, so you do not handle it yourself. [L0929.029] If the amount is beyond your launch budget, say so politely, offer to come back in a few months, and note the callback in your supplier sheet. [L0929.029] |
| $2,500 initial minimum order (2024) | Not advisable for a new store about to go live. Ask for it to be waived, or wait until the store has its own bankroll. [L0856.050] |, |
| $2,500 initial minimum order (2025) | Avoid accepting it just to get listed. Treat such a requirement as a signal to widen the niche instead. [L0966.023] |, |
| $5,000 minimum stock order (older, 2022) | A brand-new store should not pay to get approved. The first step is to ask whether the products can be tested on the store for 30 days first. [L0854.013] |, |
| $10,000 annual minimum (2020) | Work backwards from cost of goods. At $500 cost per unit, $10,000 equals 20 units a year, or about 1.7 units per month. Verify demand for the brand through reviews and buyer activity on competitor stores before committing. [L0664.013] |, |

- (undated) Never guarantee a minimum sales volume, and do not sign anything promising a minimum dollar amount. Sales cannot be guaranteed before the products are live on your site, visitors see them and buy them. [L0933.013]
- Refusal wording for a guaranteed minimum:

```
I would never agree to a guaranteed minimum. I think we are going to do great, which is exactly why we want to sell your products. But I cannot promise a sales amount, because nobody knows until your products are on our site, our visitors see them and they buy. So I am not going to sign anything promising a minimum dollar amount, and I think honesty is the right approach for both of us.
```
[L0933.014]

### Exclusivity and Territory

- (undated) Ask a supplier for exclusivity for a new product in one region for a limited time, such as six months, before testing it. This is typically only possible with a close relationship with the supplier. [L0334.043]
- (older, 2020) Exclusive distribution offers are rare. Before accepting, ask why the supplier wants exclusivity and whether they expect your store to sell only their products. Then weigh the upsides against the downsides, such as obligations that limit how you promote other brands. [L0358.048]
- (older, 2021) An exclusive retailer arrangement is rare and is usually not needed. It becomes realistic only when you already generate most of a supplier's revenue. A partnership with equity or payouts over time may then let you acquire part or all of the company. [L0469.114]
- (older, 2019) Exclusive selling rights for a product in a niche are usually granted only after a very strong relationship and a high sales volume with the supplier. They are rarely possible from day one. [L1068.036]
- (older, 2021-22) Confirm that you hold an exclusive distribution deal for a product in your market before promoting it. Exclusivity means you are the only retailer selling it locally, which lets you own its brand search traffic. [L0949.007]
- (older, 2022) For an exclusive supplier, maximize sales while the exclusivity lasts. Forecast unit sales and stay in contact so the supplier keeps stock full. [L0963.091]
- (2023) Suppliers rarely require you to list all their products or specific ones. A rare exception is a multi-brand supplier that reserves some brands for physical stores. [L0934.027]
- (2024) Some suppliers will not revoke approval or ask you to remove products due to low sales volume, even if the products do not convert. This is standard in dropshipping relationships. [L0253.022, L0253.055]

### Conditions on Trade Accounts

- (2025) Some supplier trade accounts require verified dealer status before you can buy, and some brands set conditions on your sales. One brand allowed sales only within 100 miles of your address, and another required a guaranteed 50,000 dollars in sales per year. Check these conditions before you plan your sales territory, budget and outreach. [L0857.067]
- (2024) Trade program links on supplier websites usually mean the same as becoming an authorized dealer. Open the trade program page to confirm the terms. [L0856.049]

### Tax Documents and Sales Tax

#### Forms and Requests

- (2025) The W9 is a standard supplier requirement. It is an IRS form that shows your business entity information (LLC, sole proprietor, S corporation, etc.). [L0255.005]
- (2025) W9 steps:
  1. The supplier sends a W9 form request.
  2. Complete the W9 form with your business entity details.
  3. Send the completed W9 and contact person information to the supplier.
  4. Provide an email address and phone number as the contact person.
  5. The supplier responds with a price sheet containing product information, wholesale pricing, MAP and MSRP. [L0255.006]
- (2023) When a supplier asks for a state resale certificate, send the certificate for your home state and explain that you do not have economic nexus in the state the supplier is asking about. Some suppliers may also request a W-9 to confirm you are a business reselling the products. [L0835.043]

Email template for a resale certificate request:

```
Hi [Supplier contact name], our business is based in [Home state], and we have attached our [Home state] resale certificate. We do not currently have an economic nexus in [State the supplier asked about], so please let us know how you would handle orders shipping to that state.
[Your name]
[Company name]
```
[L0835.044]

- (2023) For a supplier that needs a resale or sales tax certificate:
  1. Search Google for the state name plus "reseller certificate" (or "sales tax certificate").
  2. Use the official.gov site and apply online.
  3. Send the resulting document to the supplier. [L0964.033]
- (2023) Expect a supplier to ask for a sales certificate, and be ready to provide buyer details when they request it. [L0964.034]
- (2024/2025) If a supplier that requires a reseller permit or tax ID is for a Canadian or non-US seller, search the community for members who have done this rather than relying on general advice. An EIN is not always necessary but should be checked. [L0665.023]

#### Sales Tax Charged by Suppliers

Two positions apply to different cases:

- (2025) Supplier agreements can require the reseller to pay sales tax. Accept this as a cost of credibility when a supplier requests it. [L0472.028]
- (2023) If a supplier charges sales tax because it has no resale certificate on file, provide the resale certificate or W-9 it asks for. Do not accept the charge until the supplier has confirmed it has the documents it needs. [L0835.045]

### Insurance

- (older, 2022) If a supplier contract requires insurance, obtain it. A general liability policy from a large company with fixed rates is the recommended type. [L0963.085]
- (older, 2022) Case: a policy sold through a broker with a low premium led to an insurance audit after revenue grew. The business owed more than 10,000 USD extra. Check that the rates are fixed before buying a policy. [L0963.086]

### EDI, FTP, Portals and Order Transmission

- (2025) Suppliers that use EDI or FTP run enterprise-style order integrations, and the Shopify apps for them are expensive. If a supplier asks for EDI, ask for email or an online ordering portal to submit orders instead. [L0667.055]
- (2023) EDI is a system for connecting directly with a supplier's inventory and order systems. Shopify apps that do this are expensive because developers must configure them for each supplier system. The answer is usually no. Ask for an alternative method of ordering and inventory checks. [L0835.040]
- (older, 2021) If a supplier requires EDI integration over SPS and your business does not use EDI, say plainly that you are not set up for it. Then ask whether they will still work with you, since EDI may be a preference rather than a hard requirement. [L1112.080]
- (older, 2020) Most suppliers do not need EDI. Around 95 percent of orders are sent to suppliers as automatic purchase orders by email, which is enough for most brands. [L1277.038]
- (older, 2019) Automated electronic order transmission (EDI) to suppliers was not used in 2019. Some suppliers offered it, but for a small operation it was judged not worth building on Shopify. [L1002.077]
- (2023) Most suppliers do not accept orders through a portal. Typically the supplier agreement, usually a one- to two-page document, describes how to place orders, most often by email to an orders address. [L1283.004]
- (undated) When a supplier asks for a physical document or signature, you can handle it remotely:
  1. Forward their mail to a mailbox in your home country.
  2. Have it scanned to a PDF and emailed to you.
  3. Sign the PDF digitally and upload it to the mailbox service, which prints it and posts it to the supplier. [L1441.015]

### Marketplace and Channel Restrictions

#### Marketplaces

- (undated) Some suppliers forbid selling their products on third-party marketplaces such as Amazon, eBay or Walmart. This is common and can be ignored for the store itself, since no marketplace seller account is needed to follow the store setup. [L0360.010]
- (2024) Do not sell supplier products on third-party marketplaces such as Amazon or eBay, because most suppliers only authorize sales on the retailer's own store. [L1115.016]
- (2025) Most suppliers do not allow resale on third-party marketplaces such as Amazon or Walmart and expect sales only on your own website. The permitted sales channels are defined in the supplier agreement. [L0966.025]
- (undated) Most authorized-retailer supplier contracts forbid selling on third-party marketplaces such as eBay, Amazon and Walmart. True competitors are therefore online-only stores that sell the same brands, not marketplaces. [L0941.025]

#### Channel Permission and Ads

- (undated) Before listing a supplier's products on another sales channel, get the supplier's permission, because some suppliers may not be comfortable with it. Confirm this in writing or in the account agreement before you expand. [L1441.011]
- (older, 2020) Some suppliers forbid paid ads using their brand name, including Google Ads. In that case, do not run paid campaigns on the brand. Rely on organic search and Bing traffic. [L1277.044]

#### Accessories and Flat-Rate Shipping

- (2023) Supplier distributor and channel rules apply. If a supplier prohibits selling accessories, or offers a flat-rate shipping cost that causes losses on accessories, sell accessories only as add-ons to a main product. [L1283.019]

### Trade Accounts, Affiliate Programs and Private-Supplier Setups

- (2023) Do not affiliate with a supplier's affiliate program. If a supplier offers an affiliate sign-up, ask instead whether you can become a retailer. [L0964.026]
- (older, 2019) Working as an authorized retailer means the supplier ships without a supplier invoice, and the customer may see the shipping origin. This is normal for brand retailers and does not cause customer issues. [L0467.032]
- (older, 2019) Buying products from third-party resellers, such as eBay sellers, and shipping them with their invoice causes problems. Authorized retailer status avoids this, because the supplier never includes an invoice. [L0467.033]
- (2024) Get approved with both distributors if a supplier is in stock on only one distributor and both are offered. Pricing and stock levels may differ, and approval costs nothing. [L0665.019]
- (2025) The authorized retailer agreement and its MAP and return terms apply to every sale of that brand. [L0667.058]
- (older, 2023) Some suppliers: the affiliate, trade program and dealer routes all lead to the same outcome, so confirm which one you are on before you start. [L0856.049]
- (2024) Trade program links on supplier websites usually mean the same as becoming an authorized dealer. [L0856.049]
- (2024) Gold and private-supplier setups: Gold suppliers may reject a new store or ask to re-review it. [L0969.007]
- (2020, sole proprietor to LLC) Use this procedure when a sole proprietorship converts to an LLC:
  1. Fill out new vendor forms with the new business information and EIN.
  2. Email the same contact who received the original forms.
  3. Ask the supplier to update the business information on file to the new company name. [L0967.035]
- (2024) Resources such as the LLC setup document are shared in the supplier community channel and may require an LLC or UEN number to access. If you cannot get one, ask the community for help, and the supplier can arrange it. [L1167.023]

### Business Entity, Address and Account Setup

- (2024) To list a supplier with your business address, use the address from your Shopify settings:
  1. Contact the supplier as normal using your email and phone scripts.
  2. In Shopify settings, provide your official business address (for example, a Canadian address for a Canadian business).
  3. In the supplier approval forms, list your business address as it appears in your Shopify settings.
  4. The supplier will send its full product catalog regardless of how many products you mentioned.
  5. Upload only the products you want.
  6. This approach is standard and fully accepted by suppliers. [L0253.024]
- (2020) Ask in the initial paperwork whether the supplier ships on your account or theirs. If it is not stated, ask after approval and before orders start. [L1111.029]
- (undated) Some supplier approvals and documents can be handled remotely through a mailbox service, as described under EDI and portals. [L1441.015]
- (2025) Tax documents follow the entity type on the W9, so match the entity you register with the entity on the form. [L0255.005]

### Red Lines: What to Refuse

- (undated) If a supplier asks you to co-brand a website, to run their official site with your store content, or to take over advertising for their brand in exchange for an arrangement that sounds unusual, decline politely. Keep the current arrangement and do not agree to unclear terms. [L0941.023]
- (older, 2021) Reject any brand that asks for an extra dropshipping fee on top of its wholesale price. Argue that the store brings new traffic, market reach and sales, so the brand gains without paying more. [L0962.152]
- (older, 2021) If a new supplier asks the store to handle foreign certification, product liability or export logistics, decline unless the relationship is long-term and the extra work is worth the effort. Build such services only for established partners who rely on the store for most of their sales. [L0962.153]
- (older, 2020) Never pay a supplier upfront to start a relationship. Refuse large credit card deposits, such as $4,000. [L0852.029, L1277.031]
- (2025) Avoid accepting a large initial minimum order, such as 2,500 USD, just to get listed. [L0966.023]
- (2021) Do not pay for a deposit or stock before a customer order. [L0270.035]
- (undated) Never guarantee a minimum sales volume. [L0933.013]

#### Further details

1. When the supplier responds with interest, they will email application forms.
2. These forms request standard business information: business name, address, phone number, and billing information.
3. Complete all sections of the forms completely and accurately immediately upon receipt.
4. Submit the completed forms back as quickly as possible-do not delay.
5. Maintaining momentum is critical; delayed submissions result in significantly lower approval rates.
6. An account manager will respond within a few days with approval, account number and contact details, or a request for additional information.
7. If additional information is requested, respond immediately with what's needed.
[L0133.017]

- Before sending ID to a supplier for a credit card verification, confirm the company is legitimate by finding its products on other retailers' websites. A real brand's products normally appear in several places online. [L0468.189]

## Pricing, Margin and Profitability Checks per Supplier

### Cost inputs for the margin calculation

- Cost of goods sold (COGS) is only accurate when it is entered per product in Shopify. COGS equals the cost to deliver the product to your 3PL plus the average shipping cost per unit to the customer. [L0222.006]
- Packaging and supplies cover only the materials used to fulfil an order: the box, tape, polymailer and the cost of the shipping label itself. Postage paid to the carrier is entered separately as shipping cost. [L0433.013]
- Shipping cost is the average postage paid per order for a typical order size, for example $9.17. Take it from actual carrier costs, not from packaging. [L0433.014]
- If a 3PL charges per pick, an order with several items incurs one pick fee per item, so the 3PL cost per order must be scaled to the average number of picks. Typical 3PL pick and pack fees run about $1.50 to $2.50 per pick. If no 3PL is used, this input is $0. [L0433.015]
- Keep COGS as low as possible, but never at the expense of the quality customers notice. Cutting costs that buyers feel will hurt repeat sales. [L0378.016]
- Customisation and rush production options from the supplier carry their own costs. Add a markup to them, and ask suppliers which custom or rush options they offer before building them into your pricing. [L0920.023]
- A supplier's percentage margin must be checked for its basis. Ask whether it is a net margin left after the product cost or a markup on that cost. The two give very different selling prices, so confirm which one applies before committing. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0551.076]

**Worked cost examples**

- Ceramic flower pot: sold at $30 (about $29.90) with an $11 COGS including fulfilment costs. [L0642.044]
- Zendrop: one unit sourced at $20 with $10 shipping to the customer gives a total of $30 entered as COGS + SHIPPING per unit. [L0987.023]
- CJ Dropshipping: a cost of $17 per item on CJ was entered into profit calculators to check margin. [L0440.018]

**CJ Dropshipping price basis**

- On CJ Dropshipping, after you choose the variant and the target country, the product page shows a field called total dropshipping price. It already includes the product cost and shipping to the customer, so use it as the base cost for pricing instead of the item price alone. [L0436.024]

**Confirming cost before pricing**

1. Estimate the product cost from AliExpress listicles by checking average prices.
2. Open a sourcing request at CJ Dropshipping to get accurate supplier prices.
3. Use the sourcing result as the real cost basis for pricing.

[L0693.019]

- AliExpress prices are only estimates, because sellers without fulfilment can set any price. Use a sourcing request to confirm the cost. [L0693.021]

**Shipping outside the MAP**

- Shipping costs are typically not included in a supplier's MAP price, because shipping varies by destination. If a supplier does not explicitly state that shipping is included in its pricing, assume it is not. [L0276.012]
- Estimating shipping and margin (older advice, 2020):

  1. Find the supplier's location and zip code on its website.
  2. Note the product's size and weight.
  3. Get a freight estimate from freightbroker.com between the supplier's zip code and two customer zip codes in the states you plan to sell in, for example one in Florida and one in California.
  4. Average the two estimates to get a shipping cost.
  5. Subtract wholesale cost and shipping from the selling price to see the margin left for ads and fees.

  [L0961.036]

- Some suppliers based in Japan charge an average of about $15 shipping per product, which can remove most of the margin on lower-priced items. Add this shipping cost into the spread calculation before listing their products. [L0965.086]
- If a supplier adds tax to its wholesale prices, compare competitor prices with that tax included and recalculate the MAP-to-wholesale spread. Keep the products only if the spread still works. [L0965.027]

### Retail price basis

- Google Shopping is used for competitor price research: search your product names as a customer, then compare sellers' prices to your wholesale cost. Use store averages, not the lowest prices. [L1051.004]
- Extremely low competitor prices are often a warning sign rather than a bargain. Stores selling with almost no margin often have poor customer service, late or missing shipments and short business lifespans. Check company names, websites, reviews and ratings before treating their price as market price. [L1051.005]

### Benchmarks: MAP, wholesale and the 50% rule

The sources give different thresholds. Each one applies in a different situation, so read them together.

| Benchmark | Value | When it applies | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| 50% rule (core target) | Wholesale about half of MAP. A $1,000 MAP implies a $500 wholesale cost. | Default test for every supplier. | [L0358.003, L0835.021, L0967.014] |
| Target spread | Wholesale of $1,000 should correspond to a MAP of about $2,000. | Planning target when reviewing a price list. | [L0666.019] |
| Minimum multiple | MAP at least 2x wholesale. | Floor after fees, acquisition, shipping and overhead. (Older advice, 2020.) | [L0268.010] |
| Ideal multiple | MAP at least 2.2x wholesale. | Preferred level; thin spreads should be reconsidered. (Older advice, 2022.) | [L0665.010] |
| Formula shown by suppliers | A MAP formula such as 2x wholesale. | When the supplier sheet has no MAP policy. | [L0470.074] |
| Shipping-inclusive margin floor | At least 25% margin including shipping. | Listing decision for a product. (Older advice, 2021-22.) | [L1046.031] |
| Average spread in one example | About 25%, against about 50% required by the model. | Reference of what suppliers often offer. | [L0835.019] |
| Typical retail discount offered | 15-20% off retail is below target. | Spread check before paid ads. | [L0666.019, L0666.066] |
| Floor before expenses | Never settle on a margin leaving about 20% before expenses. | Hard floor during negotiation. | [L0835.022] |
| Discount from supplier | 30% insufficient for an online retailer; 30-35% not enough if you also pay shipping and acquisition. | Supplier discounts that still must cover shipping and ads. (Older advice, 2020; 2025.) | [L0967.016, L0807.015] |
| Discount from supplier | 50% acceptable; 20-40% usually not. | Judged by whether COGS is about 50% of planned selling price. (Older advice, 2020.) | [L0664.005] |
| Affiliate-style margin | 10-20% before payment fees and ad costs. | Sign of an affiliate-style relationship; not enough to build a business. (Older advice, 2020.) | [L0967.015] |
| Affiliate minimum | A minimum affiliate discount of 30% is not profitable; at 20%, decline or price above MAP without paid traffic. (Older advice, 2020.) | Affiliate offers. | [L0268.009] |
| No-MAP starting price | Price around three times wholesale as a starting point. (Older advice, 2020.) | Only when no MAP is given; revise against margin math and competitor prices. | [L0358.014] |
| No-MAP fallback | Set price at least 2x wholesale. (Older advice, 2022.) | When no clear competitor price can be found. | [L0854.011] |

Where sources give different starting points for a supplier with no MAP, use the 3x starting point [L0358.014] only as a first guess. Use the 2x fallback [L0854.011] when no clear competitor price exists. Revise both against competitor prices and your own margin.

- Under the 50% rule, if a supplier's price is far off and it will not negotiate, the supplier is usually not worth working with. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0358.003]
- The minimum acceptable wholesale margin is about 50%. A wholesale cost above half of MAP should be negotiated or reconsidered. (Older advice, 2022.) [L0665.010]
- Judge a discount by the resulting cost of goods, not by the percentage alone. A 50% discount can be acceptable, while 20-40% off usually is not. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0664.005]
- Off-MSRP example: a supplier offering 50% off MSRP while MAP is 25% off MSRP leaves too little margin. The deal only works if competitors sell at MSRP. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0671.265]

### Net profit and ad-spend thresholds

**Worked profit example at 2x (older advice, 2020)**

- Wholesale $800, sale price $1,000: gross profit $200. Minus 2.9% fees ($29) gives $171. Minus 10% customer acquisition cost ($100) gives $71 profit. Subtracting shipping and operational overhead leaves minimal or negative margin.
- Solution: price at $1,600 instead, and drive only organic and remarketing traffic, not paid ads.

[L0268.010, L0268.011]

- Transaction fees (2.9%) and customer acquisition (up to 10% of sale) are the costs that a 2x MAP has to cover. [L0268.010]

**Dollar profit matters as much as percentage**

- A wholesale price of $161 against a MAP of $230 leaves too little dollar profit per sale to justify advertising. Accepting a lower spread is only reasonable for very high-ticket products worth thousands of dollars, where gross profit per unit is still large. [L0835.020]
- A lower spread is acceptable only when the dollar profit per sale still makes the sale worthwhile. [L0835.021]
- Established industry suppliers offered about $125 profit on a $1,000 product and about $200 on a $4,000 product, which can be too thin for paid ads. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0671.002]
- Do not sell products at very low margins, such as 5% or a flat $50 per sale. This is especially true for suppliers without a price policy, where other retailers race to the bottom. These deals create more problems than sales. [L0835.023]
- Products sold below 25% margin including shipping should not be added to the store. Keep the supplier on file for later. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1046.031]

**Product-level margin checks**

- Product-only margin in the example was roughly 20% once outbound shipping was removed, showing that shipping was the main reason the margin was low. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1046.030]
- Outbound shipping in the example cost about $98,000 to $100,000 per year, roughly 6.3% of total income. It is one of the largest levers for improving margin. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1046.028]
- Check each product's margin including shipping before listing it. Add only products with at least 25% margin including shipping. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1046.031, L1046.032]
- Small, lightweight products have low fulfilment costs because they ship in small packages. This leaves room for a good selling price and margin, so use it when choosing test products. [L0562.018]
- Outbound shipping cost in one case was about $98,000 per year; the owner planned to bring it to about $75,000, a saving of about $23,000 per year, while keeping the rest of the cost structure. This was the third of three supplier priorities. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1046.036]

**Margin review steps (older advice, 2021-22)**

1. Contact the supplier and build the relationship before asking for terms.
2. Request the supplier's current pricing sheet.
3. Calculate the margin for each product, including shipping.
4. Add only products with at least 25 percent margin including shipping.
5. Keep low-margin suppliers on file and note any individual high-margin products they offer.
6. Keep searching for higher-margin suppliers as the first priority.

[L1046.032]

- Products from suppliers with weak sales can stay listed in free Google Shopping listings if they still show margin, so they earn traffic without ad spend. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0967.041]

### Evaluating a supplier before listing

**Supplier margin is set per supplier**

- Margins are supplier-specific, not niche-specific. No actual margin is known before approval, because pricing tiers and credit terms differ by supplier. One supplier may offer 50% margin while another in the same niche offers only 25-30%. [L0274.003]
- The profit margin is set by each supplier, not by the niche. Two suppliers in the same niche can offer very different margins, so judge every supplier on its own price list before deciding what to sell. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0671.001, L0671.157]
- Supplier margins are negotiated per supplier, not per niche. A low 20-30% margin from one supplier does not mean the niche is wrong. Keep adding approved suppliers in the niche. (Older advice, 2022.) [L0429.040]
- Margin between MAP and wholesale varies by supplier, not by niche. Calculate each supplier's margin after approval rather than assuming an industry margin. (Older advice, 2022.) [L0854.022]
- Supplier-specific prices, MAP policies and margins are unknown until approval. Use category averages for planning and confirm the real margins after approval. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0469.031]
- Wholesale cost is not known until you are approved, but you can still plan around it. (Older advice, 2022.) [L0854.022]
- A supplier's price list is the fastest way to confirm true profit per supplier. Margins can often only be confirmed after approval, but they can be calculated as soon as the price list is received. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0967.024]
- Approved suppliers typically send an Excel sheet with three columns, or three separate price lists: MSRP, MAP and wholesale. Use these to estimate margins before listing products. (Older advice, 2019.) [L0663.053]

**Checking a supplier with no MAP policy**

1. Get the supplier's price list.
2. Search Google Shopping for several of its products.
3. Check whether anyone already advertises those products and at what markup.
4. If competitors mark them up only 10% or 15%, do not list them; this is a no-go.
5. If there is little competition, become the main seller for those products; if competitors sell them at healthy margins, list them too.

[L0671.007] (Older advice, 2021.)

1. Search the supplier's products in Google Shopping.
2. Check the prices other sellers use for those products.
3. If sellers are selling at no margin, skip the supplier.
4. If sellers still make a healthy margin, sell the products and mark them as ineligible on your price match policy page.

[L0467.030] (Older advice, 2019.)

1. Search the product names on Google Shopping.
2. Compare the prices other sellers charge.
3. Drop the product if many sellers price it at almost no margin, because that is a race to the bottom.
4. If margins hold up, exclude the product from your price match policy before the first sale.

[L0664.007] (Older advice, 2020.)

1. Search for that brand on Google Shopping.
2. Check what prices competitors list the products at.
3. If competitors are listing at very low markup (e.g., 5% margin), list the product on your site but only target free/organic traffic (no paid ads).
4. If competitors have healthy markups, you can list at similar prices and use Google Shopping ads.
5. Decide ad spend based on competitor pricing and your margin.

[L0255.017] (2025.)

- When no MAP policy exists, check the supplier's price list, shipping estimate and competitor prices before listing its products. Compare the expected margin for each brand before committing, since margins can still be strong when the brand approves only selected retailers. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0961.087]
- Brands that approve retailers selectively, especially those without a MAP policy, can still offer good margins because other retailers also protect their prices. Do not undercut the best competitor price in these cases, because price wars leave no one profitable. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0961.088]
- Pricing for a brand with no MAP starts from competitor prices. If competitors sell above your planned price, match their price instead of undercutting them, since buyers pay more.

  1. Search Google for a few products of that brand as a normal customer would.
  2. Open Google Shopping and check what competitors list the same products for.
  3. If competitors sell above your planned price, match their price instead of undercutting them, since buyers pay more.
  4. If you cannot find a clear competitor price, set the price at least 2x wholesale.

  [L0854.011] (Older advice, 2022.)
